Decentralized Finance
Gain an Understanding Between Decentralized Finance and Centralized Finance - Learn About the Potential of Smart Contracts on Ethereum and Other Blockchains

Introduction
Mention the word blockchain, and the first thing that springs to most people’s minds is Bitcoin. While cryptocurrencies are the most visible manifestations of blockchain tech, the reality is that blockchain runs much deeper than that. When people talk about the potential for cryptocurrencies to change the way the world looks at finance, much of their arguments come from the qualities that blockchain possesses.
Writing any book about money and technology is tough. Combine both of these topics, and you have a deep subject with many arguments and alleys to explore. In writing this book, I have aimed focused on the promise that decentralized finance holds for the world. There’s no doubt that cryptocurrencies are important. However, they’re not the drivers of the decentralized finance revolution.
As you read this book, you’re going to learn all about the current, centralized financial system as well. After all, if you don’t know how the current system works (or doesn’t work), how can you understand why decentralized finance (DeFi) is such a big deal? I’ve minimized the complex jargon and presented all material in easily understandable language, so don’t worry about all of this flying over your head.
Everyone from Nobel Prize-winning economists to reality TV stars has weighed in on the blockchain debate. I’m not here to tell you who is right and who is wrong. Instead, I’ve presented the material as impartially as possible, while leaning slightly in favor of DeFi. That is entirely because it has the potential to solve some of the biggest problems modern economics encounters.
Issues and Solutions
One of the most notable bottlenecks in modern economic theory is the Progress Paradox. As nations become wealthier thanks to the growing industrialization and prowess in the knowledge economy, a curious situation unfolds. At some point, the middle class stops growing prosperous and stagnates, while the upper class continues to grow.
You will be familiar with the term “the one percent.” It’s a phrase that is used a lot these days to describe insanely wealthy people. It’s also used to highlight the gap that exists between the haves and the have-nots. As economies grow and goods become more expensive, the middle class is the worst place to be. Too rich to qualify for government aid and too poor to afford the goods they need; they stagnate and remain in place.
Historically, every government’s solution has been to redistribute wealth through taxation. You tax the one percent more than the rest and deliver subsidies and services to the rest. Sounds great on paper. Except, this sort of thinking only incentivizes the rich to escape the economics and flee to tax havens. It’s why places like Monaco exist, after all.
In the absence of alternatives, governments raise taxes even more, and people become even more disincentivized to contribute to the country’s economic well-being. That leads to subsidy programs becoming unwieldy and underfunded. Look at the state of the United Kingdom’s National Health Service or any Western European nation’s social security programs.
Immigration is a solution to these issues but, when programs become heavily underfunded, immigration standards tend to slip, and the result is an even greater burden on social programs. Instead of providing security to highly skilled immigrants who could contribute and grow the economy, countries increase the size of their lower economic class and strain their fragile systems even more.
In short, the more a country progresses, the more fragile its economic stability becomes because inequality rises and traditional solutions stop working. DeFi has the power to right this situation by ensuring that wealth gets pre-distributed, not redistributed, as is the general reaction. Smart contracts and the blockchain are the tools to ensure this, and you’ll learn all about how they work to rectify imbalances before they even occur in this book.
There are other impacts the blockchain can have on our societies that impact the way we transact with one another.
Government
DeFi, at its heart, is all about loosening government control over the monetary system. Look around the world, and it’s easy to see how authoritarianism and extremism are on the rise. Even highly democratic countries feature governments that are either too far to the left or right and have no hesitation in suppressing the rights of their people.
The internet has allowed these governments to exert great control over their citizens, and money is the primary tool of control. Install highly centralized monetary controls, restrict currency outflows, and control access via banks, and you have a picture of how the modern financial system works.
Centralized systems work very well, but they rely on the central authority not overstepping its reach and acting ethically. Given the average government’s composition, ethical action is the last thing one would expect, and this is why a centralized system is a poor fit for the average person these days.
The blockchain has the potential to empower the free press, government opposition, and restore legitimacy to government institutions. Everything from works of art to journalism can be verified and, the cries of “fake news” can be squashed. A side effect of this is greater trust in the monetary system and more transparency that eradicates corruption.
That is DeFi’s promise. It isn’t just about wealth management solutions and the creation of a new asset class. We’ve lived through two decades of a digital revolution where our worlds went from analog to digital at warp speed. Well, I’m here to tell you the revolution is just beginning.
The blockchain and its attendant applications are the true revolutions and, the world is not going to be the same once their full potential is realized. This book is your guide to the wonderful world of blockchain, and how it’s currently being used, and its future applications.
I’ll also highlight some of the shortcomings of the blockchain since nothing is fully positive. There are elements of a centralized system that work very well, and currently, DeFi protocols fall short on a few counts. However, it’s the best alternative we have at the moment to a centralized system that has gone too far the other way.
So sit back and get ready to learn all about how our world is about to change!

Chapter 1:
Trust Protocols
Consider an ordinary transaction in today’s world. Let’s say you walk into a restaurant and order food. You trust the waiter to enter your order correctly, the chef to cook it properly and not add extra ingredients to it, and the owner of the restaurant to maintain standards that ensure a clean and safe establishment at all times. Lastly, the restaurant trusts you when you pay your bill that the paper money you use or the card you swipe are recognized forms of currency.
Trust is at the center of everything we do, and our world quickly falls apart without it. Back in 1993, a mathematician named David Chaum came up with a truly novel idea called eCash. eCash was a system of electronic pennies and dollars that people could use to transact with one another. Instead of worrying about sending money from one corner of the globe to another, consumers could simply send eCash and forget about it getting lost.
While the idea was brilliant, there was one huge flaw. People back in 1993 still believed that the internet was a place of hope and purity. They didn’t believe that things like security and violation of trust could ever occur with institutions. It’s safe to say that this Utopian vision of the internet no longer exists.
The earliest days of the internet were characterized by hope. The world wide web was a system that brought information to anyone, regardless of their education levels or financial ability. Ivy League knowledge was now potentially available to the masses, and everyone would benefit. The earliest websites that were developed adhered to this vision.
Software projects were largely open-sourced, and there was a community feel to everything. As the web evolved and technology got better, the idea of open collaboration, of creating a better world persisted. How else could one explain Google’s idiosyncrasies and its desire to ‘do no evil?’
Fast forward to today, and the likes of Google, Facebook, Amazon, and Apple might as well be Big Brother. All of these companies collect personal data, violate it as they please, and sell it right back to us at a premium. Data has become the new money, and these firms get to use it for free, with many of us powerless to do anything about it.
After all, can you imagine going about your day without using any of the services these companies offer? Even before the internet was open to the public, scientists feared that corporate greed would take over and recreate a world that mirrored the physical one where corporations controlled everything.
Scarily, the internet had the power to destabilize society and concentrate power even more because of how easily it brought people close to one another. These days, governments use surveillance programs to “keep us safe,” banks gate access to our money to “ensure compliance,” and big tech companies steal data to “provide value.” It’s safe to say the worst visions of those early researches came true.
However, we lucked out because those researchers proposed a solution to this mess.
Cryptography
At the heart of any technological innovation is the network. A network is simply a connection of at least two nodes. These nodes could be people, computers, entities, or a combination of them. The more nodes a network has, the greater is the need for trust to exist. The early days of the internet had few nodes, and trust was easy to establish.
Think of it this way: if you grew up in a small town of fewer than 5,000 people, the odds are good that you knew everyone there. Trust (or the lack of it) is easy to establish since you know what everybody is like or can find someone to vouch for a person you don’t know very well.
As networks grew, the motivations of each node were unknown. Going back to the previous example, let’s say a big company comes into town and promises to build a school. Well, what if they also plan on polluting the water? No one knows for sure. Similarly, what if a bigger node on the internet planned on using it to gate knowledge and increase inequality?
One solution for this problem was to use cryptographic controls on who could transmit information on the network. If the big company were to do something bad (add information to the network), it would first be verified by the rest of the network. This way, everyone was on the same page, and there was no question of sneaking things under people’s noses.
Cryptography’s earliest days involved simple protocols that reflected the simplicity of the networks they guarded. As the internet grew, cryptography fell behind because of impracticality and the lack of technology to develop it. Simply put, the network grew faster than cryptography could. That helps explain why giants such as Facebook and Google control information flow these days.
A Lack of Trust
As the internet has grown, trust is increasingly disappearing online. We can't verify anyone’s identities any more than we can verify the source of a news article. For instance, anyone can open a realistic-looking Facebook or Twitter account and post information that quotes seemingly legitimate scientific sources.
If one goes to dig through those sources, they’ll likely find that there are even more sources linked and, it turns into an endless hole of source verification. The result is it’s easier than ever before to propagate false information. Deep down, we all realize this lack of trust, which is why we’re so quick to yell ‘fake news’ or veer to the extreme with our opinions.
To instill some modicum of trust, companies like Google and Facebook fact check posts; but they can’t verify everything. Their algorithms are machines, and at the end of the day, machines can be manipulated. Those who are great at manipulation tend to end up on top.
Another reality of today’s information economy is that it’s easier than ever to get rich without adding any value whatsoever. Consider what Google does. It’s an aggregation platform for the world’s information. It provides value because no one else has such a repository of information. Why is Facebook so popular? It aggregates personal information and presents it to you in a friendly format.
Why are Uber and Airbnb so popular? They’re aggregators of information, owning nothing and producing nothing. They’re “platforms” where value-givers and value-seekers interact and produce something. The platform itself does nothing, yet controls the fate of everyone involved. Go against the algorithm, and the value-creator can be marginalized overnight.
Platforms are a prime example of everything that is wrong with a centralized system. They overstep their boundaries and refuse to remain on the sidelines. They justify their overstepping in the name of establishing trust. For instance, Uber’s review system is in place to establish trust. However, who’s to say that Uber couldn’t turn around and favor certain value-givers (drivers) over others?
You might say that’s far-fetched. Well, consider the way Amazon and other eCommerce platforms operate. They routinely promote favored suppliers over others and tailor algorithms to favor their own branded products over a smaller supplier. It’s this kind of behavior that has led to anti-trust lawsuits being filed against Amazon, Google, Microsoft, Walmart, and other platforms in India, Europe, and the United States.
The aggregation economy was originally branded as the ‘gig economy’ but in reality, this was a marketing cover-up. Aggregators are essentially middlemen who seek to draw value from the system by contributing nothing. You might say that the likes of Google and Amazon add value. However, this is true only because there aren’t any alternatives to them.
Decentralization
This picture is rapidly changing thanks to advances in cryptography. Centralized networks such as aggregator platforms exist solely because of a lack of trust. You trust Amazon to verify that a seller is genuine before you buy goods from them. What if there was a system, far removed from Amazon, that could do the same for you?
What if you could access a network, call it Airbnb, that contained verified and legitimate listings of apartments in an area, and you could interact with the listing owner directly without paying any middleman fees or commissions? That is what a decentralized network secured by cryptography does.
In the 1980s, when cryptography was nascent, a network that could implement it was unthinkable because technology prevented its creation. As time wore on, the cryptographic principles increased in sophistication but the technology was a limiting factor. As early as 1995, researchers had arrived at a network design that would render centralized networks obsolete.
However, there wasn’t a catalyst that could accelerate change. Eventually, the Great Recession of 2008 occurred and this gave rise to Bitcoin and the first application that used the blockchain. If you’ve read even cursorily about Bitcoin, then you probably know this story.
In late 2008, cryptography forums were abuzz with chatter about a new network launched by an entity called Satoshi Nakamoto. To this day, no one knows who this person is or if it’s a person behind Bitcoin. All we know is that Bitcoin made blockchain a reality, and it revolutionized the way we think about networks.
The key to understanding blockchain technology lies in its architecture. It’s a decentralized network and uses cryptography to build trust. Here’s how it works. Each network is a collection of nodes, but there isn’t a central node that reigns supreme over the others. Instead, each node is equal.
When a new piece of information is added to the network, the other nodes verify it and conclude that the information is valid. If it’s valid, a new node is created. If it’s invalid, the network remains as is, uncorrupted. The implications of this architecture are immense.
For starters, existing network security solutions are rendered obsolete. The network can protect itself, and there’s no need for the hundreds of cyber security monitoring software that inundate the market currently. There’s a more powerful effect at play with the blockchain.
As centralized networks became more sophisticated, the increasing sophistication of cyberattackers and fraud artists was a natural side effect. The more organizations (nodes) guarded their secrets, the stronger the efforts to crack them open became. That meant that a technological version of the progress paradox was playing out. As networks became more sophisticated, attackers became so as well, and trust became harder to establish.
In an effort to establish trust, centralized networks overstep their boundaries and erode value to users, reducing trust even more. The blockchain and its decentralized protocols completely change this because it removes the centralized entity. There is nothing to attack. An attack on a single node is repulsed by the entire network immediately.
There is no external security layer to infiltrate. The network is the security layer. Information in one node can be corrupted, but the rest of the network immediately overrides the attacker. To succeed, the attacker needs to compromise every single node at the same time. The only way to do this is to expend vast resources, which makes any reward small in comparison.
For instance, attackers can try to compromise Bitcoin’s network. However, even at its current valuation in the tens of thousands, the reward an attacker is likely to receive from stealing every Bitcoin is diminished by the cost of having to attack every node on the network.
Crucially, the greater the number of nodes in a network (also called blocks in the chain or just blocks), the stronger the network becomes. The resources needed to attack the network increase exponentially with every new block, and thus, trust builds itself the more users increase activity.
In short, blockchain networks don’t create an insurmountable challenge for attackers. They remove the incentive to even think about attacking them in the first place. While Bitcoin and cryptocurrencies are financial examples, there are implications that such architecture has on every single application we currently use.
Decentralization’s greatest promise is the elimination of platforms and their rent-seeking behavior. In economics, rent-seeking is a term attributed to actors that add no value and yet derive monetary gain from a system. An aggregator platform adds no value in a decentralized world and even in a centralized world, doesn’t add as much value as it takes.
Consider Uber or Airbnb. They produce nothing and are valued at billions of dollars, far more than the entities that provide value to these platforms’ users. These platforms sit in the middle and charge a high fee for doing nothing other than putting two parties together. A decentralized network removes the need for Uber, Google, Amazon, or any other rent seeker.
That is because it immediately establishes trust, which is the only reason for a platform to exist. If you were to search for information on a decentralized wiki or shopping network, you’d instantly know that the seller is valid and has been verified by the rest of the network. Network information isn’t saved on a file guarded by sophisticated solutions. Instead, the entire network maintains a transaction record, and information is transparent.
How Cryptography Builds Trust
While removing the incentive to attack a network is great, it doesn’t fully build trust. After all, personal information cannot be made visible to everyone on the network. If you stored personal information such as your birth certificate, social security number, passport number, and so on on a decentralized network, you’d want to restrict access to authorized users.
That is where cryptography enters the picture. Cryptography works by enforcing encryption on a network via keys. Each blockchain network can have its own cryptography algorithm that ensures security. For instance, Bitcoin’s network has two keys, one public and one private. The public key lets everyone know your location on the network. The private key gives you access to your information. Every 10 minutes, all transactions on Bitcoin’s network are verified, cleared, and stored in blocks. Each block is connected to the previous block using a hash.
A hash is an alphanumeric code that contains information about the current block and the previous one in the chain. The Bitcoin blockchain uses blocks to store coin information. However, you can store practically any information—personal data, land records, contract information, news articles, music, art, you name it. All of it is forever secure.
The point I’m making here is, cryptocurrencies are just one manifestation of blockchain. While they may or may not become massively important, the underlying technology they depend on has the power to change the world forever. Imagine a world where there are no centralized institutions or large corporate entities. There are just peer-to-peer networks without middlemen and no transaction fees or anything else to pay.
As technology becomes ever more sophisticated, trust becomes even more scarce. For instance, look at the risk of deep fake technology. Who can say that such tech won’t be used to manipulate information in the future and discredit facts? What if original media were stored on a blockchain where they cannot be altered? People would immediately know fake and original. Transparency would reign, and trust would exist.
Pillars of Trust
Trust isn’t built in a vacuum. Every system has to have certain qualities for it to be trustworthy. While some trustworthy systems might not equally possess all of the qualities I’ll shortly mention, there’s no denying that they have them in some quantity. It’s instructive to examine our current centralized structures and compare them to a decentralized system via the lens of these qualities to see how they stack up.
Honesty
That is an easy one to understand. Establishing trust requires honesty. You wouldn’t trust a company that routinely lied about its operations and misused your data. It’s ironic that currently there’s a huge mistrust of big government and corporations, yet we’re forced to use their services because of the lack of alternatives.
Take Facebook. for example. This company has misused data in every way possible and has violated every data privacy guideline. It has even tried to monopolize the internet in developing countries by restricting access to information and has in-app surveillance algorithms that the NSA would be proud of.
Honest is the last word anyone would use to describe Facebook. Yet, it holds sway over public discourse, and it’s impossible to wean ourselves off the platform since there are no viable alternatives. Compare this to a decentralized social network. How would this latter network fare in terms of honesty?
For starters, there wouldn’t be any moderators or gatekeepers as Facebook currently has. That means everyone would be free to post information they feel is relevant, and the rest of the network would ensure the information in its original state would always exist. That is because a block in a chain cannot be erased or modified. Thus, verifying sources of data would be simple. Fake news claims wouldn’t exist.
However, this is where we would run into the limitations of a fully decentralized network. While legitimate information cannot be erased, illegitimate information cannot be erased either. For instance, if someone decides to run a smear campaign against another entity, there’s no way of removing that media from the network. The blockchain guarantees security, for better or worse.
In such situations, having a centralized moderator makes a lot of sense. It’s the best explanation of why a centralized platform isn’t entirely a bad idea. The best possible solution would be a hybrid consisting of decentralized and centralized platforms, where neither oversteps their boundary and balance one another.
Back to our examination of honesty though, there’s no doubt that a decentralized network is more honest. Information can’t be filtered through a moderator (for better or worse), and its existence is immutable. Whatever qualities one might accuse decentralized networks of being, dishonesty isn’t one of them.
Consideration
In business, consideration refers to an unspoken agreement that all parties will operate in good faith to the mutual benefit of each other. While mutually beneficial agreements don’t always take place, the idea behind this pillar of trust is that everyone receives something from the deal, even if it isn’t exactly what they wanted.
The underlying factor here is that all parties have one another’s goodwill in mind and genuinely respect one another’s desires and interests. The current state of centralized systems doesn’t really reflect this. Take a conventional platform like Airbnb, for instance. On the surface, Airbnb aims to help homeowners and renters find easy accommodations. It gives users full control and highlights a few policies that everyone must follow.
On paper, this sounds great. Renters know they can easily find listings and score prices that are sometimes lower than hotel rates. Property owners can earn more money by leasing their spaces to short-term tenants. A review system is in place that helps everyone get to know one another.
In reality, things don’t play out this way. There’s no denying the value that most people realize on the platform, but Airbnb indemnifies itself from any liability. If you show up at a property as a renter and the owner cancels at the last minute, the owner is to blame, not Airbnb. The worst that happens is that the owner gets banned from the platform.
Similarly, if a renter trashes an owner’s place and inconveniences the neighbors, it’s the owner who needs to pick up the pieces. Airbnb assumes zero liability. The platform doesn’t control the reviews either and leaves everything in the hands of its users. The result is that the system is open to manipulation. It’s no wonder that allegations of racism are rife on the platform. Again, Airbnb makes a few noises about its commitment to diversity, etc., but doesn’t really do anything beyond releasing a few press snippets.
In fairness, a decentralized network wouldn’t do much better since the network cannot govern individual attitudes. However, it’s important to note that a decentralized network will not turn into a billion-dollar company that assumes no liability and arbitrarily controls who can and cannot make money (or rent a place to stay).
Airbnb does little to nothing. They take a cut from the value that everyone else brings to the platform and count the money. It isn’t liable for anything and behaves as such. Uber behaves the same way, as does every other platform out there. If these aggregators cannot guarantee anything and do not assume any responsibility, what’s the point of paying them for a service. A decentralized, open network will do the job just as well.
Centralized platforms do not have their users’ best interests at heart. If they did, they would behave more responsibly and wouldn’t make it so hard to file a complaint. As long as the platform gets paid, they really couldn’t care less about what their users are looking for. Whether it’s taxis, hotels, or short-term stays, platforms add nothing of value to our world and only collect rent due to a lack of alternatives.
A decentralized network governed by peers will have its flaws. Such networks won’t eradicate the problem of incompetence on the user’s part. However, it certainly won’t collect rent and will justify its existence. Think of it as being a decentralized version of Wikipedia or Craigslist. That’s a far better alternative than whatever fancy features Airbnb and Uber (or any platform) bring to the table.
Accountability
This pillar of trust is simple to understand. Any entity must be accountable to stakeholders and must abide by laws and regulations. The moment an institution or entity is not accountable, the trust is lost. The typical platform’s behavior is a good example of how unaccountable they are. However, these companies do have shareholders, and they’re accountable to them.
The financial sector provides a great example of how a large firm is accountable to nobody. In fact, Bitcoin’s creation was driven by the lack of accountability displayed by the financial sector in the aftermath of the Great Recession. Wall Street banks bought toxic assets they didn’t understand, and all they sought to do was sell them to anybody that would buy them.
In return, they either took the other side of the trade (they sold while their investors bought) or took fees on the trade as commissions. Either way, they knew they profited no matter what they did and did not create enough toxic assets for people to buy. This behavior is pretty much the same as what a platform does. Uber cares very little about the quality of the drivers or customers on its platform as long as they have enough of them. Any issues that occur are the user's problem, not Uber's.
Eventually, this house of cards collapsed, and the economy fell apart because of it. In a free-market capitalist system, incompetence and carelessness are punishable by failure. A few big banks like Bear Stearns and Lehman Brothers did go under. However, they were “allowed” to go under by the government and a Treasury Department staffed with ex-employees of Goldman Sachs. When it was Goldman’s turn to go under, the government conveniently stepped in and rescued a firm (AIG) that owed Goldman money. Thus, an insurer was bailed out, and Goldman saved.
Following these failures, a round of Congressional hearings took place, and politicians and bankers took turns yelling at one another. At the end of it, one person went to jail, and even he was jailed because he lied to his bank about where he rerouted client money. Rating agencies, investment banks, and mortgage lenders perpetrated massive fraud, and no one was held accountable. In fact, all of them received taxpayer money to bail themselves out.
The ordinary homeowner did not receive such courtesy. If they lost their homes to foreclosure, that was it. There was no resetting of credit reports or stimulus checks. There was no revamping of a corrupt system. It’s easy to see why Bitcoin arose at this time.
DeFi aims to rectify this system. Financial systems are large and have many different applications. Let’s take the case of an investment asset that Wall Street banks sold to investors. Essentially, Wall Street loaded lots of worthless assets into a single security, bribed the rating agencies to rate them highly, and sold this package to investors. The entire process was opaque, and investors had no way of figuring out what was in these packages.
A decentralized system would enforce transparency easily. That is because every transaction and repackaging of assets will leave a clear audit trail to follow. There is no incentive to bribe a rating agency because it serves no purpose. In fact, the very existence of a rating agency is moot since investors can follow the paper trail easily. If you see toxic assets in the paper trail being repackaged and pushed into other securities, you know it’s a terrible investment asset.
The sales and trading operation of an investment bank will also cease to exist in its current form. Access to the network will be free, so bank traders will not need to push securities to investors. Currently, bank traders act as gatekeepers, charging a toll for access. They create piles of rubbish and charge people money to access them as if they were a bizarre tourist attraction. There’s no value added anywhere in the system, and all the average bank trader and salesperson do is collect rent.
A decentralized system eliminates the need for this structure entirely. A Wall Street bank’s trading desk will be reduced to a simple trading floor, and banks can drop the pretense that they care about their customers. They'll cease being brokers who trade against their customers and become pure traders instead. That’s a far more transparent and accountable structure than the current system.
If any user in the network sells bad assets or deceives anyone else, the rest of the network will not just know about it but will have proof of such behavior. Exclusion from participation in the network would be powerful enough to stop anyone from behaving in any way that harms other users.
Again, a decentralized network won’t solve every issue. If a large number of corrupt users banded together to manipulate the network, decentralization isn’t going to help much. Ceding majority control of a network to a small number of users will lead to monopolistic practices, and decentralized networks cannot prevent this. Each network will have to enforce its policies, and this is a form of centralization.
However, despite these shortcomings, there’s no doubt a decentralized network holds more promise than the current centralized structure when it comes to keeping participants in line and accountable for their actions.
Transparency
Transparency is the final pillar of trust that any network or system has to have to be trustworthy. If no one can figure out what the other user is up to, it leads to a lack of trust. Every company and user is entitled to privacy and trade secrets, but pertinent data that affects the fortunes of a large number of people must be disclosed. Currently, the stock market operates on a system of enforced transparency.
Market regulators like the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) enforce disclosure guidelines on all public companies. For instance, company insiders cannot buy and sell stock whenever they like. They have to disclose all activity to the SEC, and these data are publicly available on the SEC’s website for investors to refer to.
In this case, the stock markets are transparent, but the problem lies with the regulator. The centralized authority has to enforce its laws and hold everyone accountable for it to justify its existence. The SEC suffers in this regard, and at times, its employees would prefer to work for the firms they’re supposed to be regulating. That means the reach of the SEC is shallow, and only the cases with the highest priority receive a fair shake.
A decentralized system would automatically enforce transparency since every participant’s move would be apparent to everyone else. Note that a decentralized network doesn’t have to be the most transparent. It can change its rules to enforce greater privacy. For instance, cryptocurrencies such as Monero hide all transaction information from everyone except users. As a result, these networks aren’t transparent, and there isn’t any accountability.
However, a lack of transparency isn’t an inherent quality of the blockchain. If anything, a blockchain in its truest form is automatically transparent. A network built on it has to build rules to negate transparency. Conversely, a centralized system is opaque to begin with, and rules ensuring transparency have to be specified. Thus, the centralized system is beholden to the regulator or authority that governs it. Corrupt the authority, and you corrupt the entire system.
Taking Control
It’s no secret that a person’s biggest asset these days is their personal data. There’s a saying in tech circles that, “if you cannot spot the product a company is selling, you are the product.” Companies such as Facebook don’t produce anything of value in the traditional sense (or arguably in any sense). That is because their users are the product. The data they collect is sold to advertisers, and those advertisers pay large sums for it.
Something that is inherently yours is being monetized by a company that collects it without your explicit permission. Imagine if someone raided your bank account without your permission, loaned your money to someone else, made a profit on it, and didn’t tell you they were doing all of this. Even worse, what if the entity that raided your bank account couldn’t guarantee that your money would be safe?
Say what you will about banks, but you know your money will be safe with them. Even if a bank goes under, the government will insure their depositors’ money up to $250,000 per person. Facebook and Google, however, offer no such guarantees. They don’t police who uses our data and in what ways. They merely sell them to the highest bidder, earn a profit, and we see nothing of it.
While data privacy laws are increasing around the world, they’re a small step in the right direction. They don’t stop platforms from monetizing our personal assets and controlling the flow of information. The only solution is decentralization. It helps us wrest control back from these centralized structures and truly democratize our world.
There’s no doubt that decentralization in its current form has its flaws. You’ve already read some of them in the preceding section. However, these flaws aren’t as gigantic as the ones that exist in our currently centralized world. The initial days of the internet were a time of hope that was soon corrupted by corporations and platforms.
Decentralization offers a way to reset that balance and move towards a more equitable world. That’s a bold statement to make. However, as you’ll read in this book, it’s a reality that isn't very far away.

Chapter 2:
Blockchain Economy Design Principles
Much of decentralization focuses on the impact it will have on our monetary systems. As such, the term blockchain economy is gaining popularity. What does this mean, though? In short, it refers to apps and systems that are designed with decentralization in mind and do not incorporate any form of centralized control. They’re true peer-to-peer networks that incorporate all of the pillars of trust you read about in the previous chapter.
Financial applications offer a great testbed for decentralized apps because finance manages to gather many of the real-world obstacles that decentralization faces in other applications. It’s no coincidence that one of the first applications to be launched was Bitcoin. What better way to test drive the usability of a decentralized app than to launch a currency?
As such, Bitcoin has been improved upon by other cryptocurrencies. Examining the way they work is instructive because it provides lessons on how decentralization works and the principles that govern its design.
Design Principles
There are seven principles that all blockchain designs rely on. No matter a network’s rules and protocols, you’ll find these principles in every decentralized app. These principles derive from the problems that arise from centralized platforms. The most interesting aspect of these principles is how Bitcoin incorporated all of them before there was any discussion of them.
What I mean is, Bitcoin managed to organically incorporate all of these principles without the need for improvements or iterations. In contrast, if you look at the typical development cycle for a piece of software, it goes through multiple iterations until it arrives at a production-ready state. In Bitcoin’s case, it came fully equipped out of the box.
That highlights the blockchain’s strength as a solution and the ease with which other developers can create applications on it.
The First Principle
The first principle that the blockchain incorporates is network integrity. That feature has everything to do with trust and the transparency of interactions taking place on it. Before Bitcoin launched, there was no network where actions were fully trustworthy. For instance, consider a common financial transaction on the internet. It’s hard to establish trust when buying something online. It’s so hard that you have to share mountains of data with firms like Amazon to complete a transaction. Essentially, you don’t have a choice.
When it comes to money, there is a unique problem from an integrity perspective. Money isn’t data. Data can be replicated and shared in multiple formats. For instance, if you record a video, you can send it to multiple people without hesitation. The network doesn’t lose integrity because the video is the same no matter who consumes it.
However, money doesn’t work the same way. You cannot send a dollar to your friend and send that same dollar to someone else. Money is finite and isn’t replicable. Try to pay someone with non-existent money, and you’ll find yourself running a Ponzi scheme. When you transact online, you have to place your trust in some central authority to ensure there isn’t any double-spending going on. Institutions like banks, card issuers, and large merchant platforms assume this role, but it comes at a high cost.
Decentralized currency networks like Bitcoin have to solve this double-spend problem to establish trust. The way Bitcoin does it is by establishing a consensus mechanism. The best part is that a decentralized consensus mechanism works far better than a central authority. Here’s how Bitcoin’s consensus algorithm works.
Every spend and transaction on the network is time-stamped and thus, subsequent spends of the same coin by the same user are rejected. Miners, who are network participants running nodes, gather recent transactions and create blocks out of them. This process occurs every 10 minutes. As I mentioned previously, each block contains a hash that refers to the preceding block. Without the hash, a block is invalid.
The Bitcoin blockchain is public, which means everyone can view transactions taking place. In essence, Bitcoin transactions are more traceable and transparent than cash. From a technical standpoint, the consensus algorithm is a seemingly complex one. Don’t worry. I’m not going to bore you with jargon.
Bitcoin’s consensus algorithm is named Proof of Work and it’s a simple yet powerful algorithm. On Bitcoin’s network, no user can select the identity of the miner that creates the latest block. Instead, it’s a merit-based process where miners have to compete for the right to do so. The reward is doled out by having miners solve a cryptographic puzzle that is hard to solve but easy to verify.
Whoever solves the problem the quickest gets to create the next block. In solving the puzzle, miners expend computing hardware and electricity. The puzzle itself involves deciphering the next block’s hash. Once the puzzle is solved, Bitcoin is awarded to the miner. Its cryptographic roots assure no shortcuts or ways to game the system when solving for an answer.
Thus, when an answer is produced, the entire network trusts it because everyone knows the work that went into solving the problem. Also, note that the puzzle-solving process is a constant one. It isn’t as if a bell rings every 10 minutes and everyone starts figuring out puzzles. Over 500,000 trillion hashes are being deciphered every second. It’s just that the likelihood of a hash being solved is 10 minutes.
Some miners might take a minute while others might take an hour. Solving the problem itself is an iterative process with miners making a variety of inputs and trying to match the output to what the network expects. The expected hash value is set by the network and is adjusted every 2,016 blocks. If the miner’s output is incorrect, they adjust their inputs and try once again.
Currently, the number of hash attempts on the Bitcoin network is over 700 million trillion. That’s a ton of resources spent on solving hashes to earn a Bitcoin. As you can see, there are some shortcomings to the proof of work consensus mechanism. It prioritizes miners who have the most firepower. The more powerful your computer is, the greater are your chances of earning Bitcoin.
Proof of work also exposes the network to manipulation. Let’s say a group of miners who represent 51% of the network decide to band together and mine coins in tandem. The majority of coins generated from that point onwards would belong to those miners, and they would control the network. At that point, decentralization doesn’t exist anymore. Naturally, trust erodes in such an environment.
In a bid to avoid such a calamity, alternatives emerged. Ethereum is the second most popular cryptocurrency, and its first version used the proof of work mechanism. However, Ethereum 1.1 replaced proof of work with proof of stake. Proof of stake requires miners to invest in the network and have current ownership of coins to participate.
Voting on the blockchain doesn’t require heavy firepower as a result, and only those who are invested in the network and have spent time and resources developing it get to have a say in its propagation. It’s a fairer system of determining who gets rewarded. It avoids the issue of someone entering the network with an arsenal of computers and overtaking existing miners. It removes any incentive for an arms race.
There are other consensus mechanisms out there. Ripple and Stellar use social networks to build consensus and require new entrants to generate at least 100 nodes before gaining the right to vote on a transaction. Essentially, this is the proof of work algorithm with some social media thrown into it. Social engineers and trolls can manipulate it. It automatically favors someone famous and as such, it isn’t a very good model.
Another mechanism is titled proof of activity. This protocol combines proof of work and proof of stake. A random selection of miners signs off on a block by solving its hash before it’s added to the network. A list of miners who have been pre-qualified using the proof of work and stake algorithms makes up the selection. The downside of this consensus mechanism is that transaction times take a while to complete.
Currently, Bitcoin’s average transaction time is 10 minutes which is interminably long. This mechanism takes even longer since so much depends on the list of miners reaching consensus. Imagine standing in line to pay for something using Bitcoin and having to wait 10 minutes or more to confirm payment.
Proof of storage and proof of capacity are alternative consensus mechanisms. Both require miners to allocate a significant portion of their hard drive to mining or a significant portion of their shared disk space if they’re operating in the cloud. How do these mechanisms help build network integrity?
Storage on a blockchain is an important function. When we think of data storage, we usually think of the way data is stored on the internet. Think of an article published on BuzzFeed or some other clickbait website. The article is up for a long time, but it’s consumed and referred to for a relatively short time. Eventually, it gets lost in the recesses of storage. It’s time-stamped, but its time of publication isn’t important to the network it exists on.
After all, a new article published after it doesn’t derive any meaning from the original date of publication. If you fail to notice a timestamp on a humorous article, you’re not going to immediately swear off the website. However, data on a blockchain functions differently, as does storage.
Returning to the Bitcoin blockchain, a single coin cannot be used multiple times. That means it has to reference its history and the history of the blockchain. That is why the timestamp is so important. Before approving a transaction, the coin has to provide the network the history of the total number of coins that existed (to make sure coins haven’t magically appeared or disappeared) and that the coin isn’t existing doubly in someone else’s wallet (to prevent double-spending).
That calls for a lot of storage space, and thus, the amount of storage a miner dedicates to the network is a good indicator of the trust they can bring to it. The more storage a blockchain network has to call upon, the more trustworthy it is. Thus, it makes sense to value storage and reward those who contribute a lot of it. The problem is that these algorithms are proof of work, rebranded.
They turn storage into the subject of an arms race instead of computing power. As such, there are limitations here as well. However, these mechanisms are the best that we have right now. The future will undoubtedly give us even more mechanisms. Currently, proof of stake is perhaps the best solution, but it disadvantages new entrants, which justifies the existence of alternative mechanisms.
Whatever the limitations of these mechanisms might be, there’s no doubt that establishing trust is simple in a decentralized network. The entire peer network takes part in establishing it, and it isn’t left to some centralized authority to determine what passes and what fails. The result is that we don’t need the traditional central authorities like a central bank or retail banks to govern the flow of money.
As our world becomes increasingly digital, establishing trust online has become more important than ever. The blockchain’s consensus mechanisms help establish trust and create lasting changes in our societies.
The Second Principle
The blockchain’s second principle is that of distributed or democratized power. It’s a well-established fact that the best form of governance is a democracy. It isn’t perfect, but systems work better in the long run when everyone affected by it has a say in how it’s run. Computer network architecture has never followed this pattern.
Instead, most network architecture has resembled a monarchy where a single authoritarian controls everything. While decisions are made faster in such network designs, they also open it to a single point of failure. Attack the central authority or subjugate it, and you control the whole network.
Blockchain distributes network power across every participant. There is no single point of control, and neither can any single party shut the system down. If a portion of the system is isolated or cut off from the rest, it isn’t an issue since the blockchain’s record is maintained by every node in the network. Thus, once the isolated nodes come back online, they fit back in seamlessly.
The only vulnerability is when a majority of malicious nodes take over the entire system and try to manipulate it. While such attacks cannot be fully prevented, they can be spotted from a mile away since every other node will receive a copy of the transactions in the network. Thus, decentralized power exists for longer and in safer hands.
One of the reasons we need decentralized systems is because internet giants have repeatedly violated the social contract they have with their users. They’ve used private assets to get wealthy and have used them without permission. They’re unlikely to ever face any penalty for doing so. Governments are no better, using the internet to spy on their citizens and use personal data against them.
Worst of all, these centralized institutions spend billions of dollars each year securing themselves, but their inherent flaw never disappears. All of them continue to have a single point of failure and continue to provide attackers an incentive to test their defenses.
Blockchain revolutionizes this picture because there is no need for cybersecurity when the network itself is secure. Overpowering the blockchain requires more resources than rewards can compensate attackers with. What’s more, the Bitcoin blockchain (and every other public blockchain) embraces a concept called bootstrapping. That isn’t bootstrapping in the business sense.
In blockchain terms, bootstrapping refers to any user’s ability to download the Bitcoin client onto their local machine and participate in the network. It’s distributed across a volunteer network and rests on millions of computers worldwide simultaneously. The result is that attackers can never disable or isolate the network.
Governments and authoritarian heads of state cannot control the flow of money through executive orders. Currently, governments control the flow of money by squeezing bank accounts. Banks, for their part, don’t enforce citizen’s rights as much as question why citizens need rights in the first place. Blockchain removes the middleman and thus removes the bottleneck to a democratized system completely.
Best of all, the network’s rules are simple, and coin creation is transparent. A coin is a reward provided to miners who manage to solve the crypto puzzle and add a new block to the network. The entire network gets to view the proof of work, and there are no doubts about the veracity of a transaction.
No Federal Reserve is turning the supply of money on and off as they wish. There are no banks conducting compliance checks to control the flow of money. It gives every user on the network full control of their assets and creates a robust network that is almost impossible to hack or compromise.
The Third Principle
One of the curious things about centralized systems is that the incentive system is all wrong. Let’s take a bank, for instance. Banks make money from the interest they earn on loans. People who have good credit scores and can make payments on time typically receive low interest rates as an incentive to sign up for a loan. However, there are only so many people with good credit, and every bank competes for their attention.
As a result of this system, two outcomes take place. The first is that banks that market their services well capture the lion’s share of the good customers and ignore the rest. That means those with poor or no credit, such as students, get ignored. These are the very people who need financial assistance, but most banks don’t lend money to them.
That brings us to the second outcome. The less successful banks prey on these people and offer them credit cards and other costly loans with high interest rates. These loans are often packaged as credit builder loans or adjustable-rate or zero-down loans. The bottom line with all of them is a high interest rate that boosts bank profits.
Short-term incentives such as these lead to more defaults and a perennial debt cycle for the middle and lower classes. In turn, this widens the income inequality in society which fuels social unrest. That is the cycle that created the 2008 financial crisis, and it continues to this day.
The blockchain and Bitcoin’s network sought to repair this system by realigning incentives. First, distributed power removes any incentive for attackers to compromise the system. Thus, security came built-in. Next, the proof of work consensus system rewards those who take part in the system and nurture it. The earliest miners on the Bitcoin network received 50 BTC for every node they verified.
That was the reward offered during the first three years of BTC’s existence. This reward size didn’t remain consistent. The reward size was decreased to incentivize further long-term participation and widespread adoption. After all, as BTC grew more popular, the network had to make sure that the earliest and most loyal adopters were rewarded well.
This message resonated with later adopters since they understood that the earlier someone adopted BTC, the more they’d be rewarded. However, there was danger lurking here. If people kept adopting BTC, inflation would eventually strike. The value of BTC would diverge from its price since supply would be unlimited.
To counter this, Nakamoto placed a cap on the number of BTC that could be mined on the network. According to current mining rates, the final BTC will be mined in 2041. The Limited supply gives inherent value to every coin, and as a result, inflation is countered.
The bottom line of all of these features is everyone is incentivized to play by the network’s rules and assist each other in increasing the democratization of the network. There’s nothing to be gained by preying on someone else, and no authority can ever take over the network. Everyone benefits when users share the benefits. Thus, incentives are in line with network propagation.
These features have significant implications beyond currencies. What if smart contracts securing proof of assets could be stored on the blockchain? What if property and land records could be stored? The entire economic system would incentivize itself for everyone’s mutual benefit. There wouldn’t be any preying on the underserved or vulnerable sections of society.
The blockchain would also eliminate middlemen who collect rent from our activities. Given the advances in the internet of things, what if payments could be handled in Bitcoin (or any cryptocurrency) from one IT device to another without the need to convert money between multiple fiat currencies and pay a gate fee to intermediaries?
I mentioned previously that the blockchain represents a chance for our society to have a do-over with the internet. The initial internet era, call it Web 1.0, was a collaborative place where users contributed to its growth. The rise of Google, Facebook, Amazon, and Apple killed that, and these corporations took control of communications using stolen assets.
Centralized systems eventually get cannibalized by big corporations and entities. The field of artificial intelligence is currently having its transition moment from Web 1.0 to whatever came after that. Initial AI research was built on open-source libraries and collaboration. However, we’re now starting to see commercial AI development where progress is walled off from the rest of the market and benefits a select few as they seek to dominate the marketplace.
That is impossible to execute on a blockchain since there’s no incentive to do so. As a result, the network will always be decentralized and democratic.
The Fourth Principle
As with anything on the internet or any network for that matter, security is an extremely important feature. The internet, as it currently stands, is an extremely unsafe place. Cyberattacks, ransomware, malware, phishing attacks, and cyberbullying are common occurrences, and we spend significant resources protecting ourselves from their ill effects. When designing the Bitcoin blockchain, Satoshi Nakamoto engineered an almost foolproof cryptographic algorithm that leverages an age-old security concept.
It’s called asymmetric cryptography, and it uses two keys to ensure security. One key handles encryption, and the other handles decryption, which explains the term asymmetric. The concept of asymmetric cryptography isn’t new. It’s been around since the 1970s. In cryptographic circles, using old algorithms is a good thing since they’re tried and tested.
Unlike cybersecurity devices that have to keep evolving to stay abreast of attacks, cryptography algorithms that survive the test of time tend to be the most stable. New algorithms eventually get exposed by some little-known vulnerability. An algorithm that has existed for over 40 years has clearly withstood many attacks. It explains why the U.S Department of Defense is the only other network larger than the Bitcoin network to use this form of security.
In this system, the onus of security is on the user. They have to secure their private keys at all costs, and if they forget their keys, they cannot recover them. The information continues to exist on the blockchain but it’s not accessible. No one else can access it without the private key either. The earlier days of Bitcoin saw users misunderstand this concept and trust their private keys to third parties.
The result was massive theft and loss of value. However, as time has moved on, knowledge has increased. The need to always secure a private key does open a vulnerability; therefore, cybersecurity isn’t rendered totally obsolete. However, it is localized to a single device or piece of hardware that stores the key. As such it doesn’t have to be exposed to the network and this minimizes the chances that it will be hacked.
Security is also tied to incentives and ends up as a by-product. Here’s how it works. We know that the longest blockchain is the most secure since the resources needed to overpower it are too expensive. A chain grows longer only when users are active and keep adding blocks to it. In the Bitcoin blockchain, users are incentivized to add blocks as previously explained, and thus, as activity increases, the chain becomes longer and more secure.
As our world becomes more dependent on technology, adopting the blockchain for its security standards alone makes a ton of sense. Imagine a situation where a self-driving car is hacked by someone halfway across the world through a centralized network. What if missiles and other weapons were hijacked? Our world is interconnected, and distances have been reduced to nothing.
Centralized networks cannot provide full security because of their single point of failure. Attackers always know where to look. In contrast, attackers don’t know where to begin with the blockchain, and even if they do, it takes a lot to overpower the system before the rest of the network fights back. Its simplicity ensures security.
Security isn’t a principle of the network as much as it is a natural by-product. The only addition that decentralized applications like Bitcoin add, is the use of a cryptographic protocol to enhance security. Different networks use different protocols. Much like how consensus mechanisms differ, there is some debate about which protocol offers the best security.
For instance, Monero uses ring signatures that are slightly different from the asymmetric cryptography protocol Bitcoin uses. Ring signatures mask everything about a transaction and thus combine privacy and security into one bundle. Whichever protocol is used, there’s no doubt that security is a guarantee on the blockchain.
The Fifth Principle
Big data has transformed businesses in every sector. Corporations collect more data than ever before. Simply accessing a website means providing the website’s owner access to your IP address, where you live, your browsing habits, your preferences, and in some cases, your preferred third-party websites. When Google first introduced Google Analytics, it was an innocent enough project. Website owners could track what their visitors were seeing and use that to improve their sites. Google did this by installing a file called a cookie on the visitor’s computer.
Then companies like Facebook and Amazon came along and turned the humble cookie into a cookie monster. Facebook was especially prolific in this area, installing cookies that tracked browser activity not just on Facebook but outside of it as well. If you visited a shopping website after visiting Facebook, the company knew about it. They tracked how much time you spent on that website, which pages you looked at, and what you hovered your mouse over, and for how long.
Google caught up quickly with these practices, and eventually, banner ads that followed you from one website to the next began appearing. In recent times, Apple has made a huge fuss about privacy, but in reality, all they’ve done is gate customer data away from the other big tech companies. That’s why you’ve never been able to use Apple’s products easily with other platforms and have to choose Apple’s network to solve your issues.
Data is wealth these days, and data privacy is at the core of the blockchain’s design. Every application built on it adheres to this principle, and it’s impossible to erase it. Transactions carried out on Ethereum’s blockchain (or any cryptocurrency) are private and crucially aren’t identity-linked.
You can download the Ethereum client on your machine and start participating on it. You don’t have to provide an email address or any other identification. When you transact on the network, your wallet (where you store your coins) is the only data available to the public.
That doesn’t mean privacy is guaranteed. It is possible to triangulate data points in transactions and verify who owns a wallet. However, unlike centralized mechanisms, the blockchain’s apps don’t make it immediately obvious. The entire network is transaction-based, not identity-based.
That has both advantages and disadvantages. On one hand, you’re assured that there isn’t some government agent snooping on your transaction, building a data avatar of you. Think of it this way: If you use Google regularly, have an Android phone that tracks your location, visit websites that have Google Analytics enabled, and use Google Pay to pay for products and services, Google knows everything about you.
They know what you like, dislike, where you’ve been, what you ate yesterday, what clothes you like wearing, and even what your romantic life looks like. Substitute the word Google for Government, and everyone would be up in arms about this scenario. However, since a corporation is doing this and not a totalitarian government, we accept it passively. Governments do track us like this, and even democratic societies have turned into surveillance states where we have to prove that we don’t need to be tracked instead of receiving our basic right of privacy as standard.
The blockchain flips this picture on its head and removes the surveillance aspect completely. People can share what they want to share and nothing else. If you want to share browsing habits with a trusted company, you can opt-in. Currently, you have to opt out of data sharing, which highlights how our privacy is disrespected.
The flip side of the privacy debate is that without identity-linked networks, crime would spread like wildfire. This argument is true. Bitcoin’s early days were characterized by criminals laundering their cash using coins and evading taxes. As such, this doesn’t help society, and no one would argue that money laundering is a price to pay for privacy.
However, it isn’t as if identity-linked networks stop or eliminate financial crime either. There’s a reason tax havens exist. Why should every person in society give up their right to privacy for the sake of combating a select few who engage in criminal activity? In the guise of monitoring criminal activity, banks and governments routinely overstep their boundaries and treat everyone as if they’re guilty.
A good solution would be to combine decentralization with centralized networks. Currently, cryptocurrency exchanges work this way, even though they lean heavily towards centralization. Coinbase, for instance, requires users to prove their identity before granting access to their trading software. There’s no earthly reason to do this other than to allow the government to track capital gains profits. However, the IRS mandates users file taxes on profits earned from cryptocurrencies. So why is there a dual-layer of centralization?
The bottom line is that the blockchain represents a massive step forward for privacy thanks to its design. We will need to design a framework that eliminates the need for identity tracking to the extremes it’s currently taken to and prevent crime at the same time.
The Sixth Principle
Imagine you’re running an eCommerce store selling products. A customer likes your product and pays for it via Paypal. You have an account with Paypal, and you receive the money. The problem is Paypal arbitrarily decides to hang onto your cash for 30 days because there have been instances of fraud previously. Instead of prosecuting fraudulent cases, they punish everyone by assuming ownership of their cash and only send it to you if you accept their terms and conditions. There’s no way to fight the system, and customer service is non-existent.
That is a real scenario that online merchants face all the time. Banks aren’t much better. Let’s say you’re running a business and suddenly land a major deal that’s many figures greater than what you typically earn. The bank will freeze your account almost certainly until you can explain where the money comes from. Even if you do explain it adequately, you’re at their mercy and depend on them to give you access to your cash. All of this is in the name of compliance. You’re treated as a criminal and have to prove your innocence.
Centralized systems such as these trample all over our rights without a second thought. Paypal’s user agreement famously specifies that their users waive the right to impose a class action lawsuit on the company by using their services. Such arrogance comes about only thanks to centralized systems. The blockchain adjusts this balance and gives people their rights back. In the examples above, rights are being stolen by incompetent middlemen whose services we need due to a lack of alternatives.
On a blockchain, there are no middlemen, and there’s no possibility of such occurrences taking place. There’s a larger picture of the rights issue. Let’s say you’re a filmmaker and create media. This media is then uploaded on Youtube by someone in its original format, and your film is now free for everyone to see, and you don’t get a cent in return.
You can lease the rights to view the film (or artwork) to someone else in exchange for royalties, but why would anyone pay money for something that’s free? As a result, trust words. Centralized platforms such as Youtube enforce creator’s rights, but there is no end to pirated movie clips and songs on the platform. Besides, if Youtube can run ads on videos and earn money, this hardly aligns with the interests of the creators.
Even here, youtube absolves itself of all rights once the content is uploaded. If you think your content has been pirated, it’s up to you to flag it as copyrighted content and have it taken down. Youtube decides whether your claim is true or not, and there’s no way to appeal the decision. What gives Youtube the right to act as a judge? They’re a middleman, nothing else. They don’t create anything and yet assume all the power.
Social media platforms often behave this way and have humungous control over what information is disseminated. In the United States, you’ll find right and left-wing extremists complaining about censorship and might think it’s a good thing that extreme content is censored.
However, this isn’t the case in other countries. For instance, India is a democratic country. Yet, the Indian government has never hesitated to push its agenda on social media and crackdown on dissent. Dissenting opinions are censored by platforms such as Facebook and Twitter, and even Google. Dissenters are prosecuted for contradicting the government, and social media companies couldn’t care less because they get to continue with business as usual.
Middlemen get to decide who receives their rights and what gets taken away from us these days. The blockchain goes a long way towards eliminating this imbalance. No one needs a middleman on the blockchain. The blockchain isn’t perfect, though. Some content should be censored, and not everything is suitable for public consumption.
However, rectifying this problem is as simple as creating rules on the network. Currently, there are no rules, just arbitrary judgments made on centralized networks. The blockchain also allows for ownership rights preservation thanks to smart contracts. These contracts can enforce complex criteria much better than any platform can.
Returning to the example of the film, you can store a record of ownership rights on the blockchain and enforce a smart contract with a counterparty. As long as the other person pays you a certain amount of Ethereum every month, they get to use the likeness of that media. If they violate terms by not paying you the precise amount, the contract automatically disables access.
Imagine a decentralized version of Youtube where access to the original content is gated like this. Instead of paying Youtube and Google money to merely host content, you can pay the creator directly. There won’t be any need for middlemen like these platforms or even movie studios and other rent-seekers.
The biggest advantage is that rights are transferred fully back to the transacting parties. They aren’t held hostage by a middleman in the name of compliance. Everything from intellectual property to property rights can be controlled by those who own them. As a result, the economy becomes more efficient.
The Seventh Principle
For an economy to prosper, every section of society has to be included within it. It’s safe to say that the number of people not included in today’s economy is far greater than the number of people who are. For instance, the elderly, the young, and those with poor credit aren’t served by any banking institution. Instead, they’re taken advantage of.
The situation is even worse in developing and underdeveloped countries, where rights are often ignored. Inclusion is perhaps the greatest principle of the blockchain. While the blockchain on which cryptocurrencies like Ethereum and Bitcoin rest are designed on TCP/IP protocols, they can be accessed without access to the internet.
That means you can access them with a simple flip phone. Services such as BitMoby, built on the Bitcoin network, allow people in underdeveloped countries to top up their mobile phone balance by sending the service an amount of Bitcoin. This service is great because there aren’t any intermediaries that charge a fee or impose transaction limits.
The underserved portions of society typically cannot afford the bank account minimum balance. Everything is literally more expensive in our current society if you’re poor. Your home is more expensive, your credit cards charge you more, and you cannot access benefits because your credit score is too low. Anyone can transact in cryptocurrencies because there are no limits imposed.
These days money is increasingly digitized, and cash has been marginalized. The result is that the underserved sections of society have been forced to move online and accept services that don’t benefit them. They have to endure high minimum balances because they need a debit card to pay for goods.
In parts of Europe, businesses charge additional commissions for cash transactions which compounds the problem. The picture is even worse in less developed countries. When the economies of countries like Greece, Cyprus, and Turkey tanked, citizens were not allowed to withdraw large sums of money from their accounts. Some even froze the ability to withdraw cash.
Actions like these negatively impact the poorest sections of society. Without access to cash or credit, there’s no way for these people to survive. Wealthier members of society will receive credit in such times and can get by, but this isn’t an option for the underserved. A similar situation played out in 2016 in India when the government outlawed cash and embarked on a hair-brained exercise to replace the currency in circulation.
The result was a decimation of the agricultural sector and the closure of small businesses. Needless to say, any dissent over this move was quashed by the government on social media, with the platforms right alongside them. Blockchain-based networks make it impossible for such situations to occur. When Venezuela devalued its currency and banned foreign exchange, citizens traded Bitcoin for goods. It helped them beat hyperinflation and continue to live.
Aside from monetary benefits, there are social ones too. Centralized networks make it easy to squash dissent and propagate a single point of view. Decentralization allows dissent fully, and public debate has the opportunity to be more reasoned. There’s no denying that a few extreme views will be circulated.
However, it isn’t as if centralized networks prevent this from happening at the moment. The premise behind a centralized network lies in the incorruptibility of the entity in charge. Expecting Facebook and Twitter to be incorruptible is laughable, as these companies’ actions have repeatedly shown.
Blockchain and decentralization are necessary for society to continue to advance and realize the original promise of the internet. It promises to end glass ceilings and dismantle the good old boys’ clubs that currently rule centralized networks.
The bottom line is that these seven principles offer us a chance to redesign our economy along the lines of positive values. Centralization has served the world well this far, but thanks to the power they placed in the hands of central authorities, they’ve become corrupt. Individual rights no longer matter, and things like privacy and respect have become optional. Only those who can pay for it can access it.
Worse, centralized institutions gather our data but do not guarantee its safety. A single hack and your personal details are out there, ready for everyone to exploit. Even privacy-touting Apple has suffered from iCloud hacks that have exposed people’s personal and most intimate details to the world. Apple has never paid the price for such lapses and continues to push people to store data on its cloud.
Even something as simple as browsing the web requires us to install privacy-related plugins to disable tracking. It ought to be the other way around. Our privacy should come pre-installed and not be an optional extra. Blockchain isn’t just technological innovation; it offers us the chance to redesign our world. We’d be wise to take it as soon as possible.

Chapter 3:
Financial Reinvention
The majority of the world lives on less than $2 per day. Granted, $2 goes a lot further in some parts of the world than in others. However, it’s important to examine why this happens. Global remittances are a cash cow for pretty much every large bank. Let’s look at what happens when a daily laborer earns their wages and remits that money back home to their family.
The laborer gets paid via check (since they’re too poor to open a bank account). The company that pays them has to pay their bank one or two percent in commissions for the privilege of issuing that check. The laborer cashes that check using a service that deducts 4% in fees. Then, they head over to a money transfer service to wire money and get hit with commissions, exchange rate fluctuations, and intermediary fees.
This money changes hands no less than five times before arriving in the family’s hands in the destination country. If the family cannot explain who sent them the money or why, the money, less the fees charged, is sent back to the laborer. One would think the financial intermediaries are the ones adding value to the system here, but all they need to do is shift cash from point A to B. Instead, they end up sucking up 8 to 10% of the amount being wired.
Our current financial systems are a Frankenstein’s monster. The processes on which the system runs date back to the late 1700s. That isn’t an exaggeration. New York State’s money transfer laws date back to the Civil War era when money was moved as cash on horse buggies.
Technological improvements have come and gone, but banks still see fit to work as if we’re living in the 1800s. Digital improvements have simply been layered on top of old processes. Essentially, manual processes have been digitized instead of being redesigned.
No other industry has been as resistant to change as the financial sector has. It likes to portray itself as a fast-paced sector where only the best and brightest work. However, it produces nothing and charges sky-high fees. When you pay for your latte at Starbucks, the transaction is approved immediately.
However, it takes three days for the money to arrive in Starbucks’ account. In between, it passes through at least two intermediaries who siphon as much as two percent of the purchase price. If you paid using an American Express card, the merchant loses four or five percent! Starbucks has branches all around the world, and to service them, it needs bank accounts in all of those locations.
