Let’s say a branch in Midtown Manhattan has to transfer money to headquarters located in Seattle. The branch manager has to approve the transfer and initiate a wire to Seattle. The wire takes two days to go through. Starbucks cannot use that money as capital for their business while it’s in transit.
However, the bank can use it to earn interest and profits in the interim. All of this despite the fact that Starbucks is the one creating value and the bank is doing nothing except moving Starbucks’ money to Starbucks’ accounts. If a person were to do what banks do daily, they’d likely land in jail for fraud. However, banks remain the same.
Every sector has embraced technological change, but finance doesn’t change. Why is this? The best explanation is that finance is a monopoly. For the longest time, the sector has been run by a few behemoths, and they populate not just their sector but the government as well. For instance, the U.S Treasury and Federal Reserve Bank are populated with former Goldman Sachs employees.
The logic behind such hires doesn’t make sense. A career spent shuffling paper from one client to another somehow prepares an investment banker for a role dictating monetary policy? During the economic crisis from 2007 to 2009, the Treasury Department was headed by Hank Paulson, former investment banker and CEO of Goldman Sachs. Paulson repeatedly stated that the housing market was at the bottom in August 2007.
Fast forward two years later, and he had written checks to his investment banking buddies worth $475 billion. That’s taxpayer money, by the way. Paulson claimed it was returned at profit. However, the taxpayer didn’t see any of it. It was simply re-routed to other bailout programs.
All of these events are facts, and I’m not trying to whip outrage. It’s a fact that the financial sector requires a massive overhaul, and the people currently in charge are least likely to approve of it. Note that I’m not advocating for Bitcoin or cryptocurrencies here. Many of the statements regarding cryptocurrencies from bank CEOs have made economic sense.
However, none of them have adopted the blockchain, which is the real revolution.
How Blockchain Redesigns Finance
The currency financial system uses certain justifications to perpetuate itself. You’ve already read about how everything centers around identity in the modern financial world. The blockchain revises that and places the transaction at the center of everything. That means much of the “value” that the financial system claims to provide is unnecessary.
Here are the key points that modern finance pushes as value drivers and how the blockchain negates all of them.
Attestation
That is the primary pillar on which modern finance is built. “Who” is involved in a transaction is far more important than “what” is being transacted. The financial system claims that verifying identities prevents financial crimes and reduces negative impacts on society. As long as there wasn’t any alternative, this pillar is a genuine value-add.
However, this isn’t the case anymore. The reason identity is important is because it’s the only way we can build trust in a centralized system. If you know who is on the other side of the transaction, you can derive many other data points that build trust. You know where they live, their transaction history, their financial profile, and other important data.
Blockchain changes all of this. For the first time in history, trust isn’t built on identity. It doesn’t matter who is on the other side of the transaction; the network validates and secures the product or goods being transacted. The record is indelible, and there’s no need for an intermediary to validate identities.
It’s hard for most of us to imagine this since we’ve become accustomed to building trust, based on who someone is. The larger the store, the better its trustworthiness. You’d rather buy goods from Amazon than some seller in rural nowhere since you know Amazon but don’t know that seller. Never mind that Amazon sources product from that seller anyway.
The blockchain allows you to pay the seller directly since the transaction is validated by the rest of the network. If you didn’t receive goods on time, a smart contract automatically withholds payment, and you don’t have to pay them money. A centralized system has you paying money first and then waiting for your goods. If you don’t receive them, you request a refund and go through a painful process.
If your goods are defective, you need to mail them back to the seller and wait for your money to return. The blockchain removes all of this (except the mailing it back to the seller part) by guarding the transaction via a smart contract. Money can be securely and instantly placed in escrow until both parties satisfy the terms of the contract. Thus, there is no intermediary, and everything is automated.
Imagine the implications this has for trade finance. Banks designed fancy instruments that come very close to what smart contracts do. They’re called Letters of Credit or LCs, and there are many kinds of them. These contracts contain clauses that both parties in a deal must satisfy before the bank releases money from escrow. Usually, there is no escrow account. The bank merely verifies whether either party has the funds and goods needed.
The problem is that banks charge huge fees for the process since they’re the arbiter. The seller and the buyer transact through the bank and don’t have any say in how the process unfolds. It’s either the bank’s way or the highway. Furthermore, these instruments are available only to the most profitable customers the bank has. A small business cannot receive such service.
That leads to such businesses relying on credit or paying cash to someone thousands of miles away and hoping they receive their goods. Blockchain networks bring the sophistication of modern finance to the masses for free. Smart contracts can be coded to do literally anything the parties in a deal want and enforced for free. Bank officers no longer need to be in the middle, dictating terms when they’re neither producing goods nor paying for them.
A contract’s legitimacy is guaranteed since a record on the blockchain cannot be erased. The rest of the network validates it before it is added, and thus, trust is established at a transaction level. If identity verification is needed, a small centralized layer can do this. Alternatively, as is the case with Bitcoin, triangulation will reveal an identity behind an address. Thus, identity becomes a side-effect or a nice to have in a transaction.
The transaction itself is secured, and this is a huge shift in the way we think about conducting them.
Cost
Many commentators wrongly assume that the argument for decentralization is an us versus them topic between proponents of decentralized apps and banks. Banks stand to gain massively from blockchain adoption. Network maintenance and transaction validation are perennial back-office tasks at big banks. The Spanish banking giant Santander reported that they could save $20 billion in annual costs by adopting blockchain networks (Baxi, 2015).
The blockchain automatically validates and maintains transaction records, which eliminates the need for such back-office tasks. Banks can potentially use that money to offer banking services to those who really need them instead of focusing on the rich all the time. Banks earn hefty fees from transaction costs too.
The removal of a bank from a money transfer chain will bring greater value to consumers. The fees that the Bitcoin network charges for a transaction are a small fraction of what banks charge. The result is greater value for a consumer. When the cost savings to banks and lower transaction fees are combined, it’s easy to see how small businesses and bootstrapping entrepreneurs benefit from this ecosystem.
Anyone with a smartphone and access to the internet can use banking services at a low cost. That, in turn, will spur economic activity and help us build a productive society. That is one of the few areas where there are literally zero negatives to blockchain adoption. Typically, some form of centralization is needed to make a decentralized network practical.
However, cost elimination has no negatives, and everyone in the current ecosystem benefits from it. Banks currently justify charging costs because of the lack of alternatives. However, with the blockchain a reality these days, it’s hard to see how this argument is valid.
Speed
While wire transfer times are now notoriously poor, few people realize that the entire chain of financial settlements takes a long time. For instance, stock market trades take three days to settle despite electronic trading occurring in nanoseconds or less. A bank loan trade takes a staggering 23 days to clear. I’ve already mentioned how your Starbuck latte transaction takes three to four days to settle.
The reason it takes this long is because of a chain of intermediaries, all of them grabbing a piece of the pie. All they’re doing is pushing paper from one point to another. International transactions can have as many as five to six intermediaries doing nothing other than delivering a message from one to another.
Banks justify these transfer times by using a variety of arguments. Typically they’re some combination of compliance, network issues, and ledger balancing. All of them are hogwash. In a world where communication occurs instantly, nothing justifies spending three or more days clearing a digital transaction. The only explanation is inertia, or worse, an old boy’s club that doesn’t want to change.
There are side effects to such a structure. First off, transactions have more failure points. If a clearinghouse fails, the stock market is in serious jeopardy. Clearinghouses are firms that settle trades, and there aren’t too many of them in the United States. To mitigate the threat of failure, the intermediary firms require huge capital deposits. A deposit is wasted money.
It sits in the bank as a guarantee and doesn’t do anything. Imagine opening a business banking account and being asked to maintain a $10,000 minimum balance. That’s wasted money since you can’t use it in your business. The bank doesn’t pay you interest but uses it as a guarantee in case you default on some of your contracts. That’s pretty much how financial intermediaries work.
The institutions that suffer in this setup are brokerages, small banks, and individuals. The smaller institutions pass those deposit costs on to their customers since you can bet they’re not going to pay any fees out of pocket. That is why banks have minimum balance requirements and account maintenance fees. It’s why brokers sell your order flow to hedge funds for a fee. These actions only increase the degree of manipulation in the system and reduce trust.
Blockchain removes all of this since there are no intermediaries. Ethereum’s current transaction time hovers between five to ten minutes. While this is a long wait for a consumer transaction, it’s incredibly efficient when looking at it from an institutional perspective.
A wire transfer would only take 10 minutes instead of three days. A loan exchange would take just 10 minutes instead of a month. Trade financing would take 10 minutes to settle instead of a week. Smaller institutions would also benefit since they wouldn’t need to deposit large sums of capital to guarantee against collapse. That capital can be put to better use.
Risk Management
Lengthy transaction times jeopardize our financial system in many ways. The current system has no solution to this problem except huge deposits. The financial crisis in 2008 exposed these systemic risks very well. Ultimately a government bailout, funded by taxpayer dollars, was needed to reduce this risk.
Here’s the problem in a nutshell. Let’s say you enter a trade or an agreement with a counterpart, and they promise to hold their end of the deal. However, what if they can’t? That exposes you to counterparty risk. If they can’t hold their end you lose money, and that might jeopardize your capital standing.
Now, what if you’ve entered another trade with someone else. The fact that your initial trade didn’t work out means you can’t hold your end of the deal with someone else. Like a chain reaction, this phenomenon spreads throughout the system, and soon everyone’s broke. That is what happened during the Great Depression in the 1930s. Banks ran out of money because there was too much risk concentrated in a few institutions.
When one failed, so did another, and this produced a chain reaction that ran out of control. Bailing out the likes of AIG was a sound move during the 2008 crisis since that company was a counterparty to pretty much every other bank on Wall Street, especially Goldman Sachs, and Wall Street, in turn, was a counterparty to many smaller institutions.
The reason counterparty risk exists is because settlement takes a while. When initiating the deal, the counterparty might be in a sound financial position. However, while the transactions are settling, what if they undergo adverse conditions and end up defaulting? The only solution is close to instant settlement, and blockchain offers this solution. Typically, it takes 10 minutes for a transaction to settle on a blockchain network. It’s much quicker than equivalent networks, as you’ve already seen. Simply put, there are only benefits here.
The blockchain doesn’t solve issues where contractual liabilities are determined down the road. For instance, let’s say you draw an insurance contract with a large company for your home. The insurer promises to compensate you when your home encounters damage from a fire. This fire can occur today or 10 years from now; no one knows.
In the interim, that insurer might go bust or might be unable to fulfill their contractual obligations. Thus, the blockchain doesn’t eliminate counterparty and systemic risk completely. However, it goes a long way towards doing so. One solution is to record every subsequent transaction after the initial contract on the blockchain.
For instance, you could be able to view every subsequent transaction the insurer makes once they’ve signed with you. If they end up insuring a ton of properties situated in a flood plain for flood insurance, you’ll clearly know that your counterparty risk is increasing since they’re taking too many risks.
However, this solution doesn’t work in the financial markets. Typically, order books are sealed, and for good reason. Asking companies to open their trade flow to competitors is nonsensical since it erodes competitive advantage. It’s like asking every company to reveal their pricing models or their payments to suppliers. Building a free and competitive market is impossible under such circumstances.
Despite all of these limitations, the blockchain provides a clear upgrade over the current centralized system.
Asset Innovation
High finance often involves the creation of derivatives and other instruments that allow institutions to profit off the moves of any conceivable asset. For instance, it isn’t unusual to find Wall Street banks creating derivatives that pay traders out depending on the weather on a certain day. These instruments are often pushed as examples of innovation in the financial industry.
While the value addition of such contracts is debatable, there’s no doubt that they are in demand. Centralized systems complicate trading such derivatives and create imbalances in the system since a central party typically sets the price. Unlike stock markets where instruments are regularly traded, these derivatives are opaque, and the market is termed Over the Counter or OTC.
OTC markets typically have a handful of participants and prices are determined by negotiation. When creating these derivatives, Wall Street banks usually assume the other side of the trade from their investors and clients. That means they get prices according to their wishes, and the client has no recourse since the market is opaque.
This situation was brought to light during the 2008 crisis. Wall Street banks were in the middle, creating derivatives that rose with house prices and other derivatives that worked like insurance contracts on the first group of derivatives. In essence, derivative set two moved in the opposite direction from derivative set one.
As housing prices began to fall, the value of derivative set two should have risen. However, this didn’t happen. While the prices of derivative set one fell, the prices of the second set remained firmly in place. That didn’t make any sense since the second set was an insurance contract on the first. Wall Street sold fire insurance on a building and was refused to pay out when the building caught fire.
The reason was that Wall Street was busy offloading the first set of derivatives off its books. If it began cashing insurance contracts on that set, they wouldn’t be able to make money selling them. Returning to our example, they owned the house and were busy trying to sell that house. If they paid out insurance, they’d be admitting the house was on fire, and no one would buy it.
Once they sold the house, they began paying out the insurance contracts to investors and even bought these contracts for themselves. The whole situation was fraught with conflicts of interest. It shouldn’t surprise you that despite throwing clients under the bus and carrying out fraud like this, not a single Wall Street banker or trader went to jail.
Blockchain completely eliminates such conflicts of interest while pushing financial innovation. Thanks to smart contracts that can be endlessly customized, any type of asset can be monetized. For instance, you can monetize your data and trade it with someone else. If Google wants to buy your data or lease it, you can create a smart contract and secure it using Ethereum. Google will pay you a fixed amount per month for leasing rights, and the transaction is only yours and Google’s business.
Returning to OTC markets, there won’t be any need for a Wall Street bank to play the role of broker and counterparty at once. The price of the asset will be transparent and can be negotiated between parties. There won’t be a central party arbitrarily fixing prices and manipulating the market.
The result is tremendous innovation in high finance. By creating more instruments, banks and investors stand to benefit while eliminating middleman fees. Decentralization doesn’t eliminate all risks. For instance, a Wall Street bank could simply refuse to negotiate and quote ridiculous prices. However, the probability of such an action bearing fruit is significantly reduced. Thanks to greater transparency, the bank won’t be able to sustain such behavior and will have to eventually play ball.
Security
Look at an ad of any financial institution, and the first thing you’ll notice is the mention of security. Security of your money, your identity, and so on. However, the reality is much different. Most banks’ technological systems are teetering towers of rubbish, ready to pile over at any moment.
As technology became better, banks chose to patch new updates onto legacy systems. As a result, their systems are a mish-mash of old and new, gaining the worst of both worlds. Speak to any IT professional working in a big bank, and they’ll tell you about the number of redundant workflows that underlie banking systems and of multiple applications doing the same thing.
All of this puts a strain on security. Cybersecurity works best when it’s nimble. Cohesive systems are easier to protect than disparate ones with holes everywhere. Instead of fixing the problem, banks merely layer another coat of security, thinking it’ll prevent crises. However, this merely masks the symptoms and doesn’t treat the disease.
The best defense against evolving threats is, counterintuitively, open-source technology. Open-source applications rely on a large community of developers to create better versions. For instance, the Linux operating system is tough for casual users to understand, but professionals swear by it. It’s an open-source system with a large community behind it, and no huge corporations are propping it up.
This community-driven focus exists in the blockchain as well. All large decentralized networks are open source, including Bitcoin and Ethereum. As a result, innovation is guaranteed. Open source technology doesn’t apply to every circumstance. For instance, it’s unthinkable that a government organization like the CIA will open its data to the public. That will be detrimental to national security.
However, the blockchain’s open-source roots secure most of the networks out there. Besides, sensitive data can be siloed away from prying eyes. For instance, a bank’s customer data can be masked on a public blockchain that can be open source. The result is an innovation that can be migrated to a private blockchain that has original customer data. Solutions exist in greater numbers on open-source blockchains than private centralized systems.
As you’ve probably figured out by now, all of these pillars have huge applicability in a variety of functions in finance and government. Applications that are decentralized can redesign our societies. It’s merely a matter of time before the world notices these opportunities and acts upon them.
Financial Functions Ripe for Disruption
The financial sector is a primary target for disruption. Fintech companies have long aimed at big banks and have begun encroaching upon lines of business that have traditionally been the stronghold of big banks. Businesses such as online lending, digital wallets, and banking platforms have disrupted big banks in a major way. However, blockchain and decentralized finance will probably eradicate the era of the big bank as we know it.
Here are the functions that are ripe for disruption. Note that big banks can adopt the blockchain and integrate these re-envisioned functions into their workflows. It’s just that the bank itself won’t exist the way we currently think of it.
Investment and Credit Ratings
Value and identity authentication are two pillars of the financial industry. Credit ratings allow investors to figure out what the value of an investment instrument ought to be. For instance, all bonds are rated on a scale by the rating agencies, Standard & Poor’s and Moodys. It should come as no surprise that both of these agencies are corrupt, and their services are routinely bought by Wall Street banks.
The rating agencies’ behavior during the 2008 crisis isn’t a new phenomenon. The crisis merely brought it to the surface. Needless to say, none of the rating agencies’ executives faced prosecution for their role in the crisis. These agencies are often viewed as unwieldy behemoths, and they aren’t attractive employers for the best and brightest.
Verification is at the heart of what these agencies do. They verify the investment worthiness of an instrument and publish a grade. Currently, there are no alternatives to these services, but a decentralized network will eliminate the need for these middlemen.
An open network where an asset’s creation is recorded indelibly and whose history is open for everyone to view will not need a credit rating. A Wall Street salesperson cannot lie to a client about an instrument’s investment characteristics. Instead, the client can verify the instrument by themselves, eliminating the need for a salesperson.
Consumer credit analytics are also ripe for disruption by the same logic. Credit card agencies and bank card issuers rely on algorithms that help them understand an applicant’s creditworthiness. These algorithms are also applied when a customer applies for a mortgage or a loan. A bank uses tired old front and back ratios while fintech firms use algorithms that use data from sources as diverse as social media accounts.
Predicting creditworthiness is always a gamble. However, an open blockchain network democratizes loan processes. Currently, companies such as Prosper allow people to lend money to those in need, with Prosper taking a justifiable commission for their services. Online lending is one of those rare areas where the middleman takes a justifiable cut. However, these platforms will become even more powerful on the blockchain, where data can be easily verified.
The need for identity will be sidelined since a consumer’s transaction history will be up for open display. How often did the applicant apply for a loan, and what was their payment progress? How far behind are they on their car payments? All of these transactions will be indelibly recorded and, there’s no way an applicant can fake a credit history or a payment.
The result is greater trust without the need to establish identity. A side-effect is a consumer’s personal data, aside from their spending, is protected. Who you are won’t matter. Instead, your actions will carry greater weight and thus, remove any room for manipulation.
Money Transfer
That is an obvious function that is ripe for disruption. I’ve detailed the tedious process that underpins all money transfers. Cryptocurrencies eliminate all of those hassles. Perhaps the best part of a decentralized money transfer network is that even the smallest amounts of money can be transferred without having the amount eaten up by fees and commissions.
Note that this function works well only if the decentralized network remains open-sourced. A closed network, such as Western Union setting up their own blockchain, doesn’t benefit anyone. The company can levy the same fees for doing less work and eradicate value.
Value Storage
This potential disruption is the largest and perhaps the most difficult to understand. If I were to tell you that your bank account will become obsolete, you’d likely find it tough to understand how. Bank accounts have been ingrained into our existence since the dawn of money, and it’s tough to think of a world where they don’t exist. Well, decentralized finance can wipe them out.
Let’s begin with the basics. Every cryptocurrency acts as a store of value. Currently, the prices of Ethereum and Bitcoin might convince you that this isn’t the case. After all, they fluctuate more than 10% every day. A lot of this volatility is driven by speculation. The truth is the real value of these coins is worth something close to $1. After all, if they’re going to be used as daily exchanges of value, it stands to reason that their current values are unjustified.
Assuming that widespread adoption does happen, the value of these coins will plummet to reflect what they actually are: currencies. The British Pound, for instance, isn’t worth $1,000. It’s close to $1, and this is what enables cross-border trade. The blockchain and your wallet act as an automatic store of value. It secures your coins, and you don’t need to store them elsewhere. As long as you remember your private key, it doesn’t matter what method you use to connect to the blockchain. No one can steal your money.
Thus, there’s no need for a bank account. Now let’s take this a step further. What if you sign up to a blockchain network that lends existing coins to other users on the network and funds their loans. You earn interest on those loans, replicating what a savings account does. Why do you need to open a savings account now?
Furthermore, you can use your coins to pay for everyday services. Why do you need a debit card from a bank? In one swoop, checking, savings, and credit cards have been disrupted out of existence. The impact of value stored on the blockchain is more far-reaching than bank accounts.
Currently, to buy a stock or a bond, you have to contact a broker, pay a fee (commission-free trades aren’t free. They’re exposed to bigger funds who make money off you), and buy ownership of the instrument. What if the company that issues stocks or bonds can issue them on the blockchain instead? You don’t need a broker anymore. What if complex derivatives can be issued on a blockchain? Investment banks don’t need to exist, and the world will be a much better place for it.
Market regulation will also change. Currently, entities like the SEC face a tough task keeping up with the opaque nature of financial products. They rely on a whistleblower system to investigate possible issues. Even then, crimes occur. A decentralized network makes everything transparent and changes regulatory stances from reactive to proactive.
Note that all of this occurs without changing the level of innovation in the market. Consumer debt can still be repackaged and traded. Derivatives can be created through smart contracts that govern themselves. Stocks and bonds can be issued on blockchains. Nothing changes for the consumer. It’s just the complex and opaque middleman system that we call the banking system that suffers. Given the lack of value these industries add to the world, this is a very good thing.
Funding options will increase in number. Microfinance has proven to be a force for good in less developed countries. However, these programs are tough to implement, thanks to credit risk. The idea is that the lender provides loans in small amounts to less than creditworthy people and diversifies their risk amongst a large pool of applicants. However, thanks to identity-linked credit structures, it’s impossible to fully understand risk exposures.
Decentralized microfinance networks eliminate this concern. Transaction histories, which are the true gauge of creditworthiness, are public, and thus, evaluating credit is simple. Crowdfunding will also benefit, thanks to transparency. A result of this transparency is the dismantling of the tired old bank loan system. Without loans, a bank doesn’t earn interest and thus, doesn’t earn money. In such a situation, it’s hard to envision a way traditional banks can survive.
What about investment banks, though? While deal-making will survive in some form, the future of trade desks and sales operations is in doubt. Investors can transact amongst themselves and figure out creditworthiness, as explained previously. Investment banks play a huge role in storing value by settling trades and providing custodial services. Well, the blockchain removes the need for this process since the network does this automatically.
All of a sudden, the entire capital markets are ripe for disruption. Institutions such as stock exchanges, clearinghouses, brokers, and prime brokers will be rendered obsolete. These middlemen make the markets far more opaque than they need to be. In fact, the existence of a stock exchange makes no sense given the electronification that has consumed our world.
For instance, the foreign exchange market (FX) is the largest in the world, but it doesn’t have a single exchange. Instead, it operates as a chain of electronic networks. Yet, stock markets are still housed in exchanges that were built more than two centuries ago. Why? Why can’t investors trade in an electronic network that minimizes price differences between exchanges?
These price differences enable rent-seeking institutions like high-frequency trading firms to charge a tax on every trade common investors place. Democratizing the markets will eliminate these firms and restore balance to the markets. Whether a person wants to speculate or invest for the long term, an open decentralized network enables and empowers them.
Finally, let’s turn our attention to venture capitalists. There’s no doubt that these people add value to startup companies. However, not all of them manage to add value to the system. Most companies merely invest in a basket of companies and treat their portfolios as a bunch of numbers, using and throwing out startups when they want. Peer-to-peer financing, as explained previously, would remove these vulture-like firms and give entrepreneurs more options to fund their businesses.
Insurance
Risk evaluation and insurance go hand in hand. The blockchain provides insurance companies with obvious benefits. First, traceability ensures that claims can be easily tracked. More importantly, assets can be secured easily on the blockchain thanks to smart contracts.
The insurance industry is a deep one and, the field of reinsurance is perhaps the riskiest there is. Reinsurance companies insure the insurers. As such, these companies deal with long odds and huge payouts. Verifying claims and securing assets is of the utmost importance to them. Typically, reinsurers underwrite policies on complex derivative contracts.
If these contracts were housed on the blockchain, verifying their components and transaction history is simple. Thanks to the lack of meddling from rating agencies, reinsurers could price risk easily and propose policies that make sense for all parties involved.
Blockchain and decentralized finance also simplify risk management considerably. When opaque derivatives are traded, figuring out the amount of money at risk is tough. For instance, the price of a thinly traded derivatives contract might be $10 today or $1 million tomorrow. The market has few participants, and the price is whatever they’re willing to pay.
Figuring out the value at risk in these scenarios is tough. The blockchain doesn’t reduce the complexity that these contracts carry. However, it certainly removes opacity to a great extent. Currently, trading desks use the historical volatility of a derivative’s constituent instruments to figure out the value at risk. However, if a derivative’s components are obscured, calculating risk is tough.
By making the components transparent, desks can figure out their value at risk easily and price their derivatives better. As a result, insurance companies need to absorb less risk on their books and can rest assured that market valuations are closer to reality.
Reporting and Audits
The financial sector lives and dies by its audit policies. Financial companies carry complicated instruments on their books, and measuring their value at risk at all times is crucial. Companies that misprice their assets and fail to account for risk usually go under. Internal and external audit teams help them stay on top of these complex assets.
Carrying out an audit is a tough task. That is because the technological systems a big bank relies on are usually a patchwork of legacy and modern systems. One module doesn’t talk to the other and, the result is a mish-mash of systems and prices. An auditor will typically comb through everything from analytics platforms to manually filled excel files to figure out the paper trail on an instrument.
The blockchain simplifies this process immensely. First, the network creates its personal audit trail automatically. Even if the local node loses records, the rest of the network can restore audit history immediately. The network itself is incorruptible, which means records cannot be manipulated or interfered with.
How often have you heard of rogue traders losing billions and hiding them in hidden sub-accounts auditors missed? Well, that’s impossible on the blockchain since everything is transparent. The result is a quicker audit and better risk responses. External audits are also simplified since they don’t need to worry about records being obscured.
From a regulatory standpoint, the blockchain makes it impossible for auditors to get buried under paper. Usually, when a company gets investigated for accounting fraud, the typical response is to send a ton of paper and records to the investigators, which results in them not knowing where to begin. The blockchain eliminates this nefarious practice completely.
The capital markets will benefit immensely from blockchain adoption for record storage. Shareholders can rest assured that the companies they’ve invested in aren’t black boxes and that all assets are being accounted for correctly. The result is more trust in the financial system and a safer market for everyone to participate in.
Challenges
The blockchain is at a similar stage right now as the internet was in the early to mid-1990s. The open-source excitement is slowly being taken over by corporate interests. The Amazons and the Googles of blockchain are coming, and it’s interesting to see to what extent the aims of the blockchain will be corrupted.
First off, blockchain and decentralized finance are taking on a sizable adversary in the modern financial system. The big banks have dominated the world for so long that it’s unthinkable for them to give up control. One of the ways in which the banks have reasserted control is to simply buy the technology.
For instance, the challenge from fintech upstarts has been met with a wave of capital funding that integrates fintech processes with traditional bank products. That has led to a wave of digital transformation in banks and financial services. The rise of the digital bank has only empowered traditional banks since these are merely online versions of the same services physical banks provide.
Similarly, banks have begun experimenting with the blockchain for their internal processes. In typical big bank fashion, they’ve begun extracting value but providing none. Given the complexity of the trade settlement process, it was natural that the blockchain would be implemented in this area first by big banks. In 2015, Goldman Sachs filed a patent for a decentralized network powered by a token called SETLCoin that could be used to guarantee same-day trade settlement.
While the technology is impressive and, it certainly makes sense for Goldman’s internal workflows, the irony of a Wall Street firm patenting an essentially open-source process, is not lost on blockchain enthusiasts. The corporatization of the blockchain has begun, and it poses interesting questions for decentralization.
Take Blythe Masters, for instance. Masters is a towering figure on Wall Street, one of the few women to break its glass ceilings. Masters built her reputation by building JP Morgan’s credit derivatives business from the ground up. In 2012, Masters quit her position at the bank and became the CEO of a small company named Digital Asset Holdings, which aims to provide decentralized network applications to financial institutions.
Masters’ idea of a decentralized app is anything but decentralized. Her vision, as she has repeatedly described it, is of a permission-based network that provides access to regulators and market participants. A select few will be granted access to market data, and these participants can use that data for their business purposes. For instance, a big bank can access this network to figure out the settlement analytics of their trades and so on. The value proposition is instant trade settlement instead of the usual T+3 settlement.
This network that Masters envisions is endemic to Wall Street veterans who cannot see anything beyond their visions of siloed data. Accessibility is meant to be gated, not democratized. Convenience is meant for those with money, not for the underserved. Take Circle Internet Finance (Circle), for instance.
This company promises to reinvent the way money is stored and sent around the world. Its network is built on the blockchain, which allows for instant settlement and low fees. There are no middlemen. A business can sign up for its service and instantly pay and receive money from around the world, in any currency, without the need for a bank account. Withdrawing money from the network to a physical bank account is currently necessary; however, Circle plans on disrupting the bank by helping entrepreneurs use the blockchain as a store of value, as I previously described. Sounds good so far, doesn’t it?
Circle was founded by Jeremy Allaire and Sean Neville, veterans of Silicon Valley. If Wall Street is obsessed with the privacy of its transactions and gating access, Silicon Valley is obsessed with commoditizing data and leveraging it to earn money. Allaire’s description of Circle’s business adheres to this stereotype.
Circle’s services are free for businesses to use. However, as with any technological company, freedom comes at a price. The data that Circle collects will be leveraged to pitch even more products to the user, and that’s where Circle will make money. In the future, thanks to the rise of open banking protocols, Circle will undoubtedly share data across third-party members on its network to make money. For instance, Circle could partner with a bank and sell them your data. The bank can then tailor credit cards and loan offerings to you while on Circle’s network.
The aims of the blockchain are completely corrupted by companies such as these. There’s no question of Circle’s network being made open. It is a permission-based network, and somehow, its founders have managed to turn their blockchain-based product into a data mine. The very thing that the blockchain is meant to overcome is Circle’s primary aim.
People like Masters, Allaire, and Neville are quintessential examples of executives championing a centralized system. Having profited from them, there’s no way they can envision giving it up, even if it means corrupting the technology that is meant to replace dinosaurs like them.
That doesn’t mean that blockchain is doomed. There are no small number of decentralized apps in development that don’t rely on such puny visions. The Ethereum blockchain is the most active when it comes to app development. Currently, the blockchain is being used to store land registry records and other financial data. Users can participate in cryptocurrency networks that pay dividends, much like a savings account will pay the interest every year.
Regulation is always an issue with decentralized networks. While the individuals previously mentioned show how decentralization can be absorbed by a centralized network, to its detriment, complete decentralization is also a bad thing. Bitcoin’s early days proved how decentralized networks could be corrupted by nefarious actors. Some degree of regulation is a good thing.
Finding the balance between decentralization and regulation is a major challenge. There’s no doubt that the likes of Wall Street and Silicon Valley will corrupt the idea of decentralization and create private networks that reduce access. However, the majority of the blockchain is open source and, this holds a lot of promise for the average person.
It remains to be seen how financial services evolve. Currently, visions like the one Masters has is the same pattern of patching new tech onto old systems. How will real innovation emerge in this sector? No one has the roadmap as of yet, but the future is exciting nonetheless.

Chapter 4:
Changing Operational Architecture
Modern companies operate in a top-down, hierarchical structure. These operational models have existed since the industrial revolution. When the age of the internet first dawned there was much talk about how organizations would move from a top-down to a flat structure. However, examine any company these days, and it’s pretty obvious that these flat models haven’t worked out.
Business headlines are dominated by CEOs and founders that run their companies on a day-to-day basis. For instance, Jeff Bezos might have stepped aside at Amazon, but he left a CEO in charge. Mark Zuckerberg undoubtedly runs Facebook, and Elon Musk runs Tesla. Google was the only corporation to experiment with a flat structure.
In its early days, Google created an organization where employees were hired for their thinking ability, not skills, and these employees could choose what they wished to work on. Famously 20% of their time was meant to be allocated towards passion projects that didn’t need to be commercially viable to get approved.
It led to a raft of interesting and quirky applications. Many of which got canned once Google morphed into a corporation. The commercially viable ones were the only applications that survived, and this is why we still have Gmail, Docs, Maps, Drive, Calendar, Translate, and Analytics.
Google’s journey is instructive because it highlights everything that goes wrong when a company tries to expand within a centralized universe while trying to maintain a decentralized structure. Put simply, it’s impossible to hang onto a decentralized ethos. The internet isn’t decentralized in the way it’s structured. Google was the first company to migrate every application and task to the internet.
For instance, Android is an operating system that works entirely on the web. Cloud storage and other apps we use daily work on the internet. The era of storing files locally on your desktop is long gone. As such, it’s easy to think that the internet can be decentralized enough for corporations to base themselves along flat structures.
However, the lack of security on the internet means that centralization is what ultimately wins. Hence, top-down structures work in the long run, and eventually, a company’s policies come to reflect a centralized hierarchy. Its practices change, and soon, it’s no different from the robber baron companies of the past. Google’s quiet deletion of its “Don’t be Evil” policy from its company charter in 2015 is an example of how a company founded with the right intention eventually gets corrupted within centralized frameworks.
Monopolies
That isn’t to say a centralized company is bad. When a company is small, it needs quick decisions, and a single person in charge provides this. However, once it turns big, a centralized structure places too much power in the hands of a single person. CEOs are still paid disproportionately large amounts of money compared to the value they bring to the organization. Is a CEO truly 100 times more important to a company than its average worker?
Centralized companies also stifle innovation. True innovation occurs at the edges of a company’s business. For instance, Twitter emerged as an alternative social media messaging platform. It didn’t emerge from Facebook. Apple’s transformation from a computer manufacturer to a product manufacturer occurred on the edges of Microsoft’s business model. Facebook emerged from a desire to communicate and stay in touch with people better, not from Yahoo or Myspace’s vision of communication.
Centralized structures create distance between a company’s executive branch and the edges of its business. The average CEO has no idea of the disruption their company could face because they’re far too immersed in the bureaucracy they have to govern. Not a single financial sector CEO saw the rise of fintech companies and had to resort to buying out the competition to avoid obsolescence.
Such centralized structures also push capitalism towards its worst tendencies. It leads to greater inequality since one person (or a small group) has a greater say in the company’s future than the mass of workers. For instance, the opinion of a single trader on the floor of a financial company carries no weight compared to the head of the trading division in the same company.
A few companies come to control the majority of power, and these days, data and society are worse off for it. Strangely, extreme centralization pushes capitalism close to a communist societal structure. In place of a central committee, we can quote the names of a few corporations that leave everyone else powerless. Regulation is technically supposed to stop this from happening, but regulators cannot deal with the pace at which innovation is occurring.
There’s a reason antitrust laws exist. Monopolies are only the result of extreme centralization, and this kills all competition. Interestingly, a few Silicon Valley investors espouse the idea of a creative monopoly. Peter Thiel, the founder of Paypal, Palantir Technologies, and initial investor in Facebook, is one such example.
Flawed Examples
In his book Zero to One, Thiel argues that creative monopolies allow societies to escape the destruction that competition causes (Thiel & Masters, 2015). It’s an interesting argument, except that he provides no evidence other than his opinion. There’s also the fact that expecting a monopolistic organization to be creative is wishful thinking at best. Absolute power corrupts, and it’s hard to see how creativity and freedom of thought can prosper in a monopolistic environment.
Historically, we have enough evidence in business and political spheres of how monopolies work to arrive at this conclusion. In the business world, the history of John Rockefeller’s Standard Oil provides enough evidence that monopolistic creativity often takes the shape of bullying and rough-handed tactics. Value isn’t created as much as destroyed.
In political spheres, the appropriate term for a monopoly is imperialism. A brief examination of the fortunes of formerly colonized countries is instructive in how much “creativity” was encouraged amongst subjugated people. India is a good example. Despite being under British rule for over 100 years as of the year 1900, the country was still one of the wealthiest economies in the world in terms of natural resources (Ganguli, 1965). By 1947, when India gained independence from the British, India had been partitioned into three countries, namely modern-day India, Pakistan, and Bangladesh, and collectively these countries were amongst the poorest in the world.
The only “creativity” the British left behind were the railways, legal system, and postal services. While these establishments continue to flourish in India, it’s highly unrealistic to assume the British established them to assist the common Indian. The internet these days has become a battleground for neo-colonialism. Corporations duke it out instead of empires.
Google has colonized our phones thanks to its Android system and collects all kinds of data by default. Facebook sought to combat this by providing internet access in less developed countries via its platform. Called Free Basics, the company essentially colonizes the internet by confining users to its platform and restricting access to every other app.
These moves violate net neutrality laws, and interestingly enough, India has the strongest set of laws in the world in this regard. Free Basics was summarily rejected by the Indian Government, and the plan was called out for its colonialistic aims. In response, noted venture capitalist Marc Andressen of Andressen Horowitz ranted that India was much better off under colonialism, an argument that is eerily similar to the thoughts that Thiel echoes in his book.
What the thousands of Indian-origin employees of Silicon Valley companies (a group that includes the CEOs of almost every major tech company) thought of this rant is unknown. However, Andressen clearly didn’t care about the pushback from these people since his position in the centralized committee is secure for life.
The point of this section is that centralization harms companies and innovation as much as it does the average person. Trust in companies is at an all-time low these days and we tend to think of them as faceless automatons. The fact is that a company is bound by the system it operates on. There’s not much a CEO can do even if they wanted to.
How can Google or Amazon adopt a flat structure when its competition will likely devour it for doing so? It’s easy to criticize these companies, and there’s no doubt they deserve the criticism; however, they’re products of the platforms they’re built on.
Reinventing the company requires a chance in the foundation on which creativity is built. This is what is currently taking place on the Ethereum blockchain.
The Promise of the Blockchain
On July 30, 2015, the first or genesis block of Ethereum’s blockchain was created. Following this, miners rushed to create even more blocks and earn Ether, the token of the Ethereum network, as a reward. At first, Ethereum was positioned as an alternative cryptocurrency to Bitcoin. However, this is far from the case.
Bitcoin was created with the express purpose of functioning as a means of transaction. Ethereum’s purpose is much larger. While Bitcoin talks about upending the financial system, Ethereum gives developers the ability to do just that. It came about thanks to a dispute between Ethereum’s founder Vitalik Buterin and Bitcoin’s core development team.
Buterin argued that the Bitcoin network should provide developers with more tools to create decentralized apps rather than cater to a single currency. In the absence of agreement, Buterin created his own blockchain network, and these days, it’s a hotbed of decentralized apps.
The first company to base itself entirely on the blockchain was Consensys. Consensys describes itself as a “blockchain venture production studio”(Consensys, 2021). It houses many projects, from decentralized financial applications to social media platforms. For instance, a popular project currently underway is a social media network like Reddit but decentralized on the blockchain.
This project took off due to the increasingly centralized manner in which Reddit’s forums are governed, including the majority Chinese control in the network’s parent company. Here are some of the projects Consensys is building, along with notes on how the blockchain has influenced the way it’s organized.
Document and Contract Management
Along the lines of value storage as described in the previous chapter, Consensys hosts an app that allows individuals and companies to store documents and other forms of value by securing them to smart contracts. Smart contracts are lines of code customized to enforce sets of regulations as agreed upon by all parties involved. There is literally no limit to the conditions that anyone can code into such contracts.
The technology for smart contracts existed previously but, there was no network on which they could be deployed. In essence, they were all dressed up without anywhere to go. The arrival of the blockchain, specifically Ethereum, changed all that. The most powerful aspect of smart contracts is that they can be enforced and have value addition terms attached to them seamlessly.
For instance, if a supplier agrees to deliver a certain amount of raw material in exchange for money, a contract is drawn that governs this transaction. In a centralized structure, the material is delivered, multiple papers are filed, and payment is released after a certain period, usually 30 days or more. That leaves the supplier at the customer’s mercy. In many cases, unscrupulous action on the customer’s part leaves the vendor with a cash flow hole on their books.
Managing disputes is tough also, which has prompted a wave of companies touting electronic accounts receivable and payables systems. However, this software is still centralized in that no one has a complete picture as to how approvals are made. A smart contract changes all of that since it can incorporate not just the audit trail that verifies goods supply, but it can also encode payment terms.
If a supplier delivers goods, the contract will instantly release payment on the blockchain network. There won’t be any doubt about the validity of this payment since the transaction will be instantly verified by the rest of the network and by the smart contract’s protocols. Instead of waiting to gather paperwork, stamping it, cutting a paper check, or pressing a “pay now” button, companies can automate everything with complete trust.
Note that the identity of the vendor is irrelevant in this case. All that matters is whether the transaction’s terms were fulfilled or not. With the blockchain, this is easy to verify. It should come as no surprise that Consensys uses smart contacts to pay its employees and suppliers on Ethereum’s blockchain. The result is smooth cash flow and complete trust in vendor management systems.
Triple Entry Accounting
Every business runs on numbers and uses financial statements to make sense of their business’ profitability. Double-entry accounting is the most robust system of accounting designed until now, and it holds the entire centralized world of business together. It’s a simple, yet genius premise.
Let’s say a company wishes to purchase something. That is an expense. To record this expense, the company enters two line items, one each in two separate ledgers. They issue a purchase order in the purchase ledger, and once they receive an invoice, this goes into the payments ledger. Both of these records are connected, and thus, they balance each other out. At the end of the day, the sum of all ledgers must be equal. A mismatch indicates something that wasn’t accounted for, and forensic auditors can dive in.
That is where double-entry accounting runs against limitations. It reveals the existence of a mismatch, but it doesn’t tell us where it exists. Forensic auditors have to spend a ton of resources figuring out where discrepancies lie, and more often than not, it’s tough to pinpoint the source of error. Worse, fraud can go undetected in double-entry accounting. Fictitious transactions and false invoices amongst subsidiary companies can create the illusion of activity and hide poor earnings. It’s what Enron, a powerful energy trading company in the early 2000s, did to hide its trail of fraud. Enron eventually folded but not before it cost shareholders and employees billions of dollars in losses.
To combat this problem, triple-entry accounting was proposed as a solution. The idea was that in addition to the two entries, a third entry recording the connection between the first two entries would be stored in a third party, incorruptible ledger. Until the blockchain came along, this solution was impractical. After all, who could say that the third-party ledger would resist corruption? It merely created another point of failure.
However, the blockchain’s incorruptibility means that storing the third record is now simple. More importantly, the third entry makes it simple for an auditor to view the relationship between all entries in a company’s books. Thanks to a unique ID being assigned to a pair of entries, an auditor can explore all the IDs on the blockchain and view the history of transactions connected to it.
That highlights another important feature of the blockchain, which is the search functionality. I’m not talking about searching for information, but searching for resources. Companies expend tremendous costs searching for everything. In turn, shareholders and auditors expend more resources searching for information about a company’s activities. Currently, our world of search is two-dimensional.
For instance, if you want to research a topic, you search for it on Google, and the company gives you a list of articles to choose from. You click on an article and begin reading. If you’re lucky, the article gives you some background information or links to past records of the situation. Most publications don’t do this.
However, Google doesn’t do this by default because it isn’t an arbiter of news and research. It’s a platform and absolves itself of all responsibility regarding truth. Thus, you need to exercise critical thinking when viewing results since no one knows what’s true. It’s no wonder that phrases like “truthiness” and “post-truth” have entered our vocabulary.
The blockchain changes this. Search is now three-dimensional. Currently, you can access a wide range of opinions on a subject (search results from diverse sources) and depth on a particular point of view (a long blog post from a source). However, you can’t access the historical record. Did that publication change its tone of reporting? Did they say the opposite yesterday versus today? Changing tone is as simple as deleting the old record, and Google has no way of recording this and bringing it to our attention.
The blockchain adds the third crucial element without compromising the first two in the picture. It brings history to us in real-time. Since every transaction is recorded forever, it’s easier to verify claims and understand how an issue has evolved. Trust increases in the system thanks to information being automatically verified and recorded.
From a corporation’s standpoint, this means it’s easier to understand issues plaguing a project and figuring out who is the best person for the job. Consensys employees can work on any project of their choosing. There isn’t a central command that assigns work. The company’s founder acts as an advisor, recommending projects on the company’s Slack.
Employees are rated and reviewed by their peers, and their track record is transparently stored on the blockchain. There is no need for a manager to carry out performance reviews or conduct lengthy feedback sessions. There is no need to worry about compensation not being tied to performance since performance is transparent.
Teams running a task can quickly view which person has skills for the task they need executing and can either request another team member to join or post a need for someone outside the firm to work with them. The process sustains itself, and teams work independently from one another while helping the company build products that enhance its reputation.
It’s what Google sought to build but couldn’t execute because the internet doesn’t provide trust the way the blockchain does. The implications of such a system are huge. Everything from evaluating new hires to potential suppliers is now seamless. For instance, when hiring new employees, there’s no need to view the identity of the potential candidate.
Their skills are there for everyone to view, along with a fully verified track record. There’s 100% trust in the system, after all. Thus, unconscious hiring biases exit the system, and the company becomes a meritocracy, which is what capitalism is at its best. Best of all, in all of this search functionality, users can choose to share data and withhold personal details. You won’t have to share your social security number or passport details or religion, or anything else when applying for a job.
Companies won’t need this information when hiring either. Everyone gets to retain control of their wealth (data) and still build a productive organization.
Royalty and Asset Management
The first smart contracts were executed between two parties. They were simple creations: party A promised X, and party B provided Y in return. Here’s how a sample translation would have unfolded. A would pay B some Bitcoin into an escrow account. B would provide work. If A was satisfied, they would release the rest of the Bitcoin.
It doesn’t take a genius to see the flaws in this system. How can A trust B or vice-versa? What if A doesn’t transfer payment once B completes work? To counter this lack of trust something powerful was needed, and this is where multiple signature or multi-sig contracts enter the picture. The premise is simple: every contract has three signatures or keys attached to it.
The primary parties in the transaction will need two keys to access funds connected to the deal. A third party would have a key (one of the two keys needed to access funds) and acts as an escrow and impartial arbiter in case of a dispute. With three keys securing a contract, everyone is assured of a satisfactory outcome, and trust is established in the system.
The evolution of multi-sig contracts has significant implications for artists who depend on royalties and companies that hold complex deals and assets on their books. Music artists face many challenges in getting paid and, copyright laws have more loopholes in them as they do protections. Typically, content platforms absolve themselves of all responsibility and don’t protect or compensate artists for theft.
Smart contracts enforced with multi-sig are the best way to build trust in the system and ensure a fair outcome. Every user’s action is recorded, and the contract enforces itself. For instance, if the second party uses media in a manner that the contract prohibits, payment is automatically withheld. If the party contests it, the arbiter can view transaction (usage) history and reach a conclusion easily.
Moving to large corporations, litigation and liability lawsuits often center around who did what. However, judgments in court are made based on how people feel. Lawyers who appeal to a jury’s emotions tend to win more cases than those who appeal to the facts. It’s why countries around the world have different judicial systems. Germany, for instance, actively seeks to minimize the impact emotions have on a jury, and lawyers have to adhere to a strict argument framework. That isn’t the case in the United States, as you might know.
The legal system has safeguards against this in the form of the appeal system, but it doesn’t address the core problem. Thanks to the blockchain’s tamper-proof historical record, juries and lawyers won’t have much emotional wiggle room, and facts will prevail. What someone did will be more important than what they say they did.
Another issue that hampers centralized firms is internal transaction costs. The meme of the idiot boss is well entrenched into our society. Centralized structures often end up promoting people who manage to curry favor with higher-ups than those who truly innovate and find ways to create better products and services. If this weren’t the case, comics like Dilbert wouldn’t exist.
Blockchain makes the creation of a flat organization a reality. That means middle management doesn’t exist anymore, and the inherent inefficiency of poor decisions is eliminated. As a result, companies can create better products (assets) faster and more quickly. By eliminating insider transaction costs and increasing go-to-market times, companies can rely on better revenue streams and higher profits. There is literally no downside to these outcomes.
Re-Establishing Trust
As corporations come to terms with the fact that their potential consumers are losing trust in them, another significant cost is rearing its head. Trust once lost is tough to regain. Wall Street is a good example of how this makes life tough for businesses. Wall Street doesn’t exist in the same form as it was before 2008. Before the economic crisis, every bright graduate of prestigious schools sought jobs in the financial sector.
Thanks to the industry’s despicable actions and even worse aftermath, most graduates preferred to depart to Silicon Valley instead, which still held some modicum of ethics back then. These days, news about junior bankers not wanting to work long hours and calling for a reform of the way they’re compensated is normal.
Goldman Sachs has faced the highest amount of reprisals from its junior employees. The pandemic forced junior bankers to work from home, pretty much around the clock, in isolation. In response, Goldman promised to increase the amount of money they paid their junior staff and promised to give them Saturdays off. While this doesn’t sound like much of a deal, it seemed to appease their employees.
However, what of the damage to Goldman’s image. This firm is one of the least trusted in the world, and such news does little to improve its reputation. Goldman currently runs a consumer-facing bank named Marcus, which provides savings accounts to everyday consumers. How will that business fare, given the sort of negative publicity the firm routinely attracts?
Re-establishing trust for such organizations is a tough exercise. Usually, a PR blitz follows damaging news releases, but corporations have been using it for so long that people are desensitized to them. Perhaps the blockchain offers these companies some form of redemption. The blockchain doesn’t just decentralize a network; it forces integrity into a system.
Everything that someone does is verifiable, and facts can be double-checked. No one can go around making things up by stating that “everyone says so.” Sure, there will be people and entities like the ones highlighted at the end of the previous chapter who corrupt this premise. However, in the larger view, the blockchain helps companies avoid the cost of losing consumer trust.
Thanks to transparency enforced by default, trust is always maintained, and there isn’t any need to re-establish it. As a result, the costs of re-establishing trust are also eliminated, along with internal transaction costs. Companies can theoretically focus on building better products and establish better relationships with their vendors and customers.
Of course, a lot depends on how individual corporations choose to pursue the promise of the blockchain. People such as the ones highlighted in the previous chapter have already demonstrated how myopic visions can be. However, given how Consensys has gone about building its organization, there’s no doubt that we can expect great progress in this area.
The biggest challenge to reform is the thought process that corporate leaders use. For instance, Consensys was built as a blockchain company from the ground up. Expecting a tired old institution like Goldman Sachs to give up its century-old centralized business model and pivot to a new world overnight is unrealistic.
What doesn’t help is that the people who have the resources and access to technology to make an impact on the blockchain come from these centralized industries. Changes always come from the fringes, though. The great companies of tomorrow will be spearheaded by people who are unknown today.
As such, the potential the blockchain has to rethink the structure of a traditional corporation is huge. It’s up to corporate leaders and entrepreneurs to seize this chance.

Chapter 5:
DeFi and Business Models
I’ve ranted quite a bit about platforms and their rent-seeking behavior enough in this book. It’s now time to detail how a decentralized application or DApp would work on the blockchain. I picked on Airbnb before, so let’s stick with them. Currently, Airbnb takes a hefty cut of revenue from the value the renter and property owner provides.
In return, the only value that this platform provides is a rating system that helps all parties verify one another’s trustworthiness. Second, it guarantees a good amount of traffic to all parties. Renters can rest assured that they’ll always have enough properties to choose from wherever they go and owners can rest assured that renters will flock to the platform.
However, these benefits exist because there are no other viable options. Let’s look at what Airbnb charges in return for these benefits. Property owners are charged around three percent of the booking price from the money that renters pay. Three percent doesn’t sound like much but let’s put this into perspective. When you buy a property from a realtor, they’ll charge you a four percent commission on the sale.
The realtor does a lot of work. They maintain a list of properties, ensure paperwork goes through, set up the escrow, negotiate on behalf of the buyer or the seller, and communicate necessary information to all parties. They even feed you free cookies and fruits if you visit an open house!
What exactly does Airbnb do that even comes close to this? They’re a paid version of Craigslist’s properties section. In fact, during its growth days, Airbnb built a backdoor API to Craigslist’s property listing section to siphon that website’s traffic. Silicon Valley hails this as an example of growth hacking. To any sensible person in the real world, this is plain theft of another business’ assets.
Renters aren’t charged anything. However, this is because the renter is the product. The renter gives Airbnb valuable data that the platform uses to remarket services and suggest further property listings. The platform builds a user profile filled with personal identification and credit card information vulnerable to hacks. If these data are hacked, Airbnb isn’t liable for any damage it might cause. If Airbnb was to be taken over by Google or Amazon in the future, these companies will know everything about your life. Even better than your family would.
So how might a decentralized Airbnb app work?
DApps
Let’s call our DApp, the dAirbnb. This app rests on the blockchain and is a series of connected smart contracts. For starters, the app would house a review system that allows all users to verify one another’s behavior and track records. Is the owner’s property a good deal? Is the renter a good person to rent to? Everyone can upload pictures of their properties but don’t have to upload pictures of themselves if they don’t want to. I’ll explain why shortly.
The filters that you find on Airbnb regarding wifi, laptop-friendly workspaces, and washing machines will exist. In fact, the interface will look exactly like Airbnb’s. The difference is entirely under the hood. When you communicate on the app, messages and interactions aren’t stored on a database. They’re stored in a blockchain ledger. The messages themselves are cryptographically signed, which makes them impossible to hack.
You and the property owner are the only ones who can ever read those messages. What’s more, you can exchange contact information with one another easily. Airbnb doesn’t allow its users to do this since it leads to both parties signing a deal offline. That hampers the platform’s revenue. However, the DApp isn’t concerned with this since the incentive to interact on it is far greater than offline interaction.
Here are the incentives in no particular order.
Reputation
A decentralized app doesn’t have any single authority in charge. This means the review system is more important than ever. The more reviews a user has, the better their experiences will be. Now let’s take this a step further. What if dAirbnb was just one app that rested on a blockchain that contained several other apps?
There could be dGrocery that allows you to order groceries online, dUber for transportation, dStylist for any personal grooming, and so on. An entire ecosystem of apps that collect reviews from various other users who can attest to your character after having interacted with you. If you’ve never rented on dAirbnb before, landlords can view your reviews from other interactions in the ecosystem.
That is a dystopian nightmare if a centralized system was in charge. However, everything is on the blockchain, and thus, users are in full control of their data and interactions. Reveal what you would like to and withhold everything else. If another user needs sensitive information (as defined by you), you can send it to them securely, knowing that they cannot exploit it. That information isn’t going to be stored on some database where it’s vulnerable to a hack.
Thus, managing and building a reputation on dAirbnb is far easier than choosing to do so offline.
Identity Verification
Talk about the blockchain, and the first objection people have is that no one’s identity can be verified. That isn’t correct as you’ve already learned. Opponents of the blockchain like framing it as a system that allows anyone to assume any identity. However, this isn’t how the blockchain works. Users can verify another person’s ID depending on their needs.
It’s just that identity is removed from the center of attention, and this is a tough pill to swallow. Furthermore, the way blockchain apps verify ID is different from a centralized app. Airbnb, for instance, allows you to view the other person’s name and other information. Generally, this is harmless since people don’t share sensitive information. However, to verify your ID, Airbnb collects information from sensitive documents like passports and IDs.
An ID verification protocol on the blockchain works differently. In essence, it’s a smart contract drawn between two parties. The contract sits in between both parties and verifies that both are who they say they are. Neither party is provided sensitive information or any information beyond what the other person is willing to disclose.
Let’s say you’re booking a room on dAirbnb, and the landlord wants to know whether you are who you say you are. dAirbnb invokes a SmartID protocol, a separate app on the blockchain that verifies your ID securely. Again, there’s no danger of a hack or cyberattack. You share your picture and name with the landlord, and that’s it.
In return, you get to verify whether the landlord has a physical space ready for you and that they aren’t copying someone else’s listing or doing something shady. The result is a system that is far more secure than conventional Airbnb. The decentralized app is completely peer-driven and enhances trust between people despite very little personal information being revealed.
Compare this to an offline transaction where you’ll need to hand over your physical ID document and have someone verify it. The blockchain offers a far safer experience, and this is a powerful incentive.
Risk Management
Risk on the blockchain isn’t managed as much as it’s eliminated. Centralized databases can be leaked, hacked, and sold to the highest bidder. Decentralized apps don’t store information in a single place. Thus, there’s no comparison to safety on the blockchain versus what any other solution offers.
Insurance
Airbnb offers property owners $1 million in insurance protection against damages and theft. It’s one of the few value drivers that the platform provides. A cynic might say it’s just another way of washing their hands of responsibility; however, the presence of insurance cover gives landlords peace of mind.
A decentralized app would provide the same degree of protection but with greater efficiency. dAirbnb would simply invoke another protocol, call it dInsurance, where multiple insurers get to view renter and landlord data and tailor quotes based on the individual.
Thus, good renters will have to pay lower insurance premiums while renters with bad reputations will pay more. Landlords can rest assured that their unique needs are being covered. Note that risk evaluation doesn’t depend on someone’s identity. It solely depends on their transaction history on dAirbnb.
Insurers won’t need ID proofs or any other sensitive data to verify who you are. Relevant information is already present on the network, and all they need to do is access publicly available transaction data. The insurance contract can be sent as another smart contract that involves all parties and is secured by a multi-sig.
Thus, the whole experience is peer-driven, and dAirbnb facilitates this connection. It doesn’t sit in the middle collecting rent for the privilege of offering you a connection to some other party.
Payments
That is one of the biggest advantages of using a dApp. Owners don’t have to wait for lengthy periods to receive money since they get it instantly. Renters often complain of security deposits being withheld for no reason. Well, a smart contract that places deposit money in escrow solves that issue. In fact, there won’t be any need for a deposit in a long-term rental arrangement.
The lease itself will be a smart contract. If the renter breaks terms, the contract automatically compensates the owner for damages. Any data and evidence to back claims up and to record original conditions can be uploaded to the contract, and all parties can verify it.
In case of a dispute, an impartial third party can be given access and the issue can be settled. If the dispute goes to a court of law, there’s no way for either party to modify or manipulate the situation since everything is recorded. The result is a safer and seamless experience for everyone involved.
Access
One of the hurdles of monetizing a property on Airbnb is that guests can rarely check themselves in. IT technology has improved to the point where everyday devices can now run themselves or can be controlled through an app. Smart locks are available but automating their functions is tough. For instance, owners can activate smart locks to let people in, but they have to physically verify whether the guest’s information matches what is on the platform.
The blockchain can automate this entire process thanks to smart contracts. A smart contract’s terms can be linked to a smart lock.
