Once the guest approaches the lock, they can present an electronic key that is encoded into the smart contract. If the key matches, the door unlocks, and the guest checks themselves in.
A single smart contract can govern the entire rental workflow this way. Owners can manage their properties remotely knowing that their properties are secure and that no manipulation is possible. It reduces the impact a property management firm can have on fees and profits that the owner can collect.
Open Network Companies
As the case study of a dAirbnb platform shows, peer-driven networks can change the nature of an organization completely. Instead of assuming a centralized shape, organizations of the future can simply transform into a network of connected apps. The dAirbnb platform you just looked at uses an identity verification app and an insurance app. These entities are separate from each other and don’t have to be owned by the same corporation.
Decentralizing the structure of an organization has other effects too. For starters, the amount of capital needed to compete in the marketplace is reduced considerably. If someone wants to create a platform that has better features than dAirbnb, all they need to do is establish connections to apps in the ecosystem and develop their app to the best of their abilities.
Given the open-source nature of the blockchain, it opens everyone up for competition, and big companies cannot buy their way forward. For example, Google currently buys any company that can even remotely develop a better search algorithm than them. That is to stave off competition. Most owners sell to Google because the prospect of going up against a trillion-dollar behemoth that effectively controls the internet is a losing prospect.
Similar patterns play themselves out in social media networking and technology. For instance, Apple has walled its app store off completely from other networks in the name of security and holds developers captive to its demands. The internet was meant to be an open marketplace, free for everyone to compete, but big corporations have captured their corners of it and will never let go.
The blockchain brings greater efficiency to the market thanks to decentralization. Companies can reduce their internal search costs as previously described, and this leads to the creation of novel business models. Here are some possible ventures that we might see in the future.
Just in Time Sourcing
Just in time or JIT deliveries offer the best combination of efficiency and cost control for manufacturing firms. Setting up JIT sourcing currently costs a lot of resources, and only large corporations can afford them. The idea of JIT is that raw materials arrive just when they’re needed for production. That reduces storage costs and makes freight transportation more efficient.
To make sure this happens, companies have to create large databases of suppliers and delivery tracking systems of their own. That allows them to monitor every action and predict the efficiency of their systems. The irony is that increased control doesn’t give them an advantage as JIT is hard as ever to set up.
The blockchain simplifies JIT immensely by making the entire network of suppliers transparent. What’s more, the track record of every supplier can be instantly verified, and there’s complete trust in the system. Thus, smaller organizations can create better processes and reduce their working capital needs even more. The result is more efficiency, lower costs, and better business practices for everyone.
Autonomous Agents
When talking of companies, we think of corporations that have employees, offices, servers, and so on. What if the company can be fully automated via a smart agent that roams a decentralized network, making deals that benefit the organization, updating itself to account for competition, and incorporating new products to serve customers better?
Smart contracts are more than just lines of code. They can be taught to do literally anything. Currently, technology limits us from automating them beyond a few simple tasks. A lot of automation comes in the form of validation against existing conditions. However, nothing is stopping smart contracts from learning and interacting with a larger ecosystem.
For instance, Amazon’s pivotal business for a while has been its AWS or cloud computing business. The company allows other organizations to rent server space and remove the need for physical server infrastructure. What if AWS was replaced by a smart contract on an open blockchain?
This contract would seek out peer computers in the network and evaluate the amount of free storage space available. The contract would sign another contract with the peer computer and lease storage space. It would then turn around and list that space to users who can rent that server space.
All a human being would need to do is monitor the rules governing the contract. They would need to ensure it doesn’t violate any peer rules and that server needs are correctly met. Thus, companies can become far leaner than they currently are and become a network of dApps and smart contracts interacting with each other.
If you’re catching on, this sounds eerily similar to AI development. The problem with AI development currently is that it’s entirely in the hands of a select few corporations. What if its development were democratized? Surely, the pace of development would be much faster, and the result would be a corporation run by smart contracts, with human handlers making sure the boundaries of that system are preserved adequately.
The nature of a corporation as we know it is to earn profits. However, such open-source corporations aren’t in it to make money. A robot doesn’t need to be paid after all. Service fees can be kept low, and value becomes the primary driver. As a result, the cost of resources decreases.
How will this interact with a capitalist society? Well, it’s too early to tell. However, there’s no doubt that the paradigms that we take for granted will be challenged. Will the increasing development of smart contracts lead to humans not being needed at all? What if one smart contract could monitor another, thus removing the need for a human handler?
What does the function of humanity become in such a society? Again, it’s too early to tell. There are best and worst-case scenarios at play. Whatever happens, there’s no doubt that removing something as powerful as AI from centralized control can only be a good thing for society as a whole.
Art Creation
While the internet did a great deal to bring information to the masses, it never managed to compensate the people who created that information. The downfall of newspapers and publishing houses and the takeover of nonsensical Buzzfeed-like websites is one of the many ways the internet affected media consumption.
What’s more, these media houses still exercised massive control over what was reported. These days, we have social media platforms like Facebook to contend with over and above these clickbait websites. While the blockchain cannot resuscitate long-gone newspapers, it can definitely ensure that the people who create original content are rewarded for their efforts.
Currently, content creators have to use centralized platforms to spread their message. For example, an aspiring journalist has to maintain a blog, a Twitter account, and a Facebook profile to source and report stories. In addition, their stories appear on a website that exercises further control over output. In most cases, content creators lose rights to their work and don’t get compensated for how often it gets shared or consumed.
The blockchain enables creators to digitally sign their art and lease their artwork to other consumers. Non-fungible tokens or NFTs have become hot recently, and speculators have rushed in increasing the prices of everything. While the speculative boom shows how little people understand blockchain technology, the NFT itself is a smart contract that can change the way creators collect royalties.
On a decentralized platform, an artist could create a work of art (or content of any kind) and sign it, thereby verifying their rights to the work. The NFT can be coded to lease the artwork to other users in exchange for a token, and thus, royalty collection is automated. If anyone wishes to share the artwork or display it to the public, the NFT would ensure that the artist is compensated accordingly for it.
Currently, anyone can take a picture of a work of art or download a digital work of art and repost it on a website. Google picks this image and displays it in its image results, without paying the creator a fee or acknowledging that someone else created it. Facebook and Google display millions of pieces of content on their platforms, increase their share of the internet’s traffic, earn ad revenues from advertisers thanks to this traffic, and don’t compensate people who drive traffic in the first place.
If no one posted anything on the internet for a day, what would happen to Google and Facebook’s business model? The blockchain would allow people to share and use the content as they please but would also ensure fair compensation to creators. Google could upload pictures of artwork to its image search results but would have to pay the artist royalties for doing so.
A smart contract tied to an NFT can resolve all scenarios where artwork would be displayed. A display in image search results would require a smaller royalty than one displayed on a website. If the image is being displayed for educational purposes or for purposes that the artist doesn’t wish to collect a royalty from, the smart contract can specify these conditions.
There is no limit to how much this will impact the likes of music creators, artists, and digital content creators. Record companies, walled app stores such as Apple and Google, and centralized platforms will face a reckoning and deservedly so. Their days of rent collection will be numbered.
Aside from helping artists, the blockchain will also eliminate fraud. Currently, the art world is inundated with fakes that cost a ton of resources to verify and discredit. Insurance companies build entire business lines insuring art and verifying authenticity. The blockchain will simplify all of this since the record of a piece of art will be immutably stored on the network.
Verifying its origin and creation will be simple. Even if a single record is lost, its existence is stored on the entire network and can thus be restored easily.
Cooperative Companies
The term sharing has been co-opted by the likes of Uber and Airbnb-like platforms. According to these platforms, they’re participants in a “sharing” economy and, they position themselves as peer-to-peer companies. However, as you’ve already read, these companies don’t offer anything to share. They’re merely aggregators that host a bunch of traffic on their platforms without assuming any responsibility.
The word sharing implies a free exchange of goods and services. Think of kids sharing toys. Uber and Airbnb don’t offer people the chance to share anything. An Uber driver is a taxi operator at the end of the day and has to pay Uber a cut of their earnings for the privilege.
The blockchain can reinvent the so-called sharing economy of today by turning it into an actual peer-driven network. You’ve already read about how such decentralized platforms would work, so I won’t dive into the details again. There’s no doubt that such dApps give users greater control over their data and business models. As such, if the price of this control is the obsolescence of centralized platforms, then it’s worth it.
A side-effect of the blockchain is that it can turn a sharing economy into a metered economy. For instance, when you hire a car these days, it’s tough to hire one for a few hours. You’ll probably be charged for an entire day no matter how little you need the car. The reason is it’s tough to locate renters for the remaining hours of the day and enforce rental terms over short periods.
It also adds significant costs. For instance, the car company will need to inspect the vehicle multiple times, run paperwork just as often, and coordinate collection and pickup. All of this means more employees and processes. The chances of things going wrong are high.
Zipcar made waves when it allowed people to rent cars hourly. However, this platform didn’t really add any value. It merely aggregated people who were willing to rent their cars when the vehicles were lying unused. The blockchain doesn’t have a billion-dollar valuation but delivers far more value than Zipcar claims to do.
By tying rental terms to smart contracts and linking with a decentralized review app, a company could create a system where people can rent their cars when unused easily. Identities will be verified by the network and contract terms will be enforced automatically. Owners will need to physically check their vehicles for damage, of course; that cannot be automated away.
However, this business model reduces costs significantly and improves everyone’s experience. It isn’t just vehicles that can be rented. The seed of Airbnb emerged from the thought that people have a variety of resources that they don’t use all the time. For instance, many people have tools in their garage but rarely use all of them. Most tools are bought with the intention of having them when the time comes.
What if a construction worker needed a certain tool and could rent it from a decentralized tool renting app? People could specify the exact number of hours they would like to rent a tool, and tool owners could enforce rental periods by smart contracts. Powered by a network of trust verification and insurance apps, a peer-to-peer economy where resource usage is metered is now a reality.
The possibilities are endless. We could sublease our Wifi hotspots, storage capacity, even the heat generated by our computers. The only question is how creative can we get when it comes to monetizing our assets?
Reinventing Platforms
I’ve disparaged platforms quite a lot in this book, and they deserve criticism. However, the current state of their businesses isn’t entirely their fault. It costs a lot of money to maintain a platform and ensure security standards are met. That explains the relatively high fees these companies charge.
The blockchain can remove these fees entirely since trust is built into the system. However, this won’t necessarily kill their businesses. They can use the redressal of data usage to their advantage. Let's take a company like Airbnb, for instance. Currently, this platform monitors your usage and collects data about everything you do for free.
What if Airbnb instead allowed you the freedom of sharing specific data and compensated you for it? The company could then use that data and make its platform better. Health tech is another example. Wearable technology allows people to track everything they do, but they also share these data with companies.
The blockchain can redress this imbalance. What if you bought a shoe with a chip in it that transmitted data to the manufacturer? What if these data were on a blockchain, and you could choose which data to share and what to withhold? Furthermore, what if the manufacturer paid you for sharing data?
All of a sudden, we’re talking about personal data monetization. Much like how a bank pays you interest to store your money with them, and you collect rent on real estate, you can now monetize your personal data and collect rent from it. Side gig workers will rejoice at the thought!
Platforms, for their part, can scale down and provide more value to their users. Airbnb won’t need massive security functions and won’t need to worry about storage since an entire network does it for them. Instead, they can use the data they collect (from their data suppliers as raw material) and make their platforms a better place (finished products).
People would be willing to pay for these enhanced experiences, and thus, Airbnb can justify the commissions they collect.
Transparent Supply Chains
The food industry has a massive problem. Every year, billions of food go to waste due to improper storage, faulty sourcing, and just bad luck. The frustrating thing is these companies spend a lot of money strengthening their supply chain. There are many moving parts in a supply chain, and coordinating all of them is tough.
Food that arrives in improper conditions is unsafe and cannot be salvaged. Add to this consumer concerns about sustainability, and you have a system that everyone invests a lot of money into but doesn’t quite deliver. Manufacturers these days stick QR codes onto their products to help consumers view where their food came from.
This system is problematic on many levels. What prevents a manufacturer from cheating the system and creating their own sustainability data? They could have sourced tuna from a farm but might tell the consumer the tuna was caught wild under sustainable circumstances. Consumers have no way of knowing.
What if every piece of raw material in an industry could be tagged with a token (like an NFT) and tracked on a blockchain? The moment a fish is caught from the sea, a token is created, and this token can be broken into sub-tokens. This way, every part of the fish that is processed into other goods can be tracked, and consumers can quickly refer to the source of their food. No fancy QR codes or anything of the sort.
With regards to product tracking, the blockchain will not eliminate costly product tracking infrastructure. However, it will help companies create stronger audit trails that help identify the exact moment of failure. The result is a transparent community that knows where its products come from.
This use case applies not just to food but to every other industry—industrial manufacturing, healthcare, precision electronics, you name it. All of these industries spend billions tracking their products by using massive centralized systems. An open blockchain removes much of these costs and delivers value to the consumer. The blockchain also opens collaboration to a much greater degree.
If a manufacturer wishes to source logistics partners, they can trust that the suppliers of these services on the blockchain are verified. What if their shipments are too small for an entire truckload? Currently, these manufacturers have to pay extra for shipping. What if there were a dApp that listed spare cargo capacities in all vehicles in the area?
What if they could send a package through a regular vehicle instead of a truck, assuming safety protocols were followed? That would revolutionize the supply chain and increase margins in a notoriously low-margin industry.

Chapter 6:
Decentralizing Physical Ledgers
What is a physical ledger, and how can it be decentralized? A ledger is an electronic file, so attaching the word physical to it seems odd. A physical ledger is becoming a reality these days thanks, to the use of IoT devices. An IoT or smart device can monitor itself and transmit that data to a user on its network.
For instance, cars these days are IoT devices. They transmit usage data to the manufacturer and remain in touch with a central command via GPS. If your car is stolen, this center can track its location within a few minutes. Similarly, manufacturing lines these days have IoT devices working within them.
All of these devices track their usage and wear data which alerts the company about maintenance issues, and so on. IoT devices are physical representations of the internet, and their power is immense. Naturally, they generate a ton of data, and in a centralized system, this is an issue.
Take Alexa for instance. Alexa rests in your home and constantly collects data about everything you order and say to it. According to credible sources, Alexa and other voice-activated home assistants listen to our conversations even when they aren’t woken up (Su, 2021). Would you trust a corporation like Amazon with your personal conversations?
IoT has massive applications for our world. One of the best examples of the utility of IoT devices currently occurs in the Australian outback. Famed for its remoteness, farmers living in the outback rely on natural energy sources to help them combat the harsh weather.
Electricity poles transfer energy from mainframe grids to individual farms. However, if one single pole malfunctions, it results in a power outage that can prove fatal. Water pumps would stop running, and given the distance from civilization, fixing the damage can take a while.
To combat this, Australian authorities use poles embedded with sensors. These sensors transmit their condition-related information to other poles that are 200 meters apart. Each pole transfers data in succession like this until it reaches an internet substation that transfers all data to a central authority.
Thus, if a pole breaks down, other poles close to it are alerted, and eventually, word gets around to the central authority. It’s a great network that works a lot like the blockchain, with poles acting as peers. The possibilities are endless with these IoT-powered networks.
For starters, they can be used to create mesh networks. A mesh network is a self-serving one in that it monitors its condition and resources and decides in real time to allocate said resources to other networks.
Mesh Networks
The network that maintains power in the outback is a mesh network powered by an American company called Filament. Filament maintains data on a decentralized ledger instead of a centralized database. It does this for a couple of reasons. First, Filament empowers locals to act as power monitors by allowing them to connect to the IoT devices via an open network.
Here’s how it works. Let’s say you live near a pole in the outback. You can walk up to the pole, connect to it using Bluetooth, and you’ll receive its relevant data on your smartphone. You can now use this data to transmit it to a central hub or keep it for your records. Thus, individuals become transmission grid monitors, and utility providers experience less overhead.
Before the evolution of IoT poles, utility companies had to expend vast resources deploying employees across the outback to check the condition of every pole. IoT solved that issue by automating data collection, and blockchain further solved it by empowering the locals to track data.
There’s a second, grander scheme in Filament’s mind. It has to do with network usage. Within IoT circles, there’s a huge debate regarding constant versus intermittent transmission. A constant transmission device relays data all the time, while intermittent devices do so every once in a while.
Intermittent devices cost less since they require less network connectivity, don’t generate as much heat, and are less likely to wear out thanks to lower usage. The issue is that intermittent connectivity can be tough to implement. Also, it doesn't guarantee a speedy response. What if a pole checks in at the top of every hour but fails at the first minute of the hour? Supply would be down for 59 minutes at the very least.
To justify the costs of constant connectivity, Filament is brainstorming blockchain-like decentralization solutions. For instance, each pole uses bandwidth at certain times but doesn’t always need it (much like tools sitting around in someone’s garage). What if passers-by could sign a smart contract with each pole and use its network for their purposes?
Imagine a delivery truck driving through the outback. Instead of relying on costly internet infrastructure (which is tough to install in the middle of nowhere) or ineffective mobile data (cell phone signals could disappear in the bush), drivers could connect to each pole they pass and use that network to relay signals to their systems.
You just learned about deficiencies in the supply chain in the previous chapter. A mesh network that allows connectivity even in the middle of nowhere would solve tracking issues easily. It would lower infrastructure costs and enable people to access information no matter where they were. Talk about a truly democratized internet!
Social Energy
Filament’s plan revolves around the smart contract on a blockchain to monitor and verify all protocols. This plan has huge implications for the way power is transmitted in our neighborhoods. If you live in a city or any urban development these days, your home is connected to a central grid.
This grid might exist a few miles from you or a hundred miles away. The longer the distance, the greater the power loss. Now, let’s imagine you live in sunny Arizona and still have to connect to a power source that is 100 miles away. You can’t use the abundant solar power around you directly.
If you install a solar power device, this feeds energy back into the grid (where it leaks), and you receive a subsidy. Unless you unplug your home from the grid, there’s no way of actively using the power you generate. The blockchain coupled with mesh networks makes this a reality. What’s more, you could even turn it into a utility yourself and monetize your power generation ability.
You could live in a neighborhood where each pole is connected to a network. Power will be generated locally and distributed locally as well. Communities that cannot generate power by themselves can connect to a network (or multiple ones) and buy power from them via smart contracts.
Even within the neighborhood, people could buy and sell power. Let’s say you own a farm that has abundant resources for generating hydro energy. You could sell excess power to your neighbors and secure payment via smart contracts. These locally driven systems are far more efficient than our current centralized structures.
No one currently has visibility into how power is distributed, and if the central utility base fails, everyone loses power. What’s more, power transmission lines suffer from massive wastage thanks to long transmission distances. That is one of the reasons why renewable energy adoption is low.
Even if a windmill or a hydroelectric power farm generates ample electricity, transporting it to other states results in power loss. As a result, it’s cheaper for those states to stick to non-renewable sources. Installing urban windmills or some renewable energy source is one solution, but it costs a lot of money. A better solution would be to digitize these power generators and secure their monetary value on the blockchain.
Smart networks like these have the potential to turn virtually anything into energy. What if the heat generated from smartphones and computers could be harnessed and directed into our power grids? The blockchain now makes IoT even more powerful since it’s now possible to secure monetization means.
Lastly, there’s a significant impact on the environment from our current energy distribution systems. Local mesh networks like the ones I described will significantly reduce negative environmental impact.
Wasted Potential
Despite the rise of IoT, there’s no doubt that most IoT devices have failed to live up to their original billing. After all, if smart devices exist everywhere, why don’t we have smart cars communicating with smartphones interacting with smart signals and smart homes? A lot of this technology still lies in the “potential” category.
One of the reasons IoT hasn’t made as big of an impact as we hoped is that security and data concerns exist. As I asked earlier, why would you ever trust Google and Amazon with personal conversation data? A lack of trust has hampered IoT and centralization is the cause for it.
What if IoT could be combined with a decentralized ledger, though? Given the blockchain’s security credentials, IoT would rise to another level entirely. Networks would be automatically secure, and the possibility of data loss or theft would disappear.
Our world would be more connected, and power would be decentralized. The truth is that the Internet of Things requires a Ledger of Things. Without a decentralized network backing it up, IoT doesn’t have much room to grow. Combine the two though, and suddenly, a whole new world of business and social opportunities mushroom.
New Opportunities
So how will business and society evolve when the blockchain is combined with IoT? Here are a few probable ways.
Transportation
The potential for smart, self-driving cars is immense, and it has the potential to revolutionize the way we think about transportation. Currently, transportation is gated in many parts of the world, including the United States, where public transport is virtually non-existent in large parts of the country. Instead of buying a car, you can hail a smart car from your phone and rest safely in the knowledge that this car cannot be hacked or compromised since its data rests on the blockchain.
There are implications for commercial transport as well. IoT-powered devices could transmit shipment-related data to relevant authorities (they’ll always be around in some form after all) and freight managers can have their goods clear customs and inspections quickly.
Even regulating something like street parking will be simplified immensely. A smart sweeper could clean streets and deduct parking fines since every car owner will secure their vehicle registration via a token on a decentralized platform. Currently, it’s possible to do this via Bitcoin, but inertia prevents our cities from becoming truly smart.
Infrastructure Handling
IoT holds the largest promise for infrastructure monitoring. You’ve already read how power lines and poles are monitored using decentralized IoT devices. Imagine vast networks of infrastructure monitored via such shared networks. The financial implications are massive since companies can reduce their overheads.
Insuring these assets is a big industry, and thanks to constant monitoring via secure blockchain networks, insurance premiums will also reduce. The defense industry incorporates IoT to a great extent but can now design better devices for cheaper prices thanks to the reduced monitoring overhead.
Energy Management
If you currently have a busted pipe, you need to call the plumber and wait for them to fix everything. Similarly, if the power fails, you need to wait for someone to fix it. What if these devices (pipes, grids, etc.) could communicate automatically to a network of professionals who can fix and maintain them?
You could download a dApp, call it Plumber Direct, and share pipe usage data with the network. You can negotiate a smart contract with service providers directly, review ratings on the system, and confirm their identity if needed. The entire transaction is initiated by the pipe with a sensor and completed on the blockchain.
While this example involves individual households, the potential for massive infrastructure grids is the same. If power is locally generated, the size of these grids will reduce, which automatically makes maintenance simpler. Monitoring these assets via smart devices will reduce overheads and allow for cheaper power distribution.
Agriculture and Mining
Agriculture is an industry that will benefit immensely from adopting
blockchain and IoT protocols. Farmers routinely face isolated work environments, and the slightest disruption can turn an already tough business into an impossible mountain to climb. Farming equipment these days are complex machines that cannot be fixed manually. Thanks to their electronic components, farmers have no choice but to take them to the nearest dealer and have them repaired. The result is wasted time and lost profits.
Instead, what if the machines were equipped with usage statistics that were stored on a decentralized platform? A decentralized analytics app could provide farmers with usage data of similar machines from around the country (or world), and they could reasonably predict when a part was reaching the end of its lifecycle.
JIT repairs and parts replacement would become a reality, and the farming business could improve by leaps and bounds. Many farmers rear animals, and it’s a tough job. What if every animal’s health stats were stored on the blockchain? The feed a cow ate would have a unique token on the blockchain, and farmers could easily track the health of their livestock easily.
The mining industry relies on a variety of heavy machinery to get the job done. Much like farming, these machines are huge and complex. It takes more than a wrench to fix them. IoT-enabled machines can relay data safely to a decentralized network that will help companies stay on top of their machines and improve their bottom line.
Mining companies need to stay on top of environmental regulations. Sensors placed in the soil, along with water, can help environmental regulation agencies track progress towards goals and alert them of possible contamination quickly. In turn, they can quickly react to such events faster and protect vulnerable areas. Farmers can use such sensors too, and monitor the health of their land.
Healthcare
Healthcare was one of the first industries to embrace the electronic revolution. The first smart devices emerged in this sector. However, as time has moved on, the rate of progress has slowed. Devices are smart but don’t talk to each other. Security concerns and centralization on isolated medical network servers are the causes for this. A patient’s history is localized on a single server in a network. Once the patient leaves that network, the new hospital has to rely solely on the patient’s word when it comes to their medical history.
What if this process was seamless? A decentralized healthcare network where the patient can choose to share their data with the hospital or healthcare provider of their choice before accepting treatment would simplify security concerns. Any treatment they receive can be IoT enabled, which means tracking becomes simple.
For instance, if a patient has to have their hip replaced, the hip can be embedded with sensors that alert the closest medical authorities on the network that the patient will need to visit the hospital soon. This procedure can apply to pretty much any treatment. Hospitals can manage their patient traffic better and rely on other hospitals in their network to bear the load.
For instance, imagine a scenario where a patient needs treatment that isn’t critical but is urgent. The closest hospital sends a smart ambulance (self-driven, of course) that calculates patient traffic at nearby hospitals and routes itself to the best hospital automatically. All the patient does is click “accept treatment” on a dApp and, the rest is automatic. Hospital managers can communicate with each other securely thanks to the blockchain.
The implications for such a system are huge. For one thing, medical insurers will expand their treatment network, since the larger the network is, the more secure it will be. Including more hospitals and more treatment options will result in consolidation in the health insurance market. In essence, there is a danger of health insurance becoming centralized. Regulators will have to step in to prevent this from happening.
However, the quality and convenience of healthcare will skyrocket. In countries with a history of public healthcare access, the blockchain will help solve long-standing problems of lengthy wait times and lack of proper medical access. Decentralization and IoT don’t solve every issue as you can see, but their positives outweigh the negatives.
Fraud Detection and AML
Financial institutions impose heavily centralized systems on the world in the name of fraud detection and anti-money laundering compliance (AML). These functions are important without a doubt. Stopping financial crime should be everyone’s priority. However, the average bank now treats everyone as guilty by default, with people having to prove where their money came from. It ought to be the other way around. The burden of proof should rest with the bank, not with the customer.
The blockchain will help remove these barriers. Banks can trace transactions easily since every transaction is a matter of public record. If a customer doesn’t wish to make their log public, they can share it with the bank upon request. Fraud tracking and AML become simple without being intrusive since a transaction-based ledger records everything indelibly, without flagging user identities.
Large insurers who back valuable items can easily verify the origin and ownership history of the items under their purview. Fraud will be tougher to carry out, and financial transitions will automatically be vetted before approval.
Other Business Applications
Virtually any device can turn into an IoT device and, this opens a world of possibilities for many business models. For instance, retail sales and merchandising will be fully automated, thanks to shelves detecting lower product numbers. They can communicate with robots in their warehouses and restock themselves. If the inventory is running low in the warehouse, the warehouse places an order automatically with a supplier and receives goods.
Document storage, no matter the industry, is simplified thanks to the document itself being IoT-enabled. Its physical location will always be known and accessible. Entire factories could run themselves, leaving human beings with more time to do the things they like. IoT allows us to literally assemble an army of devices and robots that can work for us automatically.
Securing these devices via the blockchain is the best way to move forward. Data security hampers IoT growth, and the blockchain’s decentralized structure changes the picture completely.
Economic Benefits
IoT is often thought of as a good thing. But what are the benefits of mesh networks like the ones you’ve just read about? All of these industries will have a major impact on the financial system since pretty much every business interacts with the financial world at some point. Here are some of the most visible economic benefits, in no particular order.
Speed
Transaction speed is the most obvious benefit. Thanks to complete automation, businesses will run faster, and transactions will be secure at the same time. In our current world, speed compromises security. That won’t be the case in a decentralized world. Banks can verify and authenticate transactions instantly, and businesses won’t have to wait for their compliance checks to clear.
From a consumer’s standpoint, products will make themselves available as needed without prompting. Think of a system that customizes itself to you without compromising your data security and privacy. Currently, companies like Google and Apple claim to offer personalization but only at the cost of your privacy.
Decentralized networks won’t put your data in danger and offer you a much better world of services than anything a company can currently offer.
Reduce Transaction Costs
If there’s one lesson for you to take from this book, it’s that the blockchain eliminated middleman businesses. These businesses are paper pushers and don’t add value to the extent they remove value from the system. Centralization allows them to flourish because there are no other options. However, a decentralized world will have no space for these entities.
The result will be lower costs for products and services with increased security. Businesses can capture more of the value they deliver and experience higher margins. Access to services will also be less gated since there won’t be any entity guarding information to protect its business.
For instance, Airbnb blocks access to owners on its platform until it’s been paid. A decentralized app won’t need to do this since it will be compensated according to the value it provides.
Maximum Capitalization
Capitalization of resources is a topic that consumes economists. There are many things a society can capitalize on. How well does the United States capitalize on its population, for instance? A well-capitalized society allows all of its members to reach their full potential in whichever activity they choose. Such societies tend to impose just a few restrictions and allow members to choose their path, without judgment beyond the obvious safety and legal concerns.
Can the blockchain create such a society? Well, no one knows, but the potential is certainly there. The blockchain will certainly create a system where utilizing a resource to its full potential will be the norm. For instance, any computer that has excess storage capacity can rent space on a network and earn money. People can share their data and monetize their assets.
Capitalization allows businesses and people to monetize and utilize all of their resources. It reduces waste and builds greater efficiency in processes. Currently, the lack of security is holding us back. However, a decentralized network eliminates this concern and people can utilize their resources, whether physical or mental, to their fullest extent.
Fewer Failures
Systemic failures are distressingly common in today’s world. Take the power grid, for instance. The grid is a set of centralized nodes connecting to one mother node that controls everything. If a single is compromised, the other nodes jump in and share the load. If two fail, all bets are off, and a blackout will most definitely occur.
Blackouts in the United States are rare, but in less developed parts of the world, they’re a fact of life. People in these countries learn to live without electricity and schedule their days around blackouts. Overloaded grids and inadequate infrastructure are to blame. Turning these failure points into IoT-enabled machines will alert central authorities of possible failure faster.
However, this also makes them vulnerable to hacks. What if some unfriendly nation hacks another nation’s power supply? This isn’t as far-fetched as it sounds. Russia carried out such an attack on Ukraine during the initial stages of that conflict. Many of Ukraine’s cities suffered from power outages that took days to restore.
The blockchain brings a layer of security that is impenetrable. No longer will smaller nations have to cower in fear of their larger neighbors. Outside of warzone-like situations, governments can rest assured that their systems are fully secure and guaranteed against electronic failure.
Better Prediction
Data analytics is everywhere, and this era of big data has created mountains of variables for systems to analyze. However, much of this data has been seized, not collected. The result is the people who create data are not compensated while rent collection platforms such as Facebook and Google earn billions off of it.
As people become more aware of the injustice of this situation, the analytics these companies run will inevitably become less effective. Their predictive ability will decrease, and the result is less revenue. While not many people will mourn the death of Facebook, many companies use analytics to create their products.
For instance, grocery chains use customer visit data and purchase data to analyze in-demand goods and stock them. If such data weren’t shared, your grocery shopping experience will likely be terrible. Imagine wanting to shop for tomatoes but finding only chicken instead. Not all data collection is for rent-seeking purposes.
Thanks to the security and integrity that a decentralized network forces on a company, people will be willing to share more data. Thus, the amount of data a decentralized network can collect will dwarf whatever is being unethically seized right now. With more data to crunch, algorithms will create more accurate output, and the result will be a system that works for everyone, corporation and consumer alike.
Distributed Capitalism
This benefit is perhaps the biggest that the blockchain offers. Think of a future where the benefits of capitalism will be accessible to everyone. not just those with the resources to exploit the system. This scenario is best illustrated via an example. Let’s take the case of a decentralized Uber. This company needs no introduction. It provides ride-sharing services but does nothing to secure data. Uber drivers have had their sensitive information hacked and leaked. They’re subjected to assault, and worse from troublesome customers, and all they can do is leave a negative review since Uber takes no responsibility. Customers don’t benefit much either since they’re subjected to attacks and worse from drivers. Again, all Uber takes responsibility for is its rating system.
A decentralized Uber empowered by IoT will push the boundaries of what is possible. First off, we’ll have driverless cars soon enough. What if these cars could be turned into autonomous agents that execute tasks by themselves? For instance, a customer orders a cab, and the car pulls up. It calculates the number of riders requesting a cab along the way, asks the customer if they’d be willing to share the cab with someone else, and accordingly executes its task.
Payment will be secured via a smart contract and a token. The conditions of safe carriage are created instantly on the blockchain when the customer accepts a ride. For instance, a clause about not wanting to share the cab with anyone else can be coded into the contract in real-time. If the cab violates this, the agreement isn’t ratified and, no payment exchanges hands. All of this is automatic.
Best of all, data is always secure. You’ll notice that there is no cab driver in this scenario. Well, this is because the cab driver operates a fleet of cars, or a neighborhood or city can come together to own a driverless fleet of cars. Call it social capitalism, if you will. Everyone can buy a stake in this taxi system and earn a cut of the profits from it. Again, smart contracts verify who gets paid what.
Someone who contributes money that is a greater percentage of equity in the business gets paid more, and a smart contract figures out how much they need to get paid. Voting and other corporate governance tasks are executed securely on the blockchain. Meanwhile, the cars maintain themselves and are smart enough to schedule maintenance at registered outlets.
Assuming driverless cars achieve high standards, our roads become safer. There is no driver in the car, and the customer isn’t subject to abusive experiences. The driver or owners don’t interact directly with customers and can receive objective, unbiased reviews.
Now extend this business model to the supply chain and manufacturing. What if a large number of people could come together and establish a corporation and manufacture products that they need? This corporation could be run for profit, and everyone who owns a piece of it can get paid a share of profits securely.
The possibilities are endless. When combined, IoT and the blockchain can turn our centralized world upside down. Obviously, there will be issues that will arise. For instance, some functions will become centralized inadvertently. A fully automated economy will leave many people on the fringes without work, which increases the social strain.
There are no easy solutions to these issues. There’s no denying that our world is moving increasingly towards automated functions, and the blockchain will only accelerate this shift. Governments will need to consider the social costs of this move anyway, so it’s not as if the blockchain is going to create a special situation. It remains to be seen how our world will react to such changes.

Chapter 7:
Economic Inclusion
What is the cost of economic exclusion? Living in the United States or any developed nation, it’s hard to fathom the depths to which some countries plunge their citizens. Consider the case of Nicaragua. Blessed with nature and amazing scenery, this country is one of the least developed nations in its region and the world.
Despite abundant natural resources, its citizens have virtually no access to prosperity. The banking system and prior political malfunctioning are to blame. For a long time, Nicaragua was run by a so-called socialist party called the Sandinistas. They seized control of the country and promptly began executing their socialist aims. The first step was nationalizing everything and banning asset ownership.
That meant land was seized from private control and appropriated to the government. Internal corruption and external meddling from the Americans ensured that the government fell in the 1990s after the Soviet Union collapsed. These days, Nicaraguan politics runs on a system of fair and free elections on the surface, but in reality, is dominated by former Sandinistas.
Adding to the problem is that land formerly seized by the socialist government was then distributed to political party members. That led to a revolt from former landowners, and the legal system was mired in land ownership claims. The banking system is underdeveloped since socialist economies don’t need banks. That has led to just 19% of Nicaraguans owning a bank account and just nine percent having access to loans and traditional financial system benefits (Tapscott & Tapscott, 2016).
The problem is especially relevant when looking at the state of agriculture in the country. Like any underdeveloped nation, Nicaragua relies on an Agrarian economy. Fishing and agriculture are the biggest industries thanks to fertile soil and abundant seas. The problem is that individual fishermen and farmers have no access to prosperity.
A single fisherman cannot borrow enough money to outfit their rig. Instead, they join a cooperative where multiple fishermen come together to create resources to outfit a rig. One person borrows enough money to fund a net, another an engine, and so on. Everyone takes turns going out to sea, and the money from catches is shared. It’s a socialist solution to the broken capitalist system.
Things get worse in agriculture. No one has enough money to till the land they live on. Worse, no one knows who owns the land, which leads to corruption. Thus, money is virtually non-existent as we know it. Farmers don’t get compensated with cash but instead with pigs. A pig is a tangible resource. It provides meat and piglets, which provide even more resources. Thus, with food and water secure, Nicaraguan farmers transact in pigs. The more pigs someone has, the wealthier they are.
However, pigs aren’t a secure source of transactions. They run away, die from a disease, and can be stolen. As you can imagine, this leads to conflicts in rural areas, and seizing someone else's pigs is common.
In short, the whole system is a mess to a scale that is unimaginable to the average first-world resident. Microfinance companies are helping resolve the situation, but even here, it’s a tough sell. People simply don’t trust money. If they’re given cash, they have no bank account to deposit it into. If they do deposit money into a bank, nothing is stopping the bank from misappropriating the funds.
So what’s the solution?
Fostering Economic Inclusion
The problem of exclusion from an economy runs along multiple lines. Race, gender, religion, and other biases play a role in economic exclusion. For instance, what if you apply for a loan from a bank and the bank official simply doesn’t like your religion or thinks that a woman has no business stepping outside her home? A centralized system perpetrates such inequality.
In his book Capital, Thomas Piketty argued that capitalist systems in the modern era were contributing to increased inequality (Piketty, 2019). However, the problem isn’t with capitalism but with its centralized nature. The rich have become vastly richer than the rest of us, and this is an issue. Capitalism has lifted billions of people in India and China out of poverty, but most of those people don’t have access to lifestyles that those in the West enjoy.
In short, while centralized capitalism has lifted living standards, it has benefited some more than most. That is because existing resources have made more money than value-driven resources. For instance, if Jeff Bezos liquidates his shares in Amazon and deposits that in a savings account, the paltry one percent interest he’ll earn on that money will still make him the highest earner in the world. All of this despite him giving up his shares and not creating value.
The majority of the world’s one-percenters created value initially, but once their money began taking over, it created even more money for them. Thus, they ended up getting paid disproportionately compared to the value they were generating. Look at the revenues of any of the modern tech giants, and you’ll see how this works. They don’t generate anything of value and yet earn billions.
So can the blockchain redress this imbalance? It all begins with identity.
Identity and Financial Inclusion
Identity is the most important piece of data we have these days. Governments and centralized institutions like banks are obsessed with knowing who we are. A bank’s KYC norms require it to dive deep into customer history and figure out how much of a risk they are. Regulatory control doesn’t provide banks with incentives to serve their customers.
Instead, a bank’s primary concern lies in securing itself from regulatory penalties. That is why banks require mountains of paperwork for everything and rarely grant loans and financial product access to people who don’t qualify for their stringent terms. This behavior was a major contributor to financial inequality in the United States following the financial crisis of 2008.
Banks were flush with government money, but regulators tightened lending standards. The result was that at a time when most people were encumbered with financial burdens, banks refused to lend money to them. Instead, people who were relatively well off and had money were the only ones who received access. In short, people who needed money the most didn’t receive any.
As a result, businesses and people who already had resources were advantaged, and inequality grew. The central determining factor behind a loan approval decision is identity. Establishing it is tough, and this is why we have ridiculous systems like the FICO credit score. The Fair Isaac Corporation does nothing other than maintain this outdated system.
Some of the ways the system is designed do not make sense. It encourages people to borrow money instead of saving it. For instance, if you shut down a credit card account, your FICO score decreases since your credit limit decreases. In a FICO world, people who leave credit card accounts dormant, ripe for theft, are good borrowers. Fintech companies have begun redressing this imbalance.
Thanks to AI-driven algorithms, companies can scan social media profiles and other non-traditional indicators, such as paycheck frequency, to design an alternative credit scoring system. As a result, the underserved sections of society, such as students and the elderly, now have access.
However, even fintech has run into issues when evaluating someone who isn’t in the banking system. Take the average Nicaraguan farmer or fisherman, for instance. How does someone develop a credit score based on their economic history? Asking these people to open a bank account is especially nonsensical given the lack of banking access. Denying them financial access is a violation of their fundamental human rights. So what’s the solution?
Designing an alternative ID model on the blockchain offers the most promise. Let’s say a Nicaraguan farmer has demonstrated steady history in the number of pigs they’ve owned and traded. A peer review system rates them as trustworthy and as someone who keeps their eyes away from someone else’s pigs. Their reputation score is high, and thus, a microfinance firm can trust them to repay their microloan.
As this person begins to repay their loans, their reputation increases, and access to credit increases with it. Here’s the kicker, though. Their identity can be a universal one, not restricted to the microfinance system’s network. Currently, a farmer in Bangladesh who repays their loans on time has no credit access in the United States. Establishing KYC is impossible for the American bank.
What if the KYC chain could be replaced with a single, unified ID on the blockchain? This ID would be global, which means it would build trust from every point of contact a person has. For instance, the impoverished farmer in Nicaragua who is trusted in their community can now be trusted globally. An American financial institution can bankroll its credit request easily and expand its global reach. The farmer now has the entire world at their fingertips and can grow their business.
Good behavior is incentivized in this system, as opposed to our current centralized review system on a platform. Let’s say someone behaves poorly on Airbnb. All they need to do to erase their history of poor behavior and low reviews is use a different email address and create a new account. If Airbnb blocks them, they can use a friend’s email and gain access to the platform using their ID. The friend would simply say they’re booking a place for someone else, so the owner won’t report the activity.
Worst of all, Airbnb faces no consequences if any party behaves badly. The whole system incentivizes people to hoodwink Airbnb instead of behaving well. On the blockchain, this would be impossible since every translation is public, and an email address change isn’t going to erase a record.
Granted, there is a danger of past mistakes weighing heavily on a person. For instance, someone does a dumb thing at 16, pays a huge price for it, and has this hanging on them even when they’re 40. Their trust scores will be low. The current system gives such people a chance at starting over. However, it’s tough. It’s not as if you can simply apply for a new passport and SSN and erase yourself from government records.
As such, the decentralized system being proposed has the same worst-case scenario but a far greater upside. A secure global unified identification system can be a reality, and it will foster economic inclusion.
Democratizing Entrepreneurship
Let’s say you have a great business idea and the gumption to execute it. You’ll obviously need money to do this. If you aren’t attending an elite university such as an Ivy League school or Stanford University, your chances of raising money are exponentially harder than someone who attends these schools. Given that the majority of students at these schools are legacy applications, it’s not as if the student body is inherently better than the student bodies of a state school.
The education system mirrors the financial one in that those who are well off, tend to transfer these conditions to the next generation, and so on. Life is infinitely easier if your parents attended an Ivy League school and you have decent grades. The school’s brand opens doors for you that the immigrant cannot even dream of.
Thus, we find that entrepreneurship is concentrated in the hands of a privileged few. Research conducted by Ross Levine, an economist at UC Berkeley, showed that the overwhelming majority of American entrepreneurs are white, male, highly educated, and in their 30s when they start their venture (Weissmann, 2013).
Globally, prosperity and entrepreneurship track each other. The more prosperous a country is, the greater is the degree of entrepreneurship it fosters. That explains why American companies dominate the field of business and why Chinese and Indian companies have been increasingly making a mark globally.
There’s no doubt that entrepreneurship improves society. At its heart, entrepreneurship is about solving problems, and it’s ironic that societies that are less well off need entrepreneurship more than developed ones. The problem is that we rarely hear of entrepreneurs in less developed locations because they lack the infrastructure to grow their businesses.
A business in less developed locations is more likely to be unincorporated than one in a developed location. Thanks to corruption, red tape, and plain old theft, the entrepreneurs in the less developed location are better off running their enterprise informally. The problem is that they cannot grow unless they have a legal structure. That means dealing with a government and the banking system.
Imagine someone in Nicaragua who grew up in an impoverished farming family trying to set up their company in that country. They’re far better off pursuing farming instead of their dreams and, the country, and the world, suffer because of it. The blockchain can provide financing and a global ID that simplifies matters for such people.
It can also remove red tape to a great extent since all it takes is one record to establish trust. A company doesn’t need hundreds of documents to prove it exists or that it can do business. It doesn’t even need a bank account, as explained earlier because it can store value on the blockchain itself. The blockchain allows it to sidestep tedious regulations and get to work.
The only entities that lose in this scenario are centralized institutions such as government officials and banks. No one is going to mourn their demise for sure. On a global scale, it also means greater opportunities for investors. An investor in Ohio can invest in a company in Cambodia with full trust because of the blockchain’s ability to provide verification and authenticate transactions.
There won’t be any need to open a brokerage account in Cambodia, worry about transferring money to a Cambodian bank, worry about getting ripped off in transaction fees and exchange rates, and so on. They don’t have to worry about filing taxes the right way back home because the blockchain can automatically apply taxes and deduct them, thanks to a smart contract every American resident has with the IRS. No more problematic tax audits, which is a huge benefit for residents and government alike.
From a business model point of view, entrepreneurs can get creative with their assets. You’ve already read about the metered capacity model where they can rent assets out securely. People can monetize their personal data selectively and earn money on the side. The world will be full of possibilities.
Distributed Ownership
While entrepreneurs can benefit from the blockchain, a less-discussed aspect of this technology is how ownership itself might change. Consider the big companies of today and their one or two-person shows. Amazon and Jeff Bezos are joined at the hip, Elon Musk and Tesla, Bill Gates and Microsoft, Sergey Brin and Larry Page with Google, Steve Jobs with Apple, and so on. Seemingly, every great company needs a maniacally driven founder to push it to greatness.
However, what if ownership and company advancement was in the hands of a distributed peer network instead of a handful of people? Financial concepts like crowdfunding and online lending have disrupted traditional banking models already. Entrepreneurs and individuals don’t need to apply for loans at banks. Instead, they sign up to a platform and have their application reviewed by a large number of peers.
These investors can absorb the risk of lending to unknown people because they aren’t lending too much money. A $1,000 loan is split into 100 units of $10 each. In short, 100 people lend $10 each, and thus, the risk is distributed. Extend this to the blockchain, and you can see how $1 million loans are suddenly accessible without going through the hassle of applying at a bank.
Given the flexibility that smart contracts offer, it’s conceivable that business owners can negotiate better terms with their investors and get as creative as they want. Some investors might accept payoff in the form of the product the company makes. That is how crowdfunding works. Others might want to convert their loans into equity, while another bunch might want to earn interest continuously.
By removing intermediaries like banks, investment banks, and even stock exchanges, the blockchain puts value creators and consumers together to create an efficient system. The Apple of the future might not be created by a single, driven person. It could instead be funded by millions or billions of people around the world to create a product that benefits humanity, instead of walling everything away behind closed doors.
Stock exchanges and investment banks earn huge fees when companies go public via an initial public offering or IPO. In recent years, companies have sought to avoid going the IPO route and have used Special Purpose Acquisition Vehicles and direct listings to avoid losing money to rent-seeking investment banks. A blockchain IPO would eliminate both the investment bank and the stock exchange. It would create a trusted electronic network where investors from around the world can safely buy and sell shares in a company. The company, in turn, will pay minimal fees to join the network and gain more trust because its records will now be audited and verified on the blockchain.
Money Transfer
Money remittances are one of the biggest rackets that centralized institutions perpetrate on the public. Institutions like banks and Western Union collect as much as 20% of the amount remitted through fees and exchange rates. Add to this the man-hours lost physically transmitting and receiving cash, and the damages can grow quickly.
So how can the blockchain eliminate these institutions? One of the roadblocks to implementing blockchain solutions in developing countries is that these countries still rely on cash. However, the rise of mobile wallets has changed the game in these countries. Services such as PayTM in India, MPesa in Kenya, and Abra in the Philippines have transformed the way people look at money.
While PayTM and MPesa require individuals to have a bank account to access their services, Abra is a game-changer in more ways than one. For starters, it’s a crypto wallet and doesn’t need anyone to have a bank account. Here’s how it works. Let’s say someone in London wants to remit money to their relatives in Manila.
Traditionally, this process would require the person in London to withdraw cash, deposit it at Western Union, pay exorbitant fees, and send a code to the person in Manila. After three days, the person in Manila drops everything they’re doing to visit the Western Union branch, pays a fee, and withdraws cash. Twenty percent is lost, along with three days.
With Abra, both parties download the app. The app is secure because it rests on the blockchain. The person in London transfers their money to the app in GBP. At the click of a button on the app, the person in Manila instantly receives their money in Pesos. Abra charges 25 basis points or 0.25% for the transaction. Pretty great so far!
However, here’s where Abra takes it to the next level. The person in Manila probably needs their money in cash due to the way transactions are conducted there. How can they withdraw money from the app if they don’t have a linked bank account? Abra allows them to send a request on their network, asking for other users who would be willing to exchange their electronic coins for physical Pesos. Users close to the requester enter their bids and their commission rates. The requester chooses someone of their liking (backed by a review rating system) and transfers electronic coins to the other person’s wallet. They receive physical cash in exchange. Typically, the requester pays between 1.5 - 2% in commissions.
At the end of the day, the transaction costs all parties around three percent and saves them an untold amount of time. This system isn’t foolproof, though. Each user’s wallet is stored on Abra’s network, which means the keys aren’t in the users’ hands. This highlights the other hurdle of adopting the blockchain in developing countries where technical ability is low.
Ideally, people will own their wallets and connect to Abra’s network voluntarily. However, given the lack of infrastructure and the ease with which disinformation spreads in such areas, there is the danger of people losing their private keys or sharing them with the wrong people. Expecting an elderly person who has never left an underdeveloped country to understand how private and public keys work is a tough ask.
Thus, Abra assumes responsibility for key storage and, this creates a point of failure. If these keys are hacked, millions of users on the network can have their coins stolen. However, as time progresses, it’s easy to see how Abra can transfer key storage responsibility to their users and become fully decentralized.
Even in their current state, dApps like Abra aren’t mere remittance machines. They’re full-fledged stores of value. If a merchant begins to act as a cash exchange agent, what’s to stop people from paying for goods using their apps? The merchant charges a commission just as credit card companies currently do. However, instead of paying the card company two to three percent, the merchant earns that commission.
In such an economy, there’s no need for a bank. Service providers can build dApps on Abra’s network and provide everything from loans to reward programs. There is no limit to possibilities. There are roadblocks, though. The first has been highlighted above. The key to mass adoption is education about blockchain security basics. Without this, Abra will be taking on too much risk.
Second, there has to be a large enough number of cash exchange agents in every location. If the nearest agent is located 20 miles away, a person would rather wait three days and walk to the nearest Western Union to withdraw cash. To this end, Abra is installing its own agents to ensure a critical mass of such tellers.
However, you can see how the smartphone replaces the bank as a medium of value. Everything is stored on a network, and users don’t have to worry about security or their accounts being hacked.
Global Aid
Remittances form the bulk of global money transfers. However, global aid payments lie a close second. Charity payments and aid transfers also happen to be one of the most corrupt areas of financial transfer on the planet. Billions of dollars of aid money are siphoned away by corrupt governments and inefficient aid organizations.
In 2010, Haiti suffered a massive earthquake that brought out the best of humanity on the internet. People banded together to deliver aid via the usual charity organizations. The Red Cross raised enough funds to build 130,000 new homes for displaced people. After the initial wave had subsided and months passed by, it emerged that the Red Cross had built just six homes with the rest of the money missing or diverted to other efforts.
Initial reactions focused on the ineptitude of the Red Cross, but the organization wasn’t at fault. Haiti’s land records registry is fraught with corruption, and there was no way of proving who owned the land these homes were being built on. Thus, the Red Cross built just six and focused on other avenues of delivering aid.
Most of the money intended for the needy was undoubtedly scooped up by corrupt government officials. It’s the same story with every location that requires massive aid efforts. The African continent is a notorious consumer of billions of dollars of aid money without anything to show for it. Government officials divert funds meant for schools and roads to their personal bank accounts and duly disappear. Someone stages a coup using this money diversion as an excuse and proceeds to do the same thing once in power.
U.S aid in places like Afghanistan and Iraq has been diverted to fund groups that the U.S Army is engaged in combat with. The question is: How can the blockchain fix this broken system? The short answer is that it can rectify many mistakes that have been made, but it won’t eliminate every issue.
For starters, situations like the Red Cross in Haiti can be avoided if land registry records are stored on the blockchain. No one can bribe a lawyer to steal someone else’s property or use government forces to seize land. In terms of transferring aid, the blockchain will enforce better accountability of funds thanks to transactions being validated by everyone on the network. Aid organizations will have to establish an audit trail for all of their actions before spending money. Creating an immutable ledger of funds transfer also eliminates government middlemen who siphon large quantities of cash.
In short, transparency in the system will enhance aid efforts and deliver more money to those who really need it. There is the possibility of corruption ruining this system, though. For starters, the blockchain’s immutability could be used to further corruption. A corrupt land registrar could accept bribes and record the wrong names on the blockchain. As a result, the true landowner is now disadvantaged, and the record can never be corrected.
AID money could be transferred to fake recipients and still be siphoned away. Thus, the blockchain enforces transparency on a network, but it’s up to the people using the network to use that transparency to its full effect. Corruption can exist in any system. The blockchain reduces the incentives for it and reduces its scope. However, it can never eliminate it.
Ultimately, a region’s people represent its greatest resource. If the people cannot come together to eliminate corruption, no technology can deliver on its promises. In the developing world, literacy rates, gender rights gaps, and lack of educational infrastructure form the biggest hurdles to blockchain adoption. The blockchain cannot overcome them without significant effort from the people living in these regions.

Chapter 8:
Decentralized Governments
Libertarians often push a picture of a small government as the ideal structure for a nation. However, this vision comes with its own problems. For starters, the lack of laws and regulations will incentivize people to steal assets, and the result will be an authoritarian or oligarchical state where power is concentrated in the hands of a few. The issue hinges on trust.
For a government to minimize itself, it needs a way to account for every resident on its land, which enables it to provide services efficiently. Currently, the only way to do this is to create a unified ID system where the biometric and personal information of every resident is recorded on a card. This card can then be used to access services, open bank accounts, or even be used as a transportation card.
The problem is no one trusts their governments. Recent surveys of Americans reveal that the majority of people believe Congress is dysfunctional and the growing divide between political parties and their supporters highlights this. Everyone demonizes the other side despite agreeing on many issues. For instance, 92% of Americans want background checks on people who buy guns.
However, thanks to powerful lobbyists, the issue is turned into something else entirely, and people who agree on basic facts end up arguing opposing points that are orchestrated by lobby groups. Facebook and its engagement algorithm don’t care about the social harm it causes and perpetuates these lies in the pursuit of greater profits.
In such an atmosphere, can you imagine handing over your personal information to the government? Your first thought would be to wonder whether the government is now going to place surveillance measures on you and track your every move. The Chinese government already does this to its citizens and visitors, and no one wants to live in such a dystopian environment.
Over the past decade (2010-2020), successive Indian governments have initiated the world’s largest unified identification scheme. People were essentially forced to hand over personal and biometric information to the government, including granting access to bank accounts. This unified ID is now necessary to do everything from claiming government subsidies to getting a driver’s license.
In the midst of these drives, corruption has reared its head. People have been caught with fake IDs with addresses belonging to someone else. There have also been reports of opposing political parties being tracked using this identification system. Naturally, all negative reports of the system have been branded as “unpatriotic” and “anti-national,” and voices have been censored by Facebook and Twitter, with pressure from the government.
That is one of the reasons why countries like Canada oppose the creation of a unified ID as government policy. It clearly violates the rights of citizens and forces them into a Faustian bargain: Handover your personal data in exchange for the right to live conveniently.
However, there is one country that has made the unified ID work and has built a trustless system successfully.
Trustless Systems
Contrary to what it sounds like “trustless” doesn’t mean a system that cannot be trusted. It means that a system establishes trust without the need for human intervention while securing personal data completely from everyone except the intended recipient. The blockchain is a trustless network since it’s transaction-based.
Estonia’s e-government program is a shining example of a trustless government system. First established after the country gained independence from the collapsed Soviet Union in 1991, Estonia had the opportunity to completely rethink the role of the government in a citizen’s life.
The internet has perhaps realized its greatest vision in the country. Estonians have electronic identities that allow them to do everything from operating bank accounts to paying for public transportation. Estonians vote online, access medical records online, and even set up companies online in as little as 20 minutes.
Estonia’s e-residency program allows people from anywhere in the world to create an electronic identity, use that to open a company in an EU jurisdiction and operate it entirely online. The key to getting people to sign up to this system is trust, and the blockchain helps Estonia establish that trust.
Cybersecurity on Estonia’s network is derived by Keyless Signature Infrastructure (KSI), which is a revolutionary application on a revolutionary network. KSI doesn’t use administrators or cryptographic keys to validate the information. All transactions are verified mathematically, and there is no need for a person to verify anything at any step. Given the immutability of the blockchain, Estonia’s government cannot fudge records or change popular narratives.
Voting is conducted via direct democracy models, where citizens vote on all issues online. Votes cannot be corrupted or changed. This electronic system of government eliminates the big government problem that almost every nation on the planet suffers from.
It doesn’t matter which government you talk about, every government office seemingly attracts the least capable people to it. Any trip to the government office is an exercise in patience as bureaucracy trumps common sense. Miss one paper or enter some record incorrectly, and there’s someone ready to point out your mistake and exercise their power over you. In short, it’s the worst of all worlds.
The average government official is the biggest rent seeker in the world. In the United States, big government has led to the formation of lobby groups that skew public policy towards their agenda. Gun control laws are one issue that I highlighted previously. The voices of these minorities are so loud that issues get lost in the mire, and nothing happens.
Abroad, the picture is even worse. In the early 2010s, the Arab world blossomed with hope as successive countries experienced revolutions. Fast forward to now, and not a single country managed to improve its lot. What happened to Syria, Yemen, and Iraq is well known. Egypt descended into chaos as foreign interference ensured free and fair elections weren’t possible.
Libya is in tatters and went from being one of the most stable and prosperous North African countries to the hellhole it is today. Tunisia seemingly made the best of it but has slowly descended into chaos.
