All of these events occurred due to governments trampling on their citizens’ rights and the lack of infrastructure to accommodate citizens’ wishes. More importantly, there was no mechanism for citizens to make their voices heard and overcome a vocal, powerful minority.
To ensure a fair government, citizens need certain qualities in place. These qualities build trust in the government and ensure rebellions and other unsavory actions are not needed. Here they are in no particular order, along with a discussion on how the blockchain can bring these conditions about.
Integrity
That is a quality that has seemingly been lost in politics. Ask any person, and integrity is something they expect of their elected officials, but few believe that any elected official has any. A big reason for the lack of integrity in the political process these days is the opaque nature of government. The average person has no insight into why decisions are made and on what facts those decisions are based on.
Combine this lack of transparency with social media-driven algorithms, and you have a firestorm of conspiracy theories. In the absence of any facts, anything that looks like a fact is seized on and turned into a cycle of news. This vicious circle is so strong that even elected officials perpetuate lies to gain more votes. The rising number of representatives that come from the extreme left and right is a sign that social media is driving elections and not facts.
In the middle of all this, the government doesn’t change. It’s big, unwieldy, and inefficient. Billions of dollars are spent on projects no one has heard of. More money is spent on a military that creates more issues than solves them than on education, and the Pentagon writes off billions as an accounting error, and no one holds them to their task.
Moving government records and decision trails to the blockchain is the best way to enforce transparency and restore some semblance of power back to the people. Currently, politicians listen to their electorate once every four years or whenever a primary is around and promptly forget about them. During these times, a crazy vocal minority always seizes the stage and exaggerates issues beyond sensibility.
While all government records cannot be declassified, statements and promises made by politicians ought to be recorded on the blockchain. That prevents any of them from pushing false facts. Fact-checking will become much easier thanks to immutable trails of statements and facts.
For instance, there is a significant section of America that believes that climate change is a hoax despite ample scientific evidence being present. In the face of such proof, people prefer alternative facts. Google and Facebook absolve themselves of responsibility when presenting news results and feed people what they want above all else.
A dApp that establishes trust ratings on news sources and verifies its audit trails will go a long way towards restoring some measure of integrity in the statements that political officials make. Simply put, there won’t be much incentive to lie since every statement can be easily verified. While the blockchain cannot change human nature, it can go a long way towards installing incentives that push people to behave in productive ways.
Power
Big government is associated with power. An elected official can do and say pretty much whatever they want, and if accused, an entire legal apparatus exists to absolve them of crime and put the burden of proof onto someone else. The problem is that democracy is about the distribution of power and creating checks and balances that hold elected officials accountable.
Power is heavily concentrated currently and, this is incompatible with the changes in society that the internet has brought about. People are more willing than ever to take responsibility and look into facts themselves. Information isn’t gated anymore, and this has empowered people to take action.
The problem is there are no avenues for an ordinary person to take action. Every person’s wish or policy proposal is drowned by a vocal minority with power. Aside from associations like the National Rifle Association that pushes gun laws that neither political party’s supporters want, there are other lobby groups that keep the country mired in endless wars and grant aid to countries that oppress minorities in their part of the world.
The average American doesn’t want much beyond having some say in the way political decisions are made. Direct democracy of the kind that the Swiss and Estonians practice is pushed as being impractical since population numbers in America are huge. Well, the blockchain solves that problem.
Estonia’s keyless infrastructure would work no matter the size of a country’s population. In fact, an American network would be larger thanks to greater numbers, and this means the network will be more secure. Having a direct say in political matters means less power concentrated in the hands of a few officials. It also reduces the incentives for elected officials to practice corruption and listen to a few lobby groups.
Value
What is the value of the vote that citizens have? Viewing this from an American perspective, it’s hard to see what value exists. Votes matter during election time, but these times are spread so far apart that elected officials rarely pay the price. During their time in office, they manage to amass enough money and power to subvert any negative consequences of not being reelected.
In fact, most officials who aren’t elected become lobbyists and earn even more money by leveraging their political contacts. Once a citizen’s vote is cast, they’re now held hostage by whatever their elected official does. They have no say in matters if that official turns around and refuses to act on any promises they made during the voting phase. All the average person can do is refuse to vote for them the next time.
To ensure that big money doesn’t drive political processes, greater transparency has to be built into the decision-making process. What if there were a transparent, immutable list of big-money donors that every elected official had to maintain? Currently, there is a record-keeping process, but these records are hidden behind bureaucratic county courthouses.
Any person can access these records, but the volume of paperwork thrown at the requester and the lack of information regarding how one can apply to view these records means few people exercise this right. The blockchain, powered by a dApp on the front end can simplify this. All it takes is a single query, and you can look up the donor records of every elected official. There won’t be any need for a county courthouse clerk to retrieve records and ask for an endless stream of identity-related paperwork from the requester.
The result is the value of a single vote will increase. A real-time database that tracks all donors can be matched to a candidate’s agenda, and people can figure out which statements are lies. Again, the incentive to lie is eliminated from a candidate’s perspective.
Privacy, Inclusion, and Security
The word security has been turned on its head in our times. Governments have convinced us that to be secure, we need to hand over access to our most personal data. No one wants a camera tracking their every move, but in essence, this is what we’ve allowed governments to do to us. We’re followed everywhere by big government, and we have no choice in the matter.
Worse, centralized businesses like Google and Amazon are increasing the degree of surveillance on ordinary people in the pursuit of corporate profits. Needless to say, the higher executives of these companies have nothing to fear since they can buy their way out of this system easily.
The rise of the internet has been marked by the gradual degradation of privacy and inclusivity. People belonging to certain races, originating from certain parts of the world, or those of a particular religion, are targeted for surveillance and cannot access the fullest extent of protection that the law provides.
In essence, these people are presumed guilty and have to prove their innocence instead of it being the other way around. This system exists in its current form because of incentives being the way they are. Politicians and powerful interests have zero incentives to change anything since they benefit the most from this system.
Using the blockchain to record political decisions will force everyone to behave in line with the ideals of democracy. It distributes power and removes control from the hands of a privileged few. Security, privacy, and inclusion are natural side effects of these processes. A person’s history can easily be traced on the blockchain without them divulging any compromising personal information.
That is because their transactions will be recorded on the system, and an authority looking to secure the country, doesn't need anything beyond their public key on the blockchain. The result is the elimination of prejudice from the system and less bias towards certain minorities or groups of disadvantaged people.
Proactive Government
Government services are anything but easy to maneuver or responsive. Applying for something as simple as an identification card involves a ton of paperwork, a trip to a government office, and the possibility of the slightest mistake derailing the entire process. That is in America, of course. Try obtaining government services in less developed countries, and you’ll find that your wallet ends up being much lighter.
These services throw up a ton of artificial barriers that rent-seekers use to make money and collect bogus fees. Electronification hasn’t helped things much since these systems are still centralized, and they’re anything but intuitive or easy to use. Most government subsidy programs are riddled with corruption, and there isn’t an easy way to untangle the mess.
For starters, decentralization can remove the difficulty of investigating audit trails. Second, dApps can turn the government into a proactive organization that fulfills its original aim, which is to say that it serves the people. Most subsidy programs are opaque and require different application criteria. What if a dApp could analyze publicly available data on the blockchain, augment analysis with personal data authorized by the user, and propose the best subsidy automatically?
No more applications or waiting in line with documents. No more depending on officials to scrutinize your documents at their whim and approve or disapprove aid that is meant to come to you. Such a system will be powerful in less educated, rural areas of the world where people don’t have access to electricity, let alone the internet.
In these places, government officials hold absolute power and dictate people’s lives. A dApp would remove the need for these rent-seekers and return power to the people. In essence, the government would be reduced from a large entity to a single app. This vision isn’t a pipe dream.
Estonia’s citizens can access everything from government subsidies to medical records to free books from a public library close to them, seamlessly on the blockchain-based internet service the government has established. All they need to do is log in to the system using their trustless key, and they receive all the information they need.
On centralized platforms, customization is usually a function of data theft. Think of how Facebook operates. It snoops on your activities and presents a customized list of content for you to consume. A blockchain-based network won’t have to collect personal data to customize your feeds.
It would only require access to publicly available transactional data and the rest is up to you. You're in charge of what you share, not some system. Government can use vast resources of information without turning into something out of a George Orwell book. Citizens will be in full control, and governments will work for them. Thanks to a less bloated structure, government services will automatically become efficient.
Lifting disadvantaged communities will also become easier thanks to greater transparency. In short, governments can proactively address the needs of their citizens instead of requiring citizens to tell them what they need and gate access to subsidies and programs.
Smart Voting
Continuing with the theme of empowering citizens and holding governments accountable, what if people could design a smart contract that automatically votes on issues or for officials based on a variety of criteria. For instance, during election time, every politician cries out about how amazing they are and boasts of their superior track record.
However, how many of these people are telling the truth? Currently, we end up voting for the person who sells themselves the best, not the most qualified for the job. What if we required people participating in high-level elections to have certain objective criteria? They would have had to be present at a certain number of voting sessions, would have had to declare their finances publicly, and would have had to establish a clear audit trail proving campaign finances.
Smart contracts can empower voters by screening candidates that fulfill these criteria. Political parties would be forced to react to these changes, and would start pushing candidates that are qualified for the job, not just those who are “electable.” An entire army of rent-seeking political consultants and election campaigners would be removed from the system, and democracy will be much better for it.
Industry Regulation
Industries that are critical to the government’s revenue streams tend to be walled away behind closed doors, with the ordinary person screened away from all information. Blockchain-powered smart contracts could change the way these industries are regulated. In many countries, the abundance of natural resources leads to corruption, and ultimately war and rebellions.
A blockchain that automatically enforces regulations and transparency in a critical industry will ensure that resources are being used efficiently. They won’t erase corruption in the system since the change has to come from the people. However, once the blockchain is enforced, there’s no going back to corrupt systems that impoverish people.
A politician’s track record, when connected to industry, can also be verified quickly. For instance, if a politician tries to pass a bill, voters can quickly verify their affiliations and check the motivations behind the legislation.
Voter Protection
That is one of the biggest positive effects the blockchain will have on government processes. Voter fraud is a huge topic in the United States, but these news cycles have been poisoned by uninformed discussion. A glance at some of the less savory parts of the world will help people understand how voter fraud and intimidation really work.
Zimbabwe is a poster child for many African countries that tend to fall into a cycle of violence after gaining control of their resources. Led by Robert Mugabe from 1980 till 2017, the country witnessed a steady downward spiral despite a promising start. By the year 2000, Zimbabwe was impoverished, and mistrust of the government was at an all-time high.
This situation would continue for the next decade as successive elections came and went. In all of them, Mugabe seemingly won over 90% of the vote, and opposition party supporters and leaders suffered through campaigns of torture, murder, and jail time. At one point, Mugabe stepped down from contesting the election but still won an overwhelming majority of the vote. All of this despite inflation in the country hitting nearly 100,000% and a loaf of bread costing nearly a third of the average monthly wage.
In places like these, voting isn’t a democratic right. It’s a bonafide risk to one’s life. Even if citizens choose to stay away from voting, ballots are cast in their name without their approval. It’s easy to see how the blockchain will make these people’s lives much more secure and result in free and fair elections.
Once again, the blockchain won’t magically solve all the issues that occur in countries like Zimbabwe. However, it will make positive change more sustainable. Using Zimbabwe as an example, Mugabe’s initial years of ruling the country were great for ordinary Zimbabweans. In the early 1980s, the standard of living for the average Zimbabwean actually increased compared to when the country was under white minority rule. Zimbabwe boasted greater wealth than apartheid-run South Africa as well during this time.
However, positive change was unsustainable due to corruption and meddling from external nations. Thanks to the lack of strong systems, Zimbabwe collapsed, and two generations witnessed their potential squandered. Perhaps the presence of an immutable network like the blockchain might have made it tougher for lies and corruption to spread.
Custom Governments
Technology broadens our vision, and blockchain is no different. Thanks to the security and immutability that the blockchain presents, citizens can one day customize democratic participation for themselves. The blockchain enables direct democracy but not every citizen wishes to vote on every single issue that crops up. It’s impossible for someone to be fully informed of the issue anyway.
That is why we have representative democratic institutions where we elect officials to vote on our behalf. The problem is that once voted into power, these officials forget who put them there in the first place and begin amassing power. A dApp can help people choose the degree of their participation, and citizens can delegate votes on issues to their representatives. Thus, every person would be elevated to the status of an elected representative instantly.
What’s more, voters can choose to delegate their vote to other people, making the vote a truly valuable right. For instance, let’s say a climate change resolution is up for debate. If the average person feels they aren’t informed enough to vote sensibly on the topic, and that their elected representative doesn’t represent their individual views, the voter can delegate their vote to someone who is more informed, like a scientist or a conspiracy theorist (freedom and transparency cuts both ways after all).
While the “right” decision is never guaranteed in this system, every person can at least make an informed choice instead of having to accept whatever views some elected representative puts forward. The election of Donald Trump to the U.S Presidency offered an example of how democracy can misrepresent the majority’s views.
While there is a fringe element that agrees with every insane thing Trump uttered when President, many people voted for Trump purely because they didn’t want to see his opponent, Hillary Clinton, win the election. Voters who had cast their ballots for Barack Obama voted for Trump instead. Transparency was a core issue during the election, with Trump making one insane claim after another and no one knowing what to believe.
This insanity was best represented by Clinton’s email saga, with Trump calling for her to be “locked up.” Fast forward a couple of years, and it turned out Trump’s son-in-law, Jared Kushner, had used his personal emails to discuss state secrets, just as Clinton had done. The issue was quietly swept under the rug because there were no powerful interests to push it as part of the agenda.
If there were a real-time, transparent and immutable database that allowed people to fact check everything the candidates said, would the election have gone differently? No one knows. However, one thing is for sure. Trump did not represent the vast majority of moderate Republicans. His stances on many issues angered his voter base, but they had to accept it nonetheless.
For example, if a person voted for Trump hoping for stronger vetting of people before letting them in, they had to settle for a “Muslim ban” whether they liked it or not. The majority of the United States did not vote to discriminate entry into the country on the basis of religion, and yet, were forced to live with the policy.
In such cases, what if these people could delegate their vote to an informed authority, that would cut across political fault lines? It would increase the degree of collaboration in the country and would normalize different political opinions. It would also remind people that the loudest voices are usually the nuttiest and not the majority. It would also remove power from the hands of unqualified, power-hungry leaders who lucked their way into a powerful position.
Another consequence of Trump’s election was that it revealed deep flaws in polling methods. Almost every poll prediction had Clinton winning by a massive margin but she was pipped by Trump when the day came. Polling samplers could conduct random sample elections, where samples are truly anonymous and without bias. Identities can be withheld and data can be quickly gathered to figure out where each candidate lies. Random sample polls like this are more representative than the current statistical methods we have in place today.
Vitalik Buterin, Ethereum’s founder, has an even more radical view of how the notion of governments and elections could change. He termed it Futarchy and described the concept as voting for values and betting on beliefs. While his vision isn’t fully firmed up yet, it would run something like this.
Citizens would first pick a metric to measure their country’s progress. Let’s say it’s a higher education rate. The second step would be to use prediction markets to select government policies that would optimize this metric. Thus, every candidate's policies can be dictated by the electorate and citizens will have the power to push the agenda they want to be discussed, even if it doesn’t win out in the end.
Judiciary
The potential for an overhaul of the judicial system is massive when speaking of the blockchain. For starters, there’s no doubt that there are advantages in terms of the storage of trial records and other important evidence. Witness identities can also be protected to avoid tampering or intimidation. Again, this is a scenario that doesn’t get much play in the United States but is of real concern abroad.
Take India, for example. It’s a notoriously slow judicial system that often fails to resolve cases due to a lack of evidence or the police failing to follow judicial processes. Judges, in most cases, have no option but to throw cases out or stay proceedings until judicial processes are followed. In a country where the average policeman buys their way into their position, one can imagine that corruption is widespread amongst police and the judiciary.
Witness tampering is rife as is intimidation. The police do not have the resources to house witnesses securely, and even high-profile cases see witnesses recanting statements and falsifying their evidence. The more powerful the defendant is, the less likely the case is to receive a fair shake in court. All of this leads to ordinary citizens developing deep mistrust of the system and relying on the mob and vigilante justice to seek fairness.
The result is a society where suspects are considered guilty and punished for their alleged crimes before they’re given a fair trial. Throw in a deeply polarized society that stereotypes everyone based on religion, caste, and gender, and it’s close to impossible for a case to resolve itself according to the due judicial process.
An open and transparent trial system would do wonders for a country like this and restore faith in the system. More importantly, it would weed out corruption in the system. The judicial system is opaque and arbitrary detentions by the police are common. An open and transparent system would prevent the police from acting like licensed thugs and force them to comply with local laws.
Again, the blockchain cannot eradicate every problem overnight. However, it can sustain positive change. Despite its deep flaws, the Indian judicial system manages to get more things right than wrong. However, there’s always room for improvement, and transparency is a good thing when it comes to civic structures.
Some blockchain enthusiasts have proposed alternative judicial models where cases are tried by juries and not by lawyers. In this model, cases would be aired live, and any person, whether randomly selected to a jury or not, can ask the defendant a question. Mass juries vote on the outcome, and a decision is arrived at.
As great as this system sounds, it relies on the juries being educated and sophisticated enough to understand the judicial due process. In places where mob rule is prevalent, every trial could turn into a vote by the media, where narratives and not facts drive decisions. Anyone who doesn’t fit the stereotype of a productive citizen or looks like they might be guilty, such as an introverted person or someone who isn’t expressive with their emotions, might be found guilty.
There’s also the question of a person’s history. Let’s say a person appears in back-to-back trials and is found not guilty in the first one. Technically, the first verdict shouldn’t create a bias for the second one. However, human nature being what it is, there will be a section of the jury that believes there’s too much smoke around the defendant and might vote guilty solely based on a feeling.
Defendant identities can be hidden, which will remove biases. However, it’s unclear how witness identification procedures will work. Either way, the blockchain holds a ton of promise for our judicial systems. There’s no doubt that much needs to be discussed and debated before adopting it.
Either way, it represents a fairer system than the centralized systems currently present in large parts of the world.

Chapter 9:
Implementation Challenges
The blockchain holds enormous potential for our societies but as with any new technology, there are challenges. Throughout history, every technology has been used for both good and bad. For instance, once the enormous potential of splitting an atom was realized, governments rushed to generate electricity from it and build bombs. No technology is safe from corruption, no matter how robust its protocols are.
The blockchain has numerous features that could benefit a tyrannical state and increase inequality. For instance, the records on the network are immutable. No one can erase data or modify it in any way. That is a good thing for the long term. However, what if the data entered on the network is corrupted? Who protects the data entry process?
That is why I’ve repeatedly mentioned that a blockchain is just a tool, and ultimately, it’s the people that decide what to do with it. A corrupt nation isn’t going to become a shining beacon overnight because of the blockchain. Large businesses and their representatives can co-opt the blockchain and create walled networks that are even more robust than the ones of today. That will result in greater inequality and broaden economic disparities.
When evaluating the potential of the blockchain, there is the danger of getting starry-eyed and running away with our imaginations. Many entrepreneurs developing game-changing applications on the blockchain are also talking about revolutionizing society and so on. The previous chapter gave you a few examples of the degree of change that is possible.
It’s easy to dismiss these ideas as hogwash and mention the list of obstacles that these people face. In this chapter, I’m going to run through some of these obstacles. All of them will make it seem as if the blockchain dream is not worth pursuing since the obstacles are so large. However, be careful when dismissing the blockchain’s potential.
Ask yourself whether these obstacles are due to inherent flaws in the blockchain architecture or human flaws. Systemic flaws pose significant obstacles, as does human nature. Not every obstacle is of the blockchain’s own making. So make sure you evaluate all of these points as objectively as possible.
Lack of Readiness
This obstacle alone could stop the blockchain revolution dead in its tracks. To observe how a lack of readiness can slow the blockchain train down, it’s helpful to look at an example of a blockchain application: Bitcoin (BTC). BTC has become the flag-bearer for all blockchain applications since it was the first to burst onto the scene. Many of the flaws that BTC has also exist in other networks.
The first challenge that BTC faces is that there isn’t enough infrastructure to help it thrive currently. Let’s say there’s a financial crisis in your country today, and you’d like to exchange your plummeting fiat currency for BTC. If you understand how the blockchain works and are well-versed with wallets and crypto exchanges, you might be able to pull it off. However, exchanging your fiat money for BTC is far tougher than exchanging it for another fiat currency.
That is the issue that faced the people of Greece, Venezuela, and Zimbabwe when their madcap governments began destroying value. Some people managed to salvage their net worths by exchanging fiat for BTC. However, the majority didn’t. There were no mechanisms that allowed them to do so.
Even if these people did manage to exchange their fiat money to BTC, it brings another issue to the forefront.
Transaction Capacity
The BTC (and any other crypto network) lacks the ability to onboard more than 10-20 million users overnight. Simply put, the network would collapse from strain and create even more issues. A good way of understanding this problem is to look at what happened to the internet when email became popular. AOL was the leading provider of email addresses in America near the turn of the millennium.
Overnight, they onboarded 2.3 million users, and suddenly, these users could electronically communicate with the world. The problem was the internet wasn’t ready to handle the sudden burst of activity. While it eventually adjusted, it didn’t handle initial problems well. Other issues such as email security and password protection also came to the forefront. People lacked email etiquette since no one knew how emails worked. Spam grew exponentially, and phishing attacks began growing. Due to the lack of a gradual release, users had a tough time adjusting to the new world.
The same problems will occur on the blockchain if users aren’t carefully onboarded. Add to this the monetary value of BTC, and you can see the scale of the problem. The lack of etiquette is a bigger problem in cryptocurrency networks since monetary values are huge. Think of the people who originally signed up for cloud wallets and saw their BTC stolen.
What about people who forgot their private wallet keys and lost their BTC forever? Or users who spent their BTC on things like pizza or groceries only to see the value of their coins rise astronomically? The technology is inaccessible to the average person. In fact, the jargon surrounding the technology is impenetrable to the average person.
Crypto wallet addresses are 26 character long alphanumeric strings. Typing this to send money to someone is ridiculously complex. Compare this to how easy Google makes it to search for data on the internet. You type a word or phrase, and you’re away. Transferring money on a centralized system like Paypal requires you to enter an email address. If you’re at a point of sale machine, you tap your card, and that’s it. In an Amazon store, you don’t even bother to pay; you simply walk out.
All of these payments are instantaneous, and dispute resolution is simple. In contrast, the BTC network takes 10 minutes to clear a single transaction. While this is much faster than what the average remittance takes, it’s not an acceptable time frame for simple payment. Also, the transaction time doesn’t work for IoT devices that constantly need to stream their data on a network. It renders real-time view into data useless.
Imagine monitoring the stock market or the condition of your shipment on a supply chain with a 10-minute delay. Would you pay money to watch “live” streaming of a game that is delayed by 10 minutes? Likely not. The same problem applies to the blockchain currently. Use cases that require latency don’t work very well on it. Security use cases tend to do well, but these aren’t as vast as the former.
When speaking of dispute handling, the BTC network offers no advantages. Entered the incorrect wallet address and sent money to the wrong person? Too bad there’s nothing you can do other than hope the other person sends it back to you. Got charged twice by mistakenly approving a transaction request twice? Too bad, there is nothing you can do other than hope the merchant transfers money back to you.
Hoarding
The problem of hoarding is confined to cryptocurrencies. All the popular coins have a finite supply. BTC famously has a finite supply that will run out in 2140. That means the more that users join the network, the more valuable the coin is. Satoshi Nakamoto outlined that BTC was meant to be digital gold. Well, how is gold treated these days? People hoard gold; they don’t spend it. They hoard it in the hopes that its value will increase over time. Almost everyone on the BTC network is doing the same thing these days. No one is going to buy a pizza with their BTC.
To mitigate this issue, BTC developers have introduced microtransactions. However, thanks to the rising price of BTC over time, even these microtransaction amounts might be too much to pay for everyday goods. If 0.0001 of a BTC is worth $100, there isn’t much you can do with it. You can’t buy small purchases or one-off goods to any reasonable extent.
For this reason, developers are now looking at introducing pico transactions. Over time, even this might be too much since everything is tied to the value of a single BTC and that is designed to keep rising the more people use it. As such, it’s hard to see how BTC could ever become an everyday currency.
On a broader scale, these deficiencies affect the blockchain too, and thus far, no design solutions have been created. Society is simply not ready for the blockchain way of things yet. The biggest flaw in the blockchain’s architecture to date is that it doesn’t take human flaws into account. Human beings make mistakes and aren’t machines. In its current state, only machines are fit to operate the blockchain.
Human beings would simply make too many mistakes, and thanks to the lack of legal and infrastructural recourse, the number of disputes would be too high for the blockchain to be practical.
Energy
There’s no way to skirt around this issue. Currently, blockchain-based cryptocurrency networks like BTC burn more energy than the island of Cyprus in a day. Given the rate of climate change our world is experiencing, this isn’t an issue to sneeze at. As of this writing, there aren’t any viable environmental solutions on offer, and it isn’t due to a lack of trying.
The biggest culprit in this scenario is the proof of work consensus model. If you recall, the proof of work algorithm rewards miners that have the most computing power. BTC’s spectacular rise has led to a processing arms race with more resources devoted to developing faster processing chips. These days, ASIC mining rigs dominate the market, even though mining BTC is a losing proposition.
Miners have to take electricity and energy costs into account when mining BTC. The problem is that the reward rate on the BTC network halves after a number of transactions. In the early days, a small amount of processing power saw early adopters awarded thousands of BTC in a few hours. These days, it takes 100 days with 10 ASICs to mine a single BTC.
During those 100 days, you’ll have to pay for electricity and buy your rigs. The average ASIC costs around $2,000. That means you’ll have to spend $20,000 buying your rigs. Depending on electricity costs in your areas, you’ll end up paying close to $2,000 or more on electricity during this period. That means your initial outlay costs are $22,000. As long as the price of BTC stays above this and electricity rates hold their level, you’ll earn a profit.
This calculation doesn’t take other costs into account. ASIC rigs are noisy and generate a lot of heat. You can’t plug them into a standard electrical grid and put them to work. They’ll likely draw too much power away from the central hub and crash the grid (or destabilize it). You’ll need to relocate the rigs to some remote area where no one is likely to be disturbed.
While profitability is tough to achieve, there’s also the fact that all that mining power could be put to better use than mining something speculative. Currently, all cryptocurrencies are speculative in that they’re valuable because everyone thinks there will be a buyer who will pay more for them in the future. They don’t have intrinsic value, unlike a business that produces goods and services and has employees.
They’re similar to gold in that they’re valuable because everyone thinks so. It’s just that cryptocurrencies don’t have the historical backing of over 2,500 years of human civilization that gold does. What if that processing power was used to solve cancer or figure out better ways to redistribute wealth? Wouldn’t society benefit?
I must mention that the energy issue is currently confined to cryptocurrencies. However, the blockchain is affected by it. One solution could be to increase the token reward rates so that energy isn’t consumed too much. However, the problem still remains. If the blockchain is adopted, its rewards will need to be reconciled against its environmental cost.
Supporters of the blockchain argue that a DeFi system won’t cause more environmental harm than a centralized system currently does. How much electricity does a rent-seeking bank consume running its branches, servers, and offices? We’re willing to pay the cost of those institutions, so why not pay for DeFi?
It’s a sound argument. However, it falls apart when the DeFi application in question is speculative like cryptocurrencies are. Most coins will never be used as daily transaction tokens so all the energy we’re spending mining them is useless. It benefits a few speculators who can sell them at a higher price. However, society doesn’t really benefit and all it does is increase inequality.
It also highlights one of the primary issues with decentralized networks. There is no authority in charge who can tell people to stop mining and conserve energy. Everything runs by consensus and, the views of the majority of people tend to skew towards the less than desirable. It’s why direct democracy isn’t a good idea. To put it uncharitably, most people are uninformed fools.
Some degree of centralization is always a good idea, as I’ve mentioned earlier in this book. A centralized authority could restrict mining activity, weather the howls of uninformed speculators, and do some good for society as a whole. The problem is we haven’t figured out a way to reconcile decentralized networks with centralized ones.
There is some hope though. All that computing power is accelerating the intelligence of the average processing chip. Human beings have a limited capacity to solve problems quickly. By diverting processing power to solve the question of reducing energy consumption, we might end up arriving at a solution much faster than we might think. However, as of now, there is no solution in sight and we continue to waste energy.
Governments
Even a casual observer of the state of cryptocurrencies these days will understand how governments can block change and enforce the status quo. Cryptocurrencies like BTC were largely unregulated in their early days, and while there was illegal activity, it wasn’t as if the scale of this activity was greater than what it is with fiat currencies. However, in the name of oversight and tax collection, governments have imposed centralized gates that people pass through before accessing a decentralized network.
Not all of this centralization is a bad thing. For instance, a decentralized network that doesn’t require identification verification or processing and allows its users to transact large volumes of money isn’t a good thing for society. It’s easy to see who is incentivized in such a scenario. Imposing ID requirements to gate access is a good solution.
However, there’s no doubt that governments will overstep their boundaries, ironically in places where the decentralized blockchain is extensively needed. In fact, in such countries, the blockchain will become a tool of oppression. I’ve already highlighted how the blockchain doesn’t provide legal recourse in case of a mistake. What if the centralized authority governing access to the decentralized network is corrupt?
For example, let’s say a politician wants to usurp land from its rightful owner. A blockchain registry is immutable. The politician can enter their name, thanks to corruption, into the register, and just like that, the true owner is disadvantaged. Many decentralized networks can be co-opted into centralized walls that benefit nobody.
Earlier in the book, you read about business models that people like Blythe Masters envision on the blockchain. These ideas aren’t revolutionary in any way. They simply use new technology to enforce old paradigms, and people like Masters have immersed themselves for too long in their environments to realize how small their vision is.
The issue here is finding a balance between government protection and decentralized freedom. In a developed country, one might expect a reasoned debate to help find a balance between these viewpoints. However, in a less developed country ruled by a totalitarian government, the thought of people having a say in the way the blockchain ought to be implemented is laughable.
The scary part is that many decentralized apps can be turned into weapons when left unchecked. The basis of a dApp is that it exists on an open-source, public platform that everyone can access. That can lead to nightmarish scenarios that will only reinforce the need for centralization.
Let’s look at one particularly horrific scenario. Assume there’s an app that allows you to order goods and have them delivered to your home automatically. All you have to do is click what you want to buy and the dApp automatically deducts coins from the network you share. It hooks up to a decentralized drone delivery app that collects your physical address and delivers your package to your doorstep. If ID verification is needed, it can scan your identification securely and store it on a network where no one can access it.
Given the open-source nature of the network, anyone can use the source code and create their own apps. Using this opportunity, some enterprising Taliban or ISIS fighter decides to use the drone delivery dApp to deliver bombs instead of harmless shopping packages. Having read this possibility, the question is: should the blockchain remain open? Is the cost worth it?
Satoshi Nakamoto went to great pains to specify in his seminal whitepaper that the blockchain or cryptocurrencies are not catalysts for change. They’re merely tools, and tools are only as good as the people who use them. It takes people to bring about change. Blockchain is a great tool, but it can be made to create a dystopian state like something out of the Terminator movies.
Imagine a society where everyone’s IDs are logged onto a network, and a series of surveillance drones and cameras track everyone’s moves. It’s simple to verify who someone is, thanks to the immutable nature of the blockchain. Access to services can be gated, and people can be graded on a scale from one to 10, with 10 being the most desirable. Tens receive price discounts and access to privileged areas while ones are sent to reeducation camps and are “taught” to behave well.
Anyone who contradicts the status quo is immediately downgraded to a one and eliminated from society. All of this, brought to you by the blockchain. This vision isn’t entirely false. The Chinese government currently imposes such controls on its people and, in return, delivers prosperity. What happens when the prosperity train stops? Will Chinese people have the ability to question their government? Likely not.
Time will tell how the blockchain will impact our world. One thing is certain. A complete redesign of government and big finance is impossible. Legacy interests are far too powerful to allow themselves to get disrupted like this. However, we will see processes change. Perhaps, instead of hoping for an overhaul, a more realistic vision would be to hope for incremental change that delivers benefits and has some drawbacks.
After all, that’s how progress has been achieved throughout the ages.
51 Percent Attacks
I’ve previously mentioned the vulnerability that exists on a blockchain network. If 51% of the nodes in a system band together, they can take over the entire network and dictate terms. I also mentioned that detecting such an attack is easy for the other nodes, and they can eliminate this possibility by rejecting the transactions made by the corrupted nodes.
However, this scenario doesn’t take a centralized gate into account. A fully decentralized network is not going to be adopted by the public simply because it will never be fully accessible. A centralized gate allows everyone to access it freely and conveniently. What if this centralized gate exists in some totalitarian country?
Let’s say the country of Belarus has decided to publish all media on the blockchain. If you’re unaware, Belarus is one of the most dystopian countries on the planet. It’s been ruled by one man since the fall of the Soviet Union, and human rights violations are routine in that country. Nothing is stopping Belarus' dictator from rewriting facts and creating an alternative history.
Much like Stalin erasing Trotsky from Soviet media files, facts can be removed, and there would be no way of contradicting the narrative. In such a state, mounting a 51% attack would be simple. After all, anyone who contradicted the narrative would not be allowed onto the network.
The Bitcoin network suffered through an era of uncertainty where no one knew whether China was preparing to mount a 51% attack. The majority of mining nodes exist in China, and given the state of that country, no one knew whether these miners were government agents or private interests.
There was a lot of speculation that China could take over the BTC network as a result. However, events since then have muddled the picture. China criminalized BTC ownership since pushing its own digital Yuan. No one knows who owns those nodes, but it’s clear the government isn’t interested in them. Or are they? Is criminalization a move to capture all the BTC in the country? No one knows.
Corporations can mount such attacks as well, funded by deep resource pools. For instance, Airbnb could destabilize and take over dAirbnb thanks to deep pockets. If dAirbnb uses a proof of work consensus system, what’s stopping Airbnb from raising billions, installing a bunch of ASIC pools, and mining tokens on dAirbnb to stop it from becoming a competitor.
Under current corporate laws, such actions would not be illegal. In fact, Airbnb investors will welcome such action since it protects their interests. Another scenario is an established entity simply co-opting the technology and walling it behind closed doors. That drains the dApp of its value and pushes users towards the old app.
That is pretty much what happened with the internet. There aren’t many free areas on the internet anymore. Literally, everything costs data or money to access. Google and Apple host apps on proprietary stores, and everyone have to play ball regardless of their views. If you don’t like it, you’ll be isolated, out in the cold.
The prospect of a 51% attack on a monetary network like BTC is especially scary since it will cause the world to be even more unequal. Currently, there are no solutions to stop such attacks. The best anyone can do is hope the remaining miners detect these attacks and act swiftly. However, if gated access ensures only miners of a certain kind enter the network, there’s nothing stopping an entity from turning the blockchain into a nightmare for their rivals.
Lack of Incentives for Mass Collaboration
So far, we’ve looked at factors outside the blockchain that could hamper growth. Now, it’s time to look at a possible flaw that could originate from within. That isn’t an architectural flaw in any way. Instead, it has everything to do with miners and the incentives they receive to behave a certain way on the blockchain. Let’s use BTC as an example since there’s enough history and transactions here to present a coherent case.
Miners currently have an incentive to maintain the BTC network because they have coins at stake. If the network fails, their coins will become worthless. Many people think that miners validate transactions; however, this isn’t true. Nodes can verify transactions. Miners are in charge of the distribution of power in the network. They decide which transactions go into blocks. In essence, they vote on what qualifies as truth on a network.
Any protocol change that occurs on the BTC network has to maintain economic incentives for miners. More importantly, these incentives should propagate miner decentralization instead of centralization. After all, the miners shouldn’t be the only ones receiving value via BTC. The network has to receive equal benefits for paying miners those BTCs. Ideally, smaller miners located in dispersed nodes should be able to compete with miners in areas that are heavily centralized in terms of node location.
A miner in Antarctica should be able to compete equally with a miner in China. However, is it possible to make this a reality? Currently, the BTC network favors older miners who reap greater rewards than newer ones due to the decreasing rewards on offer.
Eventually, the rewards are going to be so small that getting into mining is not going to be worth it and, this leaves the distribution of miners around the world as it currently is. A blockchain network could solve this problem by switching up incentives to mine. By choosing not to implement halving rewards, incentives will always remain present. However, there might be other disadvantages depending on the app in question.
For instance, a currency app might not work well if supply is unlimited. Inflation will eventually eat all gains away and, this means most miners won’t look at it as a store of value. Thus, they won’t have any incentive to sign up to the network in the first place, which defeats the purpose entirely.
Also, a blockchain network that underlies a dApp, like dAirbnb, won’t have any incentives for people to become miners on it. While users will have incentives to mine the network, it limits the size of the network to the number of users on it. If a user decides to leave, that’s one less miner in the pool. What if one landlord lists multiple properties and listings and manages to mine a greater portion of the network than others?
As you can see, there are some issues with the way mining currently works. It comes down to incentives. While the blockchain removes incentives for corruption, it doesn’t provide incentives for miners adequately over the long term. No doubt, someone will develop a solution to this problem. BTC’s solution to the problem is charging fees for transactions.
Currently, miners can earn rewards for mining BTC but also for facilitating transactions. Collecting fees will eventually become the only revenue stream on the BTC network, and it gives miners incentives to remain on the network or join it. The fee model isn’t perfect, though. Given the loss of rewards from mining BTC, miners will eventually want more fees to cover their costs.
That might lead to a bidding auction for every transaction, where a user indicates the fee they’re willing to pay a miner to clear the transaction. Miners will compete for the highest commissions and leave the lower-priced ones for others to deal with. Clearly, this is a major problem. Imagine having to wait at the coffee shop for 10 hours to pay for your coffee because no miner wants to clear your transactions thanks to low fees.
Again, it’s important to remember that blockchain is a nascent technology. It’s been around for just 10 years and it’s impossible for it to solve every problem it has in that time. However, it’s important to note that mining incentives are a significant problem that every blockchain network has to deal with.
Employment Impact
When the internet first burst onto the scene, everyone viewed it as a huge opportunity, and rightly so. It made accessing information so much easier. However, as time went on, it was plain to see that some businesses weren’t long for the world. Newspapers, magazines, libraries, physical media buyers and providers, etc., began counting the days till their demise.
The problem was especially acute with the news media. Newspapers couldn’t compete with Google’s value proposition where news was now freely available, and ad money could not sustain quality journalism. As a result, in the United States, journalism has fallen to a handful of stable newspaper outlets while the local dailies have folded. Sports journalism has changed immeasurably, with old reliables as Sports Illustrated replaced with new-age blogs like Deadspin and Barstool Sports.
Was this bad for the world? Well, it’s hard to say. The internet has allowed automation technology to come to the forefront, and the simplest of tasks these days are automated. For instance, anyone who works in a company’s finance department will attest that many routine tasks are now automated. There was a time when an employee in the finance department would spend their time chasing down the paper to verify an invoice.
All of that is automated now. In the financial sector, market-making traders have been replaced by machines thanks to the internet. Back in the mid-2000s, these traders earned base salaries in the low six figures and bonuses that pushed their compensation into seven figures. These days, a market-making trader at an investment bank barely breaks $100,000 in compensation after bonuses.
While the internet brought about the possibility of automation, the blockchain will revolutionize it by making it the norm. The blockchain can facilitate even more sophisticated automation thanks to its architecture. Imagine a dApp like dAirbnb. The app won’t need employees to maintain listings and monitor user behavior. The network does everything automatically.
If smart cars become a reality and start living on the blockchain, taxi drivers will cease to exist. Think of grocery stores that replace cashiers with scan-and-go payment systems and so on. The government is always one of the biggest employers in any country. If it moves to the blockchain, there won’t be any need for government employees.
The social consequences of this change are massive. In less developed parts of the world, a government job is a hallmark of security and a better life. In places like rural India and the subcontinent, a government job effectively provides a person with a home, salary, and a family. Without it, there’s no security, and no life for all purposes.
One possibility is that everyone in society will turn into a small investor. Entrepreneurs will be able to raise money from diverse sources, and the security that a blockchain provides will incentivize investment. However, investing money doesn’t guarantee paying the bills. How will society react to massive job loss and automation?
We’re already seeing changes in societies around the world. People are demanding more of their employers while employers are busy implementing robots to replace humans. Amazon already uses robots to shuttle products from shelves to packing areas. The company famously treats its line workers like robots, with strict times allocated for breaks and work hours. It won’t be long before these people are also replaced by robots.
Workers might form unions to demand more rights and a share of profits. After all, a robot doesn’t have to be paid. Meanwhile, education systems will pivot even more towards technological education at the expense of humanities and liberal arts. How will this affect society? No one knows.
The bottom line is blockchain can make lives much better but, if not implemented effectively, it can ruin millions of lives. Only time will tell how society will respond to this revolution and how the network will adapt.
Lack of Consensus
Ironically, a network that makes its living off consensus mechanisms is fraught with problems surrounding consensus building. I’m not talking about verifying transactions. Rather, it’s the bigger picture that’s the problem. Every blockchain network has been plagued by a lack of direction over its future because of disagreements within the community.
The internet has centralized bodies such as the Internet Engineering Task Force and the World Wide Web Consortium to define protocols and direct initiatives. For instance, when Facebook effectively tried to colonize the internet in the developing world, guidelines from these bodies surrounding net neutrality helped put these moves into perspective. Countries that didn’t understand the issue at heart had an authoritative resource to rely on and inform policy.
Such a body doesn’t exist on a blockchain network and, it never will if blockchain evangelists have their way. BTC is again a good example. Currently, the network isn’t set up for mass adoption. It’s purely a speculative network where people dip in and out, trying their luck at making some money. There’s no value-added, and it consumes untold amounts of energy.
A few core developers argued that mass adoption is one of the network’s core aims, and the current block size prevents this from happening. Currently, a single block on the BTC network is one MB. These developers argued that the block size must increase to 20MB if the network hoped to cope with increased demand. However, it wasn’t as if these developers could go ahead and just increase the block size; the rest of the network (especially the miners) had to agree to changes.
That is where howls of protest began pouring in. Some developers maintained that BTC is a store of value and that mass adoption wasn’t necessary to legitimize it. Fifty-one percent (51%) attacks were not an issue due to the vast size of the network and, potentially higher transaction fees wouldn’t materialize because BTC wasn’t meant for everyday transactions anyway. In the previous section, you read about having to wait for 10 hours to pay for your coffee.
These dissenting voices argued that BTC wasn’t meant to pay for coffee and small purchases. In short, there were a lot of opinions and no consensus. At times, the arguments devolved into mud-slinging of the kind you’d expect to see at a playground for five-year-olds. If this was a tiny network that no one cared about except a small number of cryptographers, it wouldn’t be an issue.
However, a decentralized network worth billions of dollars being run by a group of arguing five-year-olds is a different issue altogether. While the network establishes trust automatically, how can someone trust those who run it? How do policies get defined, and what’s to stop some billionaire who isn’t as smart as he thinks he is taking over the network by buying the developers out?
In BTC’s case, there was another issue that cropped up. Miners from China opposed increasing block sizes because larger blocks require more computing power, which they didn’t have access to. Thanks to China throttling internet consumption, these miners would be disadvantaged. Ultimately, the developers who wanted larger blocks created a fork and launched Bitcoin XT.
Despite this fork, the issue of governance remains. Who governs the network, and who decides what goes and doesn’t? There are no answers to this. Imagine if an institution like the World Bank was run this way. It inspires zero confidence, and ultimately, no one will use the network.
That is why some degree of centralization works. However, blockchain enthusiasts remain committed to their vision of complete decentralization, which isn’t the best model in the long run.
Regulation
The blockchain will create a vast network of autonomous agents, each with the ability to reason and decide its course of action for itself. How does someone govern such an entity, and what sort of a legal system can account for them? Assume a community pools funds together to fund a smart taxi program in their neighborhood.
This smart taxi malfunctions and kills a pedestrian. Who is legally responsible for this accident? No doubt the entity that developed the car will be investigated. However, the car’s owner is also legally liable since it’s a product that malfunctioned. Who is the true owner? It’s the entire community. Does this mean every person in that community goes to jail or pays a fine?
How will fines be determined? If someone contributed more money than another person, is the former legally more liable than the latter? The question of regulation isn’t a blockchain problem per se, but it’s a significant roadblock. The internet posed so many regulatory issues that we’re still dealing with the fallout from it. Data privacy laws have only just begun to catch up.
The blockchain brings even greater complexities that our legal systems aren’t designed to handle. Pretty much every legal system in the world borrows principles from ancient Greek and Roman processes. Places that don’t follow these principles rely on religious books, which are even more stifling. These principles are over 3,000 years old. People then couldn’t even envision peace, let alone an autonomous robot that could imitate human behavior.
Then there’s the possibility of something like Skynet forming. Skynet is the villainous AI from the Terminator movies but mishandling the blockchain will make it easy for a self-aware AI to take over everything. It’s why the world needs centralized regulation.
All of these issues are problematic but remember that they exist primarily because the blockchain is nascent. When the internet first burst onto the scene, there were governance issues too and many more issues no one had even thought of. Humanity solved those issues and continues to muddle along, dealing with issues as they arise.
There’s no doubt that the same will happen with the blockchain. However, recognizing these issues is necessary since the blockchain isn’t some cure-all for the world’s evils as its evangelists will tell you. The possibility of things going wrong is very real.

Conclusion
The blockchain represents a chance for humanity to leap forward and redesign traditional paradigms. Everything from our daily interactions to our massive institutions stands to be disrupted. While there will no doubt be some negative consequences of this, the potential for positive change is massive.
Decentralized applications in finance can change the way we look at currencies, value storage, and daily transactions. It’s true the blockchain in its current state isn’t ready for mass adoption. However, it’s a solution that is sorely needed. Look around the world these days, and all you’ll see are stories of discontent.
Most of these stories are indeed driven by social media’s engagement algorithms but examine the politics of most nations, and you’ll see that far-right movements (and far-left movements) are increasingly gaining a foothold. In places like Europe where memories of Fascism are still alive, this is a troubling development. Environments that don’t guarantee equality and fair distribution of wealth foster harmful movements like these.
The blockchain offers us a chance to address these inequalities and shift the balance of power back towards the center. Currently, Western economies have adopted a K-shaped recovery model where the rich get richer and the poor get poorer. It’s increasingly hard to break from the cycle of poverty because of the strict regulations that centralized institutions place on such people.
Everything from our credit scores to the right to vote is controlled by big government, and this wasn’t the world we signed up for. The blockchain will enforce transparency on these old institutions and bring about a society where merit is truly rewarded. Due to the peer-driven architecture of the blockchain, it can help everyone from regulators to entrepreneurs and can design a society that is more inclusive and serves everyone.
Most importantly, it will certainly bring the world closer together. A farmer born in Nicaragua won’t be a slave to that country’s financial system. They can access credit and financial services from every corner of the globe. Sending money to relatives back home won’t take multiple days and cost 20% of the transaction. Instead, it’ll be instantaneous and cost a fraction of that amount.
Blockchain enthusiasts aren’t the quickest to acknowledge flaws in the decentralized model, as I have in this book. However, most of these flaws are a function of the technology’s nascent stage. Still, it’s important to note them and understand that change comes from the people, not from technology.
How will the world use the blockchain to create a better society? How will old centralized institutions react to the new threat to their power? Only time will tell.

Chapter 1:
The Rise of Digital Money
It is no secret that the way we handle money has vastly changed since it’s conception. We started off with trading physical things, moved onto coins created with precious metals, eventually decided that paper money was the way to go, and now most people don’t hold physical money in their hands anymore. Sure, there is the option to pay with cash and use the physical money as a way of trading, but for many people around the world, we just use cards and banking apps to handle, exchange, and transfer our money.
We can all expect that, somewhere in the near future, the presence of physical money might be a thing of the past. It is much easier and safer to digitally handle your money. There is a far lesser chance of having your life savings stolen from you if it is in a bank account than if you had it in a bag in your kitchen cupboard or under your mattress. Also, considering the fact that things are just so much more expensive now and we want to be able to save large amounts of money, it would just not be feasible to have all of this in physical cash.
The move towards digital money started when people started moving all over the world, and the internet began connecting us to those people that were far away from us. The fact that everyone was much more mobile and connected at the same time meant that the use of physical money had its limitations. If we were still only using physical money in this day in age, life would be significantly more difficult, or at least a whole lot less convenient. A parent couldn’t send money to their child that has gone away to boarding school or university. We would have to plan our vacations down to the last cent and take the exact cash that we would need with us. Can you imagine carrying thousands of dollars over to other countries? You can easily see how this could be dangerous and limiting in various aspects.
Most of the western world has gotten used to the idea of digital money. Most of us use it for a majority of our transactions and exchanges. EFTs, wire transfers, and even the use of credit and debit cards have made physical forms of money almost obsolete. Many people will tell you that they believe the use of physical money will eventually cease at some point in the future. We do not know when this will be, but there is no doubt that we are moving more and more towards digital solutions rather than the physical ones that we have been using for many years now.
The Difference Between Digital Money and Cryptocurrency
It is easy to confuse the two of these terms and think of them as the same thing. However, it is important to note that cryptocurrency and digital money are not exactly the same thing. Digital money would be any form of money that you are not able to hold in your hand. You can transfer and exchange with it without actually having the money with you and physically trading it. All aspects of digital money can be handled with the use of the internet and online platforms. Digital money includes cryptocurrency under its umbrella, but cryptocurrency is not the only form of digital money; these two terms are not interchangeable. If you hear someone talking about digital money, they could also be talking about the money that they have in the bank and are able to move around using a banking app of some sort.
Right now, there are a lot of people that use digital money; it has become the norm. If you were to hear of someone hiding their money under their mattress, you would probably think that is a little bit backwards just because there are so many other solutions out there that have more benefits and are safer. Cryptocurrency is a whole other kettle of fish. This is a much newer concept and therefore has been met with some skepticism. Over the years, crypto has proven that it is a reliable form of currency and might very well be paving the way for how we do things in the future.
In order to be able to properly explain the difference between digital currency and cryptocurrency, we must first explain them as separate entities. Digital currency is a balance or a record of currency that can be accessed through the internet. This can be stored on a distributed database, a stored-value card, a computer database, or within digital files. This means that there are a lot of options when it comes to digital currency. Some examples are central bank digital currency, e-cash, and cryptocurrency.
A digital currency performs in a similar manner to a physical currency. You can transact and exchange in the same way and purchase real-world goods. Since it does not have a physical form, it allows the transfer of money over large distances to be almost instantaneous in some cases. There are some that say physical money that has been deposited into a bank and is used digitally should not be classified as digital money. However, most people take it as whatever money that is not physical and can be used to purchase goods and services can be classified as digital money.
Cryptocurrency does have the characteristics of digital currency, but with some added features. Crypto has no real-world equivalent. This means that you can’t take it out of the system and spend it like physical cash. Everything stays in the blockchain system and that is how all the transactions take place. Public ledgers and cryptography are used to perform secure transactions. With Bitcoin and a few other popular cryptocurrencies, there is also no central authority that controls all the cryptocurrency. Everything is decentralized to allow for the users to have control over what goes on with the currency. If there is a change that needs to be made to the way things work or the system as a whole, there needs to be a vote conducted and only if the majority rules in favor of that change will it be implemented.
If the cryptocurrency is centralized and controlled by a specific entity, it will not have the same principles as stated above. Instead it would run almost the same as if a bank held the money as the centralized authority would have control over the currency and depending on which one you use, there will be different rules and regulations in place. It is important to do your research on the specific cryptocurrency you are planning on investing in and using. Not all of them are created equal, which means what works for one will not work for the other. There are many different cryptocurrencies out there and even more being developed, so it is even more important now than ever to stay on top with what is happening in the world of crypto if you want to be a part of it.
The Flaws in the Banking System
Many people are of the mindset that we don’t have to fix what isn’t broken. Since the banking system has been around for a long time, people feel secure with how it works and might not want to change. In order for anything to truly make it as a new idea or invention, it has to solve a problem. If it is not solving a current pain then people will not buy into it. In every way imaginable, we are looking for the easiest ways to do things. If we are to take something on the convenience and benefits of it must outweigh the inconvenience and time spent learning about that new thing.
This means that there must be some clear problem that cryptocurrency has to solve in order to be viable as an option for people to switch to. No one is going to switch over from what is comfortable if there are no clear benefits to the other option. As it stands, cryptocurrency is able to solve a whole lot of problems that the banks are not able to or that the banks have actually created. When you are aware of this, it will make it easier to understand why there has been such a big move towards cryptocurrency in the past decade or so.
Banks Are Not Accessible to Everyone
If you live in a first world country, or in a very developed part of a third world country, access to banks and the banking system might not be something you have ever thought about. It does seem like something that everyone would have access to if you don’t know otherwise. However, there are well over a billion people in the world that still remain unbanked. This means that there are no banks anywhere close to them and they do not have access to banking services. The distance from a bank is not the biggest reason that many of these people remain unbanked. The number one reason is that they do not have enough money or that the bank’s fees are too high and it is too expensive to open an account. This is a very different situation to most people reading this book who would be able to open up multiple bank accounts for themselves and for their children.
All of these people have to rely on a cash system, which can be dangerous in an area with a high crime rate. They cannot manage their wealth properly or have the ability to grow it using the tools that are available to those that have access to banks. This is creating an even bigger gap between the poor and the rich.
In order to be able to make your money correctly in a bank or just in the current financial market, you need to have a good level of financial literacy. You would need to be able to understand interest, inflation, risk diversification, and a few other key concepts of finance. Without this understanding, it becomes very difficult to build wealth. Even if the unbanked people of the world were afforded access to the banks, they would not be able to build themselves up like the people in the world who already have a level of financial literacy can.
Trusting the Financial Institutions and Governments
There is a low trust factor when it comes to the financial services industry and the government. Since there hasn’t been another alternative, people had no choice but to put their trust in these industries. This has ended up leaving many people in negative situations, especially when it came to recessions. People who were wealthy all of a sudden were struggling to make ends meet. After the recession, people still had to put their trust back into the financial system because there was no other option. Just because people are making use of banks and taking part in the financial system of today, does not mean that they trust it. The trust levels in the government sit at 40% in the USA, 42% in the UK, 32% in France, 34% in Mexico, 21% in South Africa, and 39% in Japan (Desjardins, 2019). Even though it seems a little bit higher in the USA and the UK, none of these break the 50% barrier. This means that people are forced to put their trust in a government that they don’t really trust in. Wealth is one of the most important things to people so there is a definite flaw in the system if this is how people are feeling.
Currency Manipulation
With centralized currency, the authorities have the power to decide on the value of the currency. They can manipulate it in a way that they can benefit most from it if they wish. While this doesn’t happen to a large extent in some countries, other countries really do suffer from it. While it may be better for the central powers to hold this kind of power, the people who are on the ground are always the ones to suffer from any unfair manipulation.
Having a centralized authority also gives them the power to censor the accounts of the citizens. This means that they can freeze accounts, remove funds from an account, and deny access to the banking systems. If investigations are underway, the person would not be able to access their account for a long period of time. Then they will not be able to make payments or buy basic necessities in really extreme cases. The user does not have full control over what happens with their money.
The Risk Is Greater With a Centralized System
When everything is controlled by one big entity and they make all the directions regarding the finances of the masses, one mistake can be devastating to millions. There is no guarantee that the banks will not make a mistake, and if they do, there will be a ripple effect throughout the country and even the world. All you have to look at the U.S. subprime mortgages that caused the housing bubble that started in 2008. Which if you think about it, wasn’t that long ago.
With the banking system as it is now, all of our eggs are in one basket. So if someone slips, everything will crack and we will all be in trouble. All the general public has to go on is the word of the central authorities, and since most of the time you have to sign a contract that states that they are not liable should anything go wrong, this isn’t a very good basis for a trusting relationship.
The Clear Benefits of Cryptocurrency
There are many benefits for the use of cryptocurrency. This is made evident by the amount of people that have bought into the idea and are planning to use it as the only way to trade in the future. Below are six of the benefits of cryptocurrency which seem to be the biggest selling points for the system. These benefits will be discussed in more detail throughout the book. However, it is important to know what they are now so that you can understand why there is such a push towards crypto in today’s world. Later on you will find how these benefits are able to take place and the structures that are put in place to make sure that cryptocurrency is a safe and viable option for the future.
Transaction Fees Are Lower
When you are transacting with a credit card or even send money through an EFT, the transaction fees can be quite high. It is usually a percentage of the amount you just transacted with, which ranges between 2%–5%. This can be higher depending on where you bank and the type of transaction you are completing. International transactions tend to have higher fees than local transactions.
With Bitcoin and other cryptocurrencies fees are much lower, even if it is an international transaction. It is not impacted by the type of transaction it is or where the receiver of the payment is situated. You could pay the same fees to send money to your next-door neighbor or to your friend in Australia. For someone who has a lot of international business, this can have serious benefits for their clients and for you if you have international suppliers.
No Risk of Chargebacks
A chargeback is when a customer buys a product, takes it home, and then asks the credit card company to issue them a refund.
